## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

- Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with crossreference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
- Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.
- The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60 . Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
- The recommended font is Arial, size 11.
- Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

Please submit the completed proposal to:

fundingproposal@gcfund.org

'FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]'

- A.20. Executing Entity information

In the four countries, the Executing Entities for the implementation of Components 1 and 2 will be:  in the Republic of Kenya , acting through its National Treasury (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the State Department for Livestock Development ('SDLD') of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development (the 'MoALD') (for the implementation of  the  Programme's  Activities) ;  in  the United Republic of Tanzania ,  acting  through  its Ministry of Finance (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and its Ministry of Livestock and Fisheries (the 'MLF') (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) ;  in  the Republic of Rwanda , acting through its Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the Rwanda Agriculture Board (the 'RAB') within the Ministry of Agriculture and Animal Resources ('MINAGRI') (for the implementation of the Program me's Activities) ;  and  in  the Republic of Uganda ,  acting  through  its  Ministry  of Finance, Planning and Economic Development (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and its Ministry  of  Agriculture,  Animal  Industry  and  Fisheries  (the  'MAAIF'')  (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) , in each case, with respect to the Activities to  be  implemented  in  the  Host  Country  of  the  relevant  Executing  Entity,  together  the 'National Executing Entities' or 'NEEs'.

The Executing Entities for the implementation of Components 3 and Output 3.1 will be: in the Republic of Kenya , acting through its National Treasury (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds)  and  the  Agricultural  Finance  Cooperation  (for  the  implementation  of  the Programme's Activities); in the United Republic of Tanzania , acting through its Ministry of  Finance  (for  the  channelling  of  the  GCF  Proceeds)  and  the  Tanzania  Agricultural Development Bank (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities); in the Republic of  Rwanda ,  acting  through  its  Ministry  of  Finance  and  Economic  Planning  (for  the channelling  of  the  GCF  Proceeds)  and  the  Development  Bank  of  Rwanda  (for  the impleme ntation  of  the  Programme's  Activities);  and in  the Republic  of  Uganda ,  acting through its Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the Uganda Development Bank (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) ; and in the case of Output 3.2, SDLD, MLF, RAB and MAAIF, in each case, with respect to the Activities to be implemented in the Host Country of the relevant Executing Entity. The Agricultural Finance Corporation, the Tanzania Agricultural Development Bank, the Development Bank of Rwanda and the Uganda Development Bank are together, the 'Wholesale Banks', (together, the 'Executing Entities').

## A.21. Executive summary

1. Climate context. Observed and projected climate change scenarios (Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs)  2.6  and  8.5)  in  Kenya,  Rwanda,  Tanzania,  and  Uganda  indicate  significant  changes  in  precipitation  and temperature patterns, as well as heat stress and other extreme weather events that will negatively impact the dairy sector.  The  frequency  and  severity  of  droughts  are  projected  to  increase,  resulting  in  substantial  losses  of  dairy livestock, affected pastures and rangelands. This combination of factors has profound implications for the dairy industry and dairy farmers in East Africa, leading to lower milk yields and production, reduced cattle health, deterioration in pasture and feed quality, and increased risks in milk losses along the value chains. In addition to the sector's existing vulnerabilities, such as farmers' lack of access to knowledge and new skills, insufficient milk collection facilities, limited access to quality inputs, natural capital and finance, climate change-related factors faced by dairy farmers in East Africa make  adaptation  strategies  essential  to  its  sustainability  and  resilience.  On  the  mitigation  side,  methane  (CH4) emissions  from  the  dairy  sector  dominate  the  greenhouse  gas  (GHG)  emissions  inventories  of  Kenya,  Rwanda,

1  According to the World Bank statistics (https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/search/dataset/0037712), there are 54.2 million people in Kenya, 65.5 million in Tanzania and 47.2 in Uganda and 13.8 in Rwanda.

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

Tanzania,  and  Uganda.  The  expected  growth  of  the  dairy  sector  means  that  absolute  emissions  and  emission intensities will continue to rise if no action is taken. The urgency of reducing the sector's GHG emissions and increasing its resilience to climate change is highlighted in national policies and plans, more particularly in the countries' Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The IPCC recognizes 'efficient livestock systems' as a feasible adaptation option that has synergy with mitigation. This is shown in IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report, Figure SPM.7 (4th row).

2. Programme objectives and intervention approach. The proposed Programme, which will be implemented in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda, aims to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from the dairy sector while  increasing  the  resilience  of  livestock-dependent  communities.  The  Programme  will  support  a  shift  from  low production efficiency to improved, gender-sensitive, integrated and NDC-aligned practices that reduce emissions at farm and food system levels (including interventions that reduce milk losses and increase milk collection, decarbonize processing  with  renewable  energies,  formalize  dairy  value  chains  and  strengthening  partnerships  with  the  private sector), as well as interventions that promote circularity, and increase rangelands ecosystems resilience and services (including carbon sequestration in grasslands). The paradigm shift of the Programme will be accompanied by measures to strengthen policies, regulations, and institutional capacities for a low-carbon climate-resilient dairy sector at national and regional levels. To achieve this, the Programme comprises three components, each uniquely designed to promote and encourage climate change adaptation and mitigation within the dairy sector.
3. Component 1 of the intervention focuses on initiating a paradigm shift towards a more productive, climateresilient, and low-emission dairy sector in East Africa. The Component's strategic approach is to review and update national  and  regional  regulations,  policies,  standards,  and  investment  frameworks  that  promote  public  and  private investments aligned  with the NDCs,  ensuring these  promote  gender equality and social inclusion. It  also includes launching  nutrition  awareness  campaigns  on  milk  consumption  and  food  safety,  strengthening  public  and  private veterinary and extension services, improving national capacities for measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) of emissions and climate risk analysis. In addition, a carbon certification system will be developed to give the sector access to carbon finance. With a focus on institutional strengthening and knowledge dissemination at the national and regional levels, the Component ensures that the sector is well equipped to manage and adapt to the changing climate landscape.
4. Component 2 targets smallholder dairy farmers and cooperatives, with a view to a low-emission and climateresilient dairy sector. It will focus primarily on farm-level interventions, where 90 percent of sector's emissions occur, and aim to reduce total emissions and reduce emission intensities in all dairy production systems, moving beyond the business-as-usual scenario. On these farms, it will improve the efficiency in milk production (through animal health, breeding, fodder, and feeding interventions), improve nutrient circularity and recycling through manure management (including biogas production). It will invest in pasture and grassland management to increase ecosystem resilience and carbon sequestration. Interventions also include capacity building for smallholder farmers, comprising of women, men and youth, and cooperatives, including access to markets, the use of emission reduction technologies (renewable energies) and milk aggregation to reduce losses. Primarily benefiting smallholder dairy farmers,  Component 2 will encourage  the  adoption  of  productivity-enhancing  and  emission-reducing  technologies,  paving  the  way  for  these farmers to access finance through Component 3 for further growth and sustainability.
5. Component 3 is key to driving the transformation of the East Africa's dairy sector towards sustainability and climate resilience. It encompasses two strategies: (i) launching the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF) to provide green loans to climate-resilient dairy businesses, and (ii) provide high quality, well-targeted technical assistance to participating banks and the borrowing dairy-sector businesses to ensure the achievement of DaIMA's CC adaptation and mitigation targets and its sectoral transformation objectives. By addressing financial barriers, including those for women and the youth, and encouraging investment in sustainable practices, Component 3 aims to catalyze a significant shift in productivity, resilience, and reduced emissions in the East African dairy sector.
6. Implementation arrangements. As Accredited Entity (AE) for the Programme, IFAD will be responsible for overall management, which includes Programme appraisal, fiduciary and technical oversight, fund management, quality monitoring, and reporting to the GCF, resulting in the Programme completion and final evaluation. This role extends to IFAD's various offices, including its headquarters in Rome and regional office in Nairobi, as well as its country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda. A Regional Steering Committee will ensure regional alignment and added value  of  the  Programme.  National  Programme  Steering  Committees  (PSCs)  and  Programme  Coordination  Units (PCUs) will be established in each country to oversee (for the former) and coordinate (for the latter) the implementation of the Programme. International and national entities, including ministries of agriculture and specialized entities (e.g. the  International  Livestock  Research  Institute  (ILRI))  will  provide  targeted  services  to  the  national  implementation teams.

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

7. Programme costs and financing . The total cost of the Programme is US$ 358.26 million, of which: US$ 58.20 million  for  Component  1,  US$  234.98  million  for  Component  2,  US$  45.0  million  for  Component  3  (Green  Dairy Financing Facility), US$ 13.36 million for Programme management, and US$ 6.72 for monitoring and evaluation.
8. GCF  will  provide  US$132.50  million  grants  to  finance  Components  1,  2  and  3,  as  well  as  programme management and monitoring and evaluation (M&amp;E) to the 4 countries, IFAD will contribute US$193.28 million cofinancing in the four countries through its national projects, while the Governments will contribute US$14.98 million. Finally, for the Green Dairy Financing Facility under Component 3, a total of US$45 million is allocated. Of this amount, US$35 million will be for on-lending loan capital (GCF loan of US$17.5 million, IFAD loan of US$ 17.5 million).
9. Beneficiaries and impacts . The Programme aims to directly benefit 2.5 million people. The Programme aims to reduce the milk emission intensities by 29 percent, of which by 28 percent in Kenya, 50 percent in Rwanda, 29 percent in Tanzania and 33 percent in Uganda over a 20-year horizon. Over the same period, the Programme is expected to reduce total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms, while significantly increasing milk production by 34 percent, of which by 31 percent in Kenya, 73 percent in Rwanda, 36 percent in Tanzania, and 30 percent in Uganda. This increase in milk production will contribute to agricultural GDP growth in these four countries, marking a substantial impact on the economy and climate resilience of the region. Total GHG emissions from milk production are expected to decrease by 4.01 percent at the level of the Programme, or (i) by 2.02 percent (-61,967 tCO2-eq) in Kenya, (ii) by 9.56 percent (-56,507 tCO2-eq) in Rwanda, (iii) by 4.69 percent (-65,718 tCO2-eq) in Tanzania and, (iv) by 5.22 percent (-82,372  tCO2-eq)  in  Uganda.  Methane  from  enteric  fermentation  is  expected  to  decrease  by  4.61  percent,  while methane from manure management would decrease by 5.93 percent. The Programme is also expected to generate 54,435 tCO2eq/yr savings from energy generation and access.
10. Economic and financial analysis . The financial analysis shows that the scenario's proposed for different types of dairy farms are financially viable, while increasing milk yields and reducing emission intensities per litre of milk. At regional level, the aggregated Economic Net Present Value (ENPV) of the countries' incremental net benefit streams, discounted at 13.25 percent (social discount rate), is US$ 181.20 million, with an Economic Rate of Return (ERR) of 25.4 per cent for the baseline scenario. At a 6 per cent social discount rate, the ENPV is US$ 518.0 million. Two additional economic scenarios were tested by integrating the economic value of the Programme's GHG emissions balance with a low carbon market price (LCP) and with a high carbon market price (HCP). Under the LCP scenario, the ERR is 27.90 percent and the ENPV is US$ 217.87 million. Under the HCP scenario, the ERR is 30.30 percent and the ENPV is approximately US$ 254.64 million. The sensitivity analysis confirms that DaIMA represents an economically worthwhile investment.
11. Programme  will  directly  contribute  to  the  following  targeted  results  in  alignment  with  the  GCF's  Updated Strategic  Plan  (USP-2);  T1  (by  supporting  the  Country's  adaptation  and  mitigation  goals),  T4  Food  (2,5  million beneficiaries adopting low-emission climate-resilient dairy production practices) and T5 Ecosystems (178,362 hectares of  rangelands/pastures  to  be  conserved,  restored  and  sustainably  managed)  through  T9  locally-led  adaptation approach. The Programme will also support dissemination of climate information and MRV system, directly contributing to T3 -Climate Informational and Early Warning Systems (CIEWS). The Programme also aligns with T10 (by supporting resilient market chains) and T11 (by developing a Green Dairy Financing Facility).

## B. PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate context

For more details, see Annex 2 (Feasibility Study)

12. Smallholder farmers dominate agriculture in East Africa. They rely heavily on livestock for their livelihoods 2 , more particularly dairy,  which are found  in  various  production  systems  and  diverse  agroecologies,  all  driven  by climate. The region is dominated by a tropical savannah climate, separated by a corridor of arid steppe and desert climates parallel to the Indian Ocean coastline and northwest of Kenya, as well as in the central parts of Tanzania. Tropical rainforest conditions are found in the Lake Victoria and temperate climates are present along the highest altitude areas of the Great Rift Valley bordering Kenya and Tanzania. Kenya is the driest among the four targeted countries, with a total annual rainfall ranging between 400 to 600mm in the northwest of Lake Turkana and in areas bordering Somalia. Conversely, Uganda and Rwanda have the highest total annual precipitation, ranging between 1000  and  1600mm.  These  conditions  lead  to  a  mosaic  of  climates  and,  consequently,  to  a  wide  range  of agroecological zones.
13. The dairy sector in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda is highly vulnerable to climate change (CC) and is characterized  by  high  GHG  emissions,  especially  methane 3 .  The  four  countries  face  significant  CC  challenges, including extreme weather events and changing rainfall patterns 4 . In the dairy sector, the impacts of CC include heat stress  on  dairy  animals,  prolonged  and  more  recurrent  droughts,  intensifying  heavy  rainfall,  flooding,  and  other extreme weather events that reduce dairy cow productivity. Indirect effects include changes in disease patterns and occurrence, decreased feed and water availability, and decreased pasture quality 5 . As a result, the Programme is cross-cutting and addresses both mitigation and adaptation. Section B.1 describes the adaptation needs following IPCC's AR5 Risk Conceptualization (compounded effect of observed and projected climate hazards, exposure6, vulnerability 7 ,  modulated  by the existing adaptive capacity 8 ), as well as the mitigation needs based on the GHG emission profile of the dairy sector. 9

## CLIMATE HAZARDS 10

14. East Africa's dairy systems and their spatial distribution are diverse, exposing dairy farmers' livelihoods to a wide range of hazards. Climate change poses risks both because of the potential impacts of weather hazards and

## Observed climate change

15. Precipitation declined  over  most  of  the  four  countries  during  the  1950s  (-6.7mm/yr.)  and  the  1960s  (21.8mm/yr.) but increased during the 1970s (+16.0mm/yr.) 11 . There are also statistically significant differences on seasonal precipitation trends (p-value 0.05) across central Uganda between January and March, extending to the north of Kenya and northern Tanzania between April and June where seasonal precipitation rates have declined at a rate of -4 to -8 mm/yr. over 1981-2016. On the other hand, a precipitation increase has also been observed in the southeast of Tanzania during the austral summer (January to March) over 1981-2016 (Figure 1).

Figure 1. Changes in accumulated seasonal precipitation (mm) over the 1981- 2016 period using the W5E5 dataset 12

January-March (JFM)

<!-- image -->

April-June (AMJ)         July- September (JAS)   October-November (OND)

<!-- image -->

16. Temperature . The annual average maximum temperature (mean Tmax) has increased significantly (p-value 0.05) across Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania. In Uganda (Eastern and Northern region) and Kenya (north of Lake Turkana County), for example, the annual mean Tmax has increased by +0.030 to +0.035°C/yr., equivalent to +1.1 to +1.3°C during 1981-2016. In addition to having the highest mean Tmax increase, the Kenyan north Rift Valley has the warmest mean Tmax year-round among the four targeted countries. There is also a significant change in average daily minimum temperature (mean Tmin) in Kenya and Tanzania, but not in Rwanda or the west of Uganda. For mean Tmin, the increase is observed in the east of Uganda and the northwest of Kenya where temperatures have increased by +0.035ºC/yr., equivalent to +1.3ºC during 1981-2016.
17. Mean relative humidity . The Rift Valley in Kenya has the lowest average annual relative humidity (mean RH &lt;50 percent), while central and southern Uganda, especially the areas bordering Lake Victoria, have the highest mean RH (75-85 percent). The Kenyan and Tanzanian coastlines are characterized by high mean RH (70 to 80 percent) all year round due to the incoming moisture from the Indian Ocean. A statistically significant decrease (pvalue 0.05) in mean RH between -0.10 and -0.25 percent/year was observed in most areas of Uganda and Rwanda between 1981 and 2016. In addition, eastern and northeastern Kenya have experienced a statistically significant decrease (p-value 0.05), while central and southern Tanzania have experienced the largest decline (-0.10 to -0.15 percent/year).
18. Rainfall  variability .  The  region's  climatic  seasons  are  influenced  by  a  coupled  transboundary  oceanatmosphere phenomenon called the Inter Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ). A long rainy season occurs from March to May (MAM), and a short rainy season from October to December (OND) when the ITCZ migrates southwards. There is high rainfall variability in OND due to large-scale oceanic-atmospheric patterns such as the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD), which frequently coincides with ENSO events. The relationship

11  Ongoma, V., &amp; Chen, H. (2017). Temporal and spatial variability of temperature and precipitation over East Africa from 1951 to 2010. Meteorology and Atmospheric Physics, 129, 131-144.

12  Source: Annex 2: Feasibility Study

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

between  the  3-month  Standardized  Precipitation  Index  (SPI-3)  and  ENSO  is  significant  for  understanding precipitation variability in East Africa (Figure 2). SPI-3 categorizes precipitation conditions, indicating whether they are normal (-0.99 and +0.99), moderately dry (-1.49 to -1.0) and moderately humid (+1.0 to +1.49), severely dry (1.99 to -1.5) and severely humid (+1.5 to +1.99), and extremely dry (≤ -2.0) and extremely humid (≥ +2.0). During the strong El Niño of 1997-1998, SPI-3 values indicated very humid conditions, while the subsequent strong La Niña of 1999-2000 resulted in severe dry conditions over East Africa. The impacts of ENSO are apparent, since peak ENSO events occur between October and March, coinciding with the short (October to November) and long (March to May) rainy seasons in East Africa, further amplifying precipitation anomalies.

## Extreme weather events

19. Dry-spells and very wet days . The average annual number of dry days (R≤1mm/day) in East Africa exceeds 300 days in the northern Rift Valley and northeastern Kenya. Conversely, the annual number of dry days decreases toward Lake Victoria (including Mount Elgon in Uganda and Kenya) and the tropical rainforest near the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). On average, Tanzania records 180 dry days/yr. along its border with Uganda and Rwanda and over 280 days in its interior. The Indian Ocean coastline has seen an increase in dry days by +1.0 days/yr., equivalent to +36 dry days per year since 1981. In eastern Uganda, the number of dry days by -0.50 days/yr., equivalent  to  +18  wet  days  on  average  each  year  since  1981.  Trends  on  very  wet  days  (R≥20mm/day)  show statistically significant differences (p-value &lt;0.05) along the Great Rift Valley in Kenya and Tanzania, with an average annual increase of +0.15 days/yr. over the period 1981-2016, equivalent to an additional +5 very wet days on annual average since 1981 (Figure 2). In contrast, neither the interior of Kenya, Tanzania nor Uganda have seen an increase or decrease in the number of very wet days since 1981.

Figure 2. Spatiotemporal analysis of SPI-3-month over the 1981-2016 period in (a) Kenya, (b) Rwanda, (c) Uganda, and (d) Tanzania 13

20. Extreme temperatures .  Most  of  the  change  in  the  number  of  very  warm  days  (Tmax  ≥35ºC)  on  annual average is reported in the northern Rift Valley, where the number of very warm days has increased by +1.2 to +2.8 days/yr., which is equivalent to an additional 40 to 100 very warm days as an annual average since 1981. In addition, northern Uganda experienced an increase in very warm days at a rate of +0.8 days/yr. although this increase is not statistically significant.

Climate  and Agriculture Risk Visualization  and Assessment  (CAVA)  is  an approach to climate information with a focus on agriculture. Its goal is to provide long-term, climate services by democratising access and simplifying the use of past and future climate and impact data. CAVA analytics uses the W5E5 merged dataset over the 19762005 period to bias-correct the outputs of atmospheric models. More information is available here: https://fao-cava.predictia.es/

B

<!-- image -->

21. Annual precipitation . Some parts of East Africa may experience an increase in rainfall in the distant future (Figure 3) under various Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) 15 . Arid and semi-arid regions are expected to receive less precipitation, while the highlands and lake regions may receive more rainfall. Dry seasons in Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda could see an increase in precipitation. Future projections indicate an eastward increase in Kenya under RCP 2.6 (+100mm), accentuated under RCP 8.5 (+100 to +200mm) in the distant future (2070-2099). In  Rwanda,  a  decrease  in  rainfall  is  expected  under  RCP  2.6  (-0.20mm/yr.)  and  an  increase  under  RCP  8.5 (+0.18mm/yr.). In Uganda, climate projections show a decrease in rainfall of -0.30mm/yr. under both RCPs (Figure 3).
22. Mean maximum temperatures . Under RCP 2.6, the mean Tmax are expected to peak in the mid-term (20402069); whereas under RCP 8.5, the highest mean Tmax increase is expected by the end of the century (2070-2099). Under RCP 8.5, mean Tmax may increase by up to +4.5 o C in central parts of East Africa by the end of the century (2070-2099). The rate of increase in both RCPs is slower (+1 to +2 o C depending on the RCP) towards the ocean since water can hold more heat than air, thereby taking longer to warm up than continental parts. Accordingly, the coastal areas of Kenya and Tanzania may experience a mean Tax increase of +0.5 to +1.0 o C (RCP 2.6) and +2.0 to +3.0 o C (RCP 8.5), respectively, by the end-of-century (2070-2099) compared to the baseline period (1976-2005). Similarly, because of its large size, Lake Victoria may warm more slowly than its surroundings and, thus, have a lower rate of mean Tmax increase over time. Overall, most models agree on a positive climate change signal for mean Tmax (Figure 4).

23. Mean minimum temperatures .  The mean Tmin increase over East Africa is expected to follow the same spatiotemporal pattern as mean Tmax. However, the rate of change in mean Tmin is slightly higher to that of mean Tmax. Overall, mean Tmin could increase by +1.5 to +2.0ºC (RCP 2.6) and by +4.0 to +5.0ºC (RCP 8.5) inland and by less than +1.5ºC (RCP 2.6) and +3.0ºC (RCP 8.5) on the Tanzanian and Kenyan coast by 2070-2099 compared to 1976-2005.
24. The Mean relative humidity (mean RH) will change over the course of the century for RCPs 2.6 and 8.5. The annual mean RH (+2 %) is expected to increase along Kenya and Lake Victoria. Under RCP 8.5, however, the mean RH is expected to decrease on a yearly average (-2 to -8 %) in the central parts of Tanzania, with a high rate towards the south.
25. Rainfall variability .  In all  countries, positive SPI-3 values will remain lower under RCP 8.5 than RCP 2.6, implying fewer wet-years under RCP 8.5. For negative SPI-3 values (dry periods), negative trends are accentuated in Rwanda, Uganda, and Tanzania, implying that 3-month precipitation values are increasingly more dispersed from the mean.
26. Dry spells .  The  models show a positive agreement in the time of emergence on the number of dry days (R≤1mm/day), slightly earlier and widespread for RCP 8.5 (2030-2050) compared to RCP 2.6 (2040-2060). The positive  change  (increase  in  the  number  of  dry  days)  is  widespread  throughout  East  Africa,  particularly  towards western Kenya, all of Uganda and Rwanda, and most parts of Tanzania. Conversely, the timing of the emergence of negative values (reduction in number of dry days) is projected from 2060 onwards for RCP 8.5 and a little earlier for RCP 2.6, mainly in eastern and southern Kenya. In addition, most of the increase in very wet days (R≥20mm/day) is projected in southwestern Kenya under RCP 2.6, where there could be an extra +1 to +2 very wet days on average per year by the end of the century (2070-2099). For RCP 8.5, the increase in very wet days is widespread over Uganda, Rwanda, Tanzania, and southwestern Kenya (Figure 5).

27. Extreme temperatures . Very warm days (Tmax ≥35ºC) are expected to increase in frequency and intensity (Figure 6). In the low emission scenario (RCP 2.6), +25 to +75 additional very warm days on average per year are expected in Uganda, the Rift Valley of Kenya, and central Tanzania in the mid-term (2040-2069). Most of the increase in very warm days is projected by the end of the century (2070-2099) under RCP 8.5, showing an increase in the range of 100 to 200 additional very warm days on average per year compared to 1981-2010. On the contrary, the cooling  effect of large  water masses such as Lake Victoria and the Indian Ocean can significantly modulate the upward trend of very warm days over time.

## EXPOSURE

28. Exposure is the presence of people, livelihoods, species, and ecosystems that could be affected by hazards, more particularly droughts and floods that cause disasters (Figure 7).
29. In East Africa, droughts tend to be more frequent, longer, and more severe in boreal spring and summer, as rainfall and water storage decrease overall. Table 1 shows how drought will change under different climate scenarios. Haile et al. (2020) show that the drought area in East Africa will increase, with extreme droughts increasing faster than severe and moderate droughts. For example, by the end of the century (2080s), severe droughts are expected to increase by 13.5 percent under RCP 8.5. In general, farmers can cope with mild droughts, but severe droughts lead to large-scale livestock losses, decreased pasture regeneration, and an increased prevalence of drought-related livestock  diseases. 20  Overall,  droughts  affect  the  availability  and  quality  of  water  for  pasture  regeneration,  feed production, water and hygienic practices in the dairy production system.

19  Source: Annex 2: Feasibility Study

20  Huho, J. M., &amp; Mugalavai, E. M. (2010). The Effects of Droughts on Food Security in Kenya. The International Journal of Climate Change: Impacts and Responses, 2(2).

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

30. Floods .  East Africa experiences frequent flooding due to climate variability and anthropogenic factors that exacerbate livestock exposure to flooding (Figure 7). Among its flood-prone areas, Uganda has the highest number of flood-related casualties, particularly in the Northern, Eastern, and Central regions along the White Nile River. Each year, floods affect between 10,000 and 25,000 people per administrative boundary in these regions of Uganda, with up to 36,000 people per administrative boundary in the Northern region. In Tanzania, floods affect 10,000 to 25,000 people per administrative boundary in Tanga, Morogoro, and Pwani. In contrast, in the central parts of Kenya, fewer than 100 people per administrative boundary were affected by floods on an annual basis. Due to the increase in population and increased rainfall in Lake Victoria under RCP 8.5, flooding along the White River Nile is likely to increase.
31. Disasters . In East Africa, between 1971 and 2023, more than 95 million people were affected by disasters caused by meteorological (storms), hydrological (floods, landslides) and climatological (droughts) 21  events. Since 1971, 248 disasters have occurred, of which 78 disasters in Kenya, 70 in Tanzania, 60 in Uganda, and 40 in Rwanda. About 69 percent related to floods and 17 percent to drought. Over time, the number of disasters has increased exponentially, from 28 during 1971-1990 to 174 during 2001-2020. The most common disaster is flooding, followed by drought and landslides. Between 1900 and 2023, 176 floods were recorded in the targeted countries, most of them river floods caused by heavy rainfall. Evidence shows that women and men are affected by drought differently, and that gender inequalities diminish women's capacity to cope with these calamities.

## VULNERABILITY

32. CC also poses many challenges and threats to the East African dairy sector, which is subject to pre-existing physical and socio-economic vulnerabilities. By reducing these vulnerabilities, which will be exacerbated by climate change, the sector becomes more resilient. These vulnerabilities are presented below:

21  Centre for Research on the Epidemiology of Disasters (CRED) database (2023). 2022 Disasters in Numbers. Available at: link

22  Based on Haile et al. (2020)

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

- a) Poverty  and  income  inequality. Kenyan,  Tanzanian,  Rwandan  and  Ugandan  societies  are  marked  by important poverty and income inequality. The Human Development index (HDI) 2022 ranking in Kenya (146 th ), Rwanda (159 th ), Uganda (159 th ) and Tanzania (160 th ) reflect low achievements in some of the key dimensions of human development, while high GINI indices (38.7 in Kenya, 43.7 in Rwanda, 40.5 in Tanzania and 42.7 in Uganda) 23  evidence strong income inequalities.  The vulnerable - more particularly poor dairy farmers and women 24  - will be the most affected by climate change.
- b) Degraded of rangelands and ecosystems .  The availability of feed depends on the quality of rangelands, which are degraded in the region. Figure 8 shows that degraded land accounts for 27 percent of total rangelands areas in Tanzania, 3.6 percent in Kenya, 2.1 percent in Uganda and 2.1 percent in Rwanda. The main factors leading to the degradation of rangelands and pasture are soil structure and erodibility, climate (rainfall intensity and  temperature),  reduction  of  vegetation  cover  and  topography  (influencing  water  runoff  capacity). Degradation  prevents  rangelands  from  providing  ecosystem  services  such  as provisioning (production  of above-ground biomass, such as grass, as well as water) and regulating (such as the capacity of rangelands to sequester carbon in the soils 25 ). It also has a negative impact on the forage balance, as well as on the livestock carrying capacity, which highlights the pressure that the land is under in terms of maximum number of animals that the ecosystem can sustainably support over time, without causing damages to the environment (Feasibility Study, Annex 2).
- c) Limited  access  to  quality  inputs .  Feed  that  reduces  enteric  emissions,  veterinary  services  and  climateresilient breeding stocks are difficult to access for dairy smallholder farmers. Limited veterinary services lead farmers to prefer local or crossbred animals that are not as high yielding, whereas exotic breeds are highly susceptible to local disease. In addition, limited access to improved breeds hinders genetic progress.
- d) Limited water availability. Climate  change  affects  water  resources  with  changes  in  precipitation  patterns leading to water scarcity or excessive water supply in different regions. In food insecure areas, limited access to  safe  and  reliable  water  resources,  and  limited  water  harvesting  systems  can  hinder  livestock  and  dairy production, contributing to food insecurity and increased vulnerability, especially for women who often provide labour for livestock.
- e) Frequent disease outbreaks . Foot-and-mouth disease, mastitis, and East Coast Fever (ECF) are common livestock diseases. Disease outbreaks result in a high mortality rate, reduced milk production, and increased veterinary  costs.  Poor  disease  control  measures,  inadequate  quarantine  facilities,  and  limited  veterinary services contribute to the prevalence of diseases in the dairy farming sector.

Figure 8. Level of degradation of rangelands and pastures in the four countries between 2012 and 2020 (left) and carrying capacity, expressed in above-ground biomass surplus (ton of dry matter) (right) 26

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

<!-- image -->

- f) Dairy  processing  facilities,  energy  supply  and  access  to  markets .  Dairy  farmers  in  East  Africa  face inadequate infrastructure along the dairy value chain. The availability of reliable (and sustainable) energy supply is  also  irregular,  which  affects  the  ability  to  cool  and  process  milk,  and  reduce  losses.  Additionally,  price fluctuations  and  market  volatility  are  common  challenges.  Most  milk  prices  are  determined  by  bulkers  and processors.  Access  to  stable  higher-value-added  markets  is  further  hindered  by  limited  outreach  of  milk collection centers (MCCs), dairy cooperatives and business alliances. More details are provided in the Market Assessment (Annex 2).
- g) Limited access to credit and financial services .  Critical aspects of dairy farming, such as infrastructure, animal  health,  and  feed,  require  access  to  credit  and  finance.  Small-scale  dairy  farmers  and  pastoralists, especially women and young people, have difficulties accessing these services, due to lack of collateral, which limits investments in modern equipment, infrastructure, and improved livestock.
- h) Land tenure . In Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda, a main risk for intensive grazing is tenure insecurity, i.e. tenure rights or land ownership. Access to natural capital, particularly women's and youth ownership, management and control of land and land resources are major barriers to sustainability, and the adoption of climate-resilient and low-carbon practices (Annex 2).

## CLIMATE ADAPTIVE CAPACITY

33. About 70-90 percent of East African agriculture is rain-fed, with small-scale farmers relying on seasonal rainfall for  feed,  fodder  crops  and  pasture.  As  dairy  systems  are  subject  to  significant  seasonal,  temporal,  and  spatial variations, dairy farmers face extreme difficulties in planning investments, which also become riskier under CC. The resilience of the dairy system depends on its ability to adapt to different climate conditions and to cope with the impacts of CC. Identifying adaptation needs facilitates prioritizing actions and allocating resources for addressing critical challenges.
34. Rising temperatures are disrupting agriculture, livestock and dairy, the economic backbone of East Africa. Water  scarcity,  pest  outbreaks,  and  declining  productivity  threaten  food  and  nutrition  security.  Droughts  and deforestation exacerbate soil erosion. The intensification and frequency of droughts will hinder access to water under different climate scenarios. Areas affected by extreme drought will expand faster than those affected by severe and moderate droughts, especially under RCP 8.5. Storms and floods have become more frequent and intense, causing deaths, damage to infrastructure and loss of livelihoods. The dairy sector's adaptive capacity stems from the following:

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

- a) Access to climate-smart 27  practices  and  technologies .  Improved  dairy  breeds,  drought-tolerant  forage varieties, and efficient milk processing equipment are some of the climate-smart technologies farmers need. These practices and technologies should be labor saving to reduce workloads for women.
- b) Capacity building and knowledge transfer . Farmers' technical knowledge and skills in dairy management can be enhanced by providing training programmes, Livestock Farmer Field Schools (L-FFS), and extension services on sustainable feeding practices, breeding strategies, disease control measures and water resource efficiency.
- c) Financial  and  market  support .  Affordable  financing  supports  farmers'  investments  in  productive  assets, infrastructure, and value-addition. Dairy cooperatives and value chain linkages can improve farmers' incomes. Agribusiness can provide input and output markets that improve the productivity of smallholder dairy farmers 28 .
- d) Strengthening infrastructure .  Improving  rural  infrastructure,  such  as  water  harvesting  systems,  renewable energy, milk collection and processing facilities, will reduce transport costs and improve market access for dairy farmers, and reduce the drudgery associated with fetching water by women.
- e) Climate information .  Informed decisions on feed management, breeding and disease control can be made through climate information, early warning systems, and advisory services. This will help dairy farmers anticipate and adapt to climate-related risks. These skills equip youth as agents and service providers in dissemination of new information.
- f) Policy support and institutional strengthening . To enable dairy farmers to thrive, it is imperative to develop policies, regulations, and institutions that are tailored to their needs,  especially addressing barriers related to gender  inequalities  and  youth,  as  well  as  strengthening  research  and  extension  actors.  Key  adaptation requirements require collaboration between governments, research institutes, dairy cooperatives, and financial institutions.

## IMPACT OF CLIMATE CHANGE

35. East Africa's dairy sector is one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change, the impact of which are felt at all levels of the dairy value chain (Figure 9). The impacts at the dairy farm level are as follows: (i) a decline in milk yields, especially of high milk-yielding breeds; (ii) heat stress in dairy cattle; (iii) increased development of pathogens, diseases, parasite and vectors in dairy cattle; (iv) decreased feed production and productivity, due to reduced or excess soil moisture and fertility; (v) a decrease in the quantity and quality of pasture; (vi) deterioration of fodder in an unprotected warehouse. At the rangeland level, livestock owners face: (i) reduced access to rangelands resources; (ii) ecosystem degradation; (iii) a decrease in rangeland and pasture productivity and forage quality. At the dairy value chain level, high temperatures compromise the safety of milk and dairy products, higher risks of milk losses, and higher costs per unit of produce.

Figure 9 . Potential impacts of climate hazards on the livestock land-based food supply chain 29

For  more  details,  please  see  https://www.fao.org/climate-smartagriculture/en/

B

<!-- image -->

<!-- image -->

36. Climate change and milk yields. Temperature and humidity have a significant impact on  milk production ( Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Chapter 2). Significant milk production losses have been observed in Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania's coastal regions between 1981 and 2016, from 4 to 8 kg/day for the Holstein-Friesian breed and between 0.5 and 1 kg/day for the Boran breed. Over the period 1981-2016, milk production declined less than 2 kg/day  for  the  Holstein-Friesian  breed  in  the  high-altitude  areas  of  Kenya,  Tanzania,  and  Rwanda.  Future  milk production is expected to decrease up to 1 kg/day for Holstein-Friesian cows and up to 0.15 kg/day for Boran animals by the end of the century under RCP 2.6, and even up to 2.5 kg/day for Holstein Friesian cows and 0.5 kg/day for Boran cows under RCP 8.5 30 . Heat stress also causes reproductive problems (missed mating or insemination).
37. Ruminant feed and forage supplies are most affected by climate change due to changes in cereal, forage, and rangeland productivity . Changes in temperature and CO2 levels will affect the composition of pastures by altering the species competition dynamics due to changes in optimal growth rates 31 . In addition, availability of rainfall determines rainfed crop production. Feed and fodder are available in the rainy and early dry seasons and sparse in the dry season, leading to strong seasonal fluctuations in milk production and milk prices. Even during the rainy seasons, there is often not adequate forage (fodder and pasture) to be stored for utilization during the dry season 32 . Feed availability fluctuates throughout the year, challenging livestock production. It can also lead to conflicts between communities and competition with wildlife in pastoral areas. Pastoral communities are more vulnerable to feed shortages because of droughts, degradation of rangelands, and limited access to grazing lands. Both RCPs predict the greatest yield change in the north of Kenya and Uganda in the mid- and long-terms (2031-2060 and 20612090). Under RCP 2.6, maize yields, commonly used as feed, are likely to decline by 5 to 15 percent in Uganda and by 20 to 25 percent in Kenya under RCP 8.5. Napier grass yields are likely to decline mostly in Uganda (e.g., North region by more than 30 percent), the north of Kenya by 30 percent, and the southeast of Tanzania by 20 to 30 percent. Under RCP 8.5, Napier grass yield losses are up to 10 percent lower in the southwest of Tanzania both in the midterm (2031-2060) and into the future (2061-2090). For maize, models show that Africa is likely to suffer up to 10 percent yield loss by 2055 due to CC.
38. Climate  change  will  affect  pathogens,  vectors,  hosts,  or  reservoirs ,  as  well  as  human  behaviour. Estimating climate change effects on livestock diseases is difficult due to the complex interactions between climatic and non-climatic factors. Temperature and humidity play a significant role in pathogen and parasite development. Wind patterns affect the movement of locusts and pathogens. Pathogen propagation may be limited by hot and dry conditions, changes in rainfall and temperature regimes which influence disease vector distribution, such as ticks, mosquitoes and flies. Vector dynamics are also influenced by soil and vegetation type, and topography. In most cases,

31  Rojas-Downing,  M.M.,  Nejadhashemi,  A.P.,  Harrigan,  T.  and  Woznicki,  S.A.,  2017.  Climate  change  and  livestock:  Impacts, adaptation, and mitigation. Climate risk management, 16, pp.145-163. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S221209631730027X

32  Mudavadi, O. et al. (2020). Effects of Season Variation on Water, Feed, Milk Yield and Reproductive Performance of Dairy Cows in Smallholder Farms in Eastern Africa. Journal of Agriculture and Ecology Research International.

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

vectors and pathogens negatively affect livestock. Zoonotic diseases spread during droughts and feed shortages. Further details are available in Annex 2, Chapter 2.

39. The above impacts of climate change on milk production at farm level will have a direct impact on national  and  household  food  and  nutrition  security .  At  household  level,  less  milk  will  be  available  for  dairy consumption, with an immediate impact on nutrition and health of young children and women. It will also reduce revenues from sales of milk. In the four countries, small daily revenues from the sales of surplus milk, often 1 to 5 litres per household, are essential for rural poverty reduction. At national level, a reduction of domestic milk production will lead to higher imports from the international market and require monetary reserves. Also, a higher dependence on the international market to purchase feed, in particular maize and soybean, will impact the national balance of trade.

## GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSION PROFILE OF THE DAIRY SECTOR

40. Dairy production systems . In Kenya, 39 percent of milk is produced by intensive systems or zero-grazing systems, 43 percent by semi-intensive systems, and 18 percent by extensive systems. In Rwanda, 93 percent of the milk  is  produced  in  intensive/zero  grazing  systems,  4  percent  in  semi-intensive  and  3  percent  in  extensive. In Tanzania, dairy systems are traditional and intensive. The traditional system is characterized by meat-milk production and large herds of cattle grazing natural pastures, producing 91 percent of the country's milk. In Uganda, systems are also traditional and intensive (Figure 10). Traditional systems produce 98 percent of the country's milk. Further disaggregation of systems in Annex 2 (Chapter 2.2) .
41. Absolute GHG emissions (baseline, present and future). Methane (CH4), nitrous oxide (N2O) and carbon dioxide (CO2) are released by ruminants (cattle, sheep, goats) during the enteric fermentation and through manure production. 34  East Africa represents about 5% of GHG emissions of the global cattle sector, but they are increasing. Enteric  methane  emissions  from  the  dairy  sector  dominate  the  GHG  emission  inventories  of  Kenya,  Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda, followed by cattle manure. Enteric methane is not only a short-lived climate pollutant, but it represents a waste in the production process that doesn't result in milk or meat. In addition, cattle manure releases N2O and CH4. Over 90 percent of dairy sector emissions occur on farm 35 . Land-use changes and degradation further contribute to GHG emissions due to the disruption in carbon stocks.

Figure 10 . Livestock production systems in East Africa 33

B

<!-- image -->

42. In Kenya, GHG emissions have increased from 57 MtCO2eq in 1955 to 94 MtCO2eq in 2015 36 . Agriculture produces half of all emissions. The GLEAM 37  tool of the Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations (FAO), estimated that dairy cattle (4.5 million animals) emit 6.4 MtCO2 eq (based on 2019 data). The dairy sector emits the most CH4 and N2O at 93 percent and 6.6 percent respectively. About 82 percent of CH4 emissions are caused by enteric methane, while 11 percent are caused by manure management. While business-as-usual (BAU) emissions from agriculture are projected to reach 142 MtCO2eq by 2030, FAO's GLEAM tool estimates that total milk production and enteric CH4 emissions will respectively increase by 43 and 36 percent by 2050 under current practices, in a baseline scenario carried out at national level.
43. In Rwanda , total GHG emissions were estimated to be 6.7 MtCO ₂ eq in 2018, of which 3.3 MtCO ₂ eq, or 49 percent  from  agriculture.  During  the  last  decade,  agriculture,  forestry,  and  other  land  use  (AFOLU)  emissions increased  by 34 percent. Methane emissions from the livestock sector represent about 46 percent of total GHG emissions. Enteric fermentation accounts for 74 percent of emissions, followed by CH4 and N2O emissions from manure management (15 percent and 11 percent, respectively). Dairy systems in Rwanda emit about 3.6 MtCO2-eq in 2020 38 . Crossbred cattle contribute 58 percent to methane emissions in zero-grazing systems, while exotic dairy cattle contribute only 0.2 percent. Zero grazing practices account for 93 percent of these emissions, semi-grazing systems 2 percent, and open grazing 5 percent. FAO's GLEAM model estimates that by 2050 milk production and enteric CH4 emissions will increase by 64 percent and 59 percent respectively, under a baseline scenario.
44. In Tanzania , total GHG emissions are estimated to be 286.5 MtCO ₂ eq 39 , to which agriculture contributed 49.7 MtCO ₂ eq or 17 percent. Methane emissions represent 94 percent of total GHG emissions from dairy cattle systems. Enteric fermentation counts for 92 percent of GHG emissions, and manure management by 2 percent. Emissions from the dairy sector are expected to increase by mid-century, in response to the projected dairy herd growth to satisfy the future demand for dairy products, which is expected to grow from 1.4 billion litres of raw milk in 2020, to 2.0 billion litres in 2030, and 8 billion litres in 2050. Projections based on milk demand under a BAU scenario (from 2020 data), will be associated with an increase of enteric methane emissions by 43 percent, escalating from 19 to 27 Mt of CO2eq between 2020 and 2030. FAO's GLEAM model estimates that enteric CH4 emissions will increase by 117 percent by 2050, under a baseline scenario.
45. GHG emissions  in Uganda totalled  95  MtCO2eq  in  2017,  of  which  the  AFOLU  sector  accounted  for  79 MtCO2eq 40  or 84 percent. The GLEAM tool estimated that the dairy sector in 2019 24.4 MtCO2eq or 30 percent of the AFOLU sector's emissions. CH4 emissions from enteric fermentation (78 percent, 19 MtCO2eq) and CH4 and N2O emissions from manure management (4 percent or 2 MtCO2eq) dominate GHG emissions from dairy cattle sector and mostly concentrated in the Eastern, Central, Southern and Northeast (Karamoja) regions. FAO's GLEAM model estimates that milk production and enteric CH4 emissions will increase by 80 percent by 2050, under a baseline scenario.
46. Total GHG emissions from dairy cattle in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda are presented in Figure 11. The pattern follows the distribution of the dairy herd in the country.

47. Emission Intensities (EI). The methane emission intensity, defined as the volume of methane emissions generated per kg of milk, varies widely across intensive, semi-intensive and extensive dairy production systems, with more productive systems having much lower EIs per kg of milk. In East Africa, EIs vary from 1.5 kg CO2eq./kg milk for exotic dairy breeds in Kenya to 24.2 kg CO2eq./kg milk for traditional systems in Uganda. This shows the range of performances between (but also within) systems and the potential for improvements, including  by importance of increasing  the  productivity  in  dairy  production  and  promoting  intensive  and  semi-intensive  practices  from  dairy systems with a low emission intensity per kg of milk. Details per country and per dairy production system are presented in Figure 12.
48. In Kenya, the enteric CH4 EI is on average 2 kg CO2eq./kg of milk produced, ranging from 1.4 kg CO2eq./kg milk in intensive exotic systems to 5 kg CO2eq./kg milk in extensive indigenous systems. Emissions intensities were on average higher for the indigenous systems (3.5 kg CO2eq./kg milk) then for exotic systems (1.5 kg CO2eq./kg milk).
49. In Rwanda's dairy herd, crossbreeds account for 63 percent of enteric methane emissions, local breeds for 29 percent, and exotic breeds for 8 percent. The intensity of enteric CH4 emissions lays between 1.8 (exotic/ crossbreed pasture grazing) and 4.6 kg (local semi-grazing) of CO2eq. per kg of milk. This highlights the importance of mitigation strategies targeting the crossbreeds. Approximately 93 percent of the dairy sector emissions are attributed to zero grazing practices.
50. In Tanzania, enteric methane represents about 92 percent of total GHG emissions. About 97 percent of enteric CH4 emissions are associated with traditional dairy cattle systems, whereas improved dairy systems contribute 3 percent. The intensity of enteric CH4 emissions varies between 1.9 and 19 kg of CO2eq. per kg of milk. Unlike CH4 production, CH4 intensity is notably elevated in the traditional system when compared to the improved system.

The highest emission intensity is observed for pastoral systems at 24.2 kg CO2eq./kg milk.

## PROPOSED CLIMATE SOLUTIONS

52. The Programme's interventions will address the climate change adaptation and mitigation issues across the various dairy production systems and along the dairy value-chain, while addressing the barriers identified in the theory of change (ToC, see B.2). It will scale-up: (i) farm and herd level practices to increase productivity, reduce emission intensity,  and  decrease  absolute  emissions  compared  to  a  BAU  scenario;  (ii) farm  level solutions  for  manure management and nutrient  recycling,  to  improve  circularity,  resilience  and  reduce  emissions;  (iii) landscape-level interventions to improve the resilience and services of rangelands ecosystems, particularly soil carbon sequestration, and water and biomass supply for animal feed; and (iv) value-chain level interventions, to reduce milk losses (and corresponding GHG emissions) and increase the use of renewable energy. This holistic and sustainable approach, that  will  also  address  enablers  such  as  access  to  markets,  finance,  knowledge  and  services,  will  lead  to  lower emissions from the dairy sector when compared to the business-as-usual scenario. A detailed table, linking the climate drivers with interventions and their benefits is available in Annex 2, Chapter 6.
53. Interventions at farm and herd levels. Interventions will focus on the various climate risks and impacts, namely the decrease in milk yields, heat stress of animals, the increase in cattle mortality, the change in dairy cattle diseases, the decrease in feed productivity, the deterioration of fodder in unprotected warehouses due to excess humidity. For all dairy systems, the Programme will encourage the combined adoption of climate adaptation and mitigation  practices,  including  the  enhancement  of  animal  genetic  resources  (artificial  insemination  with  locallyadapted improved and indigenous livestock breeds, sexed semen), improved herd health (e.g. vaccination against East Coast Fever), animal welfare, water access, recycling, harvesting and reuse for cattle watering. In intensive and semi-intensive systems, in  addition  to  the  above,  interventions will improve the availability of forage grasses and legumes, feeding practices, and use of conserved silage and complementary feed. In intensive systems, interventions will include the establishment of forage grasses and legumes (Calliandra, Leucaena, etc.), the use of conserved silage

<!-- image -->

grazing

<!-- image -->

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

and urea-molasses multi-nutrient blocks (UMMB), the use of methane inhibitors (e.g. tannins rich Calliandra, lipids), supplementation (use of non-conventional feed resources, e.g. biochar or sweet potato vines), precision feeding and improving animal welfare and housing. In extensive systems, activities will include improving pasture management (e.g. rotational grazing), the use improved pasture species and management practices to improve forage quality and quantity, and use of multipurpose leguminous trees.

54. On-farm solutions for manure management and nutrient recycling. These interventions will address the CH4 and N2O emissions on dairy farms and limits related to the cost and availability of mineral fertilizers. Interventions include  promoting  simple  circular  manure  management  practices  to  reduce  nutrient  losses  and  GHG  emissions (covering, compaction, composting, liquid-solid separation, production and addition of biochar). The Programme will also  invest  in  biogas  solutions  to  produce  renewable  energy  for  cooking  and  electricity,  as  well  as  nutrient-rich biofertilizer.
55. Interventions at the landscape level will  address  the  negative  impact  of CC  on ecosystem degradation (rangeland and pastures) that result in reduced soil carbon sequestration, decreased quantity and quality of pasture, reduced feed and water availability and livestock carrying capacity, all leading to lower milk yields. The interventions will  focus  on  improved rangeland management, through participatory learning and promoting sustainable grazing practices. Land management activities will rehabilitate degraded rangelands to increase soil carbon sequestration, promote access to fodder and address the natural barrier to rangeland degradation. In the four countries, soil carbon impacts  were  identified  as  a  strategy  for  carbon  sequestration  activities  such  as  grazing  intensity  management, mowing  frequency,  organic  inputs  and  fertilizers  used  in  fodder  production,  and  improved  agroforestry  systems associated with mixed crop-livestock production systems.
56. Interventions at the dairy value chain level will include the upgrading or equipping MCCs, milk collection points  (MCPs)  and  processing  centres.  This  will  increase  the  supply  of  milk  through  formal  aggregation  and processing channels and create a stable market outlet for dairy farmers. Interventions will also aim to reduce milk losses  and  its  carbon  footprint,  and  reduce  GHG  emissions  through  the  use  of  photovoltaic  (PV)  systems  as  a renewable energy (RE) source for milk collection, cooling and processing.
57. Interventions to improve the enabling environment. The focus will be on (i) a climate-sensitive policy and regulatory environment (institutional framework, dairy value chain governance, farmer awareness campaigns) for the dairy sector; (ii) strengthening proximity public and private services to the dairy sector, in particular expanding access to veterinary services and innovative gender-inclusive advisory services combined with input supply services; (iii) strengthening dairy cooperatives, MCCs and MCPs to encourage farmers to adopt the above- mentioned pathways. Improved  MRV  capabilities  will  provide  more  precise  Tier2-emissions  data  and  improve  the  quality  of  national inventories, as well as policies, planning, and investment decisions. Through regional cooperation, knowledge sharing and joint learning, coordinated strategies will be adopted and efforts of the individual countries will be amplified.

## TARGET AREAS

58. Climate risk . A climate risk mapping (Figure 13) was conducted by overlaying the sum of climatic hazards, exposure, vulnerability and subtracting the adaptive capacity. The maps show that most of the region has a high risk to  climate  hazards  in  the  mid-term  (2040-2069)  and  far  future  (2070-2099).  More  details,  including  the  selected geospatial layers under each risk category, can be found in Annex 2, Chapter 4.

## COUNTRY OWNERSHIP

61. The NDCs, National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in the case of Kenya, and other national CC strategies outline the countries approaches to reduce GHG emissions and adapt to climate change (Table 2a). The proposed Programme is fully aligned with these national strategies, which propose measures such as: climate-resilient crops and livestock production, CC resilient animal breeds, improved husbandry, and windrow composting to reduce enteric fermentation emissions from livestock, and more efficient manure management systems. In addition, the Programme will support the Ministries responsible for the dairy sector to review and formulate national policies, strategies, and regulations that are essential for a sustainable transformation of the dairy sector aligned with the NDCs.
