223. Implementation of Component 3. This component will be implemented as four separate lines of credit in the target countries, under the coordination and supervision of DaIMA regional manag e ment and the National PCUs. The green dairy sector financing operations will be managed by a local Public Development Bank (PDB), which will act as a Wholesale Bank for the GDFF intervention. The Wholesale Banks will at national level manage the financial DaIMA services to the programme target group based on eligibility criteria and other terms and conditions, agreed upon in the programme design and included in the financing agreements between IFAD, the Governments, and the Wholesale Banks. The Wholesale Banks will report quarterly, semi-annually, and annually on the financing progress of GDFF to the PCUs and IFAD in each country based on a pre-defined reporting schedule, indicators, and formats.
224. From the bulk loans provided by the Wholesale Banks, on-lending to the eligible borrowers will be conducted and  managed  by  Retail  Banks,  which  can  be  commercial  banks  or  such  microfinance  banks  and  commercial development banks that are prudentially supervised. Operating with the targeting guidelines and other conditions of the wholesale loan agreements between the Wholesale Banks and Retails Banks, the actual lending methods used for  retail  loans  under  GDFF  financing  will  follow  each  Retail  Bank's  operational  structure  and  network,  rural  and agriculture finance strategy, and their digital tools development approach.
225. The  technical  support  activities  of  Sub-Component  3.2  with  both  the  participating  banks  and  the borrowing SMEs will be managed and implemented by experienced Technical Assistance Service Providers, to be identified and selected through regional competitive bidding organised by DaIMA PMU staff for each of the four DaIMA countries and supervised by IFAD through the standard no-objection process. Practices used and experiences gained from using private TA service providers in the four target countries in IFAD-supported rural finance programmes will be fully utilised when organising the bidding processes. In the planned ARCAFIM operation,

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

mapping of service  providers  with  direct  experience  in  climate  finance  TA  provision  will  provide  guidance  to  this selections process.

## Partnerships

226. During implementation the Programme will involve relevant and specialized government agencies, academia and research institutions. Several partners (NGOs/ non-profit organizations, service providers, private sector (including informal/formal milk  collectors  and  processors),  financial  intermediaries,  ILRI,  FAO,  and  other  international development organizations, etc.) will also participate in the Programme to ensure complementarity with their activities, provide technical assistance and/or the provision of goods, works and services. The PCUs, on behalf of the EEs, will enter into Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs) and/or subsidiary agreements with the implementing partners (also referred as 'project parties') for implementation of the respective activities. Pre-identified Government and private entities are provided below; others will be identified and/or procured on a competitive basis, based on performance, needs and comparative advantage. The list of implementing partners is described in the components, in the Feasibility Study.

227. In Kenya, implementation  will  be  supported  by  KDB,  the  Kenya  Agricultural  and  Livestock  Research Organization (KALRO), the Kenya Animal Genetic Resources Centre (KAGRC), among others.
228. In Uganda, implementation will be supported by DDA, NAGRC&amp;DB, NARO, the Ministry of Local Government (MoLG), universities, private sector and NGOs, amongst others.
229. In Rwanda , implementation will be supported by RAB, RNDP, REMA, RCVD, the Ministry of Environment, the University of Rwanda; Faculty of Veterinary Medicine and Animal Sciences (UR-CAVM), among others.
230. In Tanzania ,  implementation  will  be  supported  by  TDB,  TALIRI,  the  Vice  President  Office,  Regional Administration and Local Government (PO-RALG), the Ministry of Lands, the National Land Use commission (MoL NLUC), the Ministry of Water (MoW), the Ministry of Community Development, Gender and Special Groups, NGOs, universities, the Livestock Training Agency.

## Programme Co-financing

231. The co-financiers (Table 3a) are responsible for reporting of co-financing activities and the amount of their disbursement to the AE in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the GCF policies as well as in AMA, the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between IFAD and GCF and the co-financing agreement signed between the cofinancier and IFAD in its capacity of AE. The amounts of co-financing are detailed in Section C.1.
232. In Kenya, the Programme will be co-financed by INReMP to the amount of US$24.75 million from IFAD. IFAD funds will be used to cofinance Component 1 and 2, as well as Programme management, and M&amp;E.
233. In Rwanda, the Programme will be co-financed by RDDP-2 for US$ 26.61 million provided by IFAD. In addition, Government of Rwanda will provide US$14.75 million cofinancing to Outputs 2.1 and 2.2. IFAD funds will be used to cofinance  Component  1,  2  and  3,  as  well  as  for  Programme  management  and  M&amp;E.  This  will  include:  under Component 1 the activities related to the development of veterinary and extension services, dairy consumption and food  safety  aspects,  and  knowledge  management.  Under  Component  2,  IFAD  funds  will  be  used  to  finance cooperative development and business linkages. Under Component 3, credit lines will be provided to finance Output 3.1.
234. In Tanzania, the Programme will be co-financed by the C-SDTP in an amount of US$ 50.81 million provided by IFAD. These IFAD funds will co-finance Components 1 and 2, as well as for Programme management and M&amp;E. This will include: under Output 1.1, the activities related to policy review, stakeholder participation, as well as dairy consumption and food safety aspects; under Output 1.2 the activities related to the development of veterinary and extension services (except for the development of specific training modules and training of facilitators); under Output 2.1, the development of dairy cooperatives (except for the activity related to renewable energy); under Output 2.2, the vaccination campaign, as well as the investments in improved cowsheds. Programme Management will be financed using IFAD and Government Funds.
235. In Uganda ,  the  Programme  will  be  co-financed  by  the  IFAD-funded  ReLIV  project  for  a  total  amount  of US$  94.23  million.  The  IFAD  proceeds  will  be  used  to  cofinance  Component  1  and  2,  as  well  as  Programme management and M&amp;E. In addition, IFAD will cofinance Output 3.1.

## B.5. Justification for GCF funding request

236. The  Programme will  be  implemented  in  four  countries  in  East  Africa,  three  of  which  are  classified  Least Developed  Countries  (LDCs).  They  have  limited  budgetary  space  to  devote  to  the  public  service  activities  of  a Programme of this nature. These LDCs are the most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change, as their economic development, growth and food security are highly dependent on climate-sensitive sectors, such as livestock and  dairy.  The  wars  in  Ukraine,  Lebanon,  Palestine  and  Syria  have  deeply  affected  these  countries,  through contractions  in  supply  and  demand  both  in  domestic  and  export  markets.  The  public  debt-to-GNI  ratio  has  risen considerably in all four countries. Half of public debt is external. Between 2015 and 2021, external debt relative to gross national income GNI (%) increased from 31.3 to 37.9 percent in Kenya; from 30 to 48.3 percent in Uganda; from

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

- 41.2 to 82.9 percent in Rwanda; and from 39 to 42.2 percent in Tanzania 87 . High debt limits their ability to finance the investments to meet climate change mitigation and adaptation needs in the dairy sector.
237. The implementing of NDCs, NAPs and NAMAs will remain slow without GCF support, due to few alternative options for climate finance. Low-income Countries received only 8 percent of all climate finance, compared to 70 percent invested in  Middle Income Countries (UNEP 2022 Adaptation Gap Report). In addition, according to the Climate Policy Initiative, climate finance in Africa stands at US$ 29.5 billion annually, well below the US$ 277 billion needed each year for countries to meet their NDCs by 2030. In East Africa, annual flows amount to US$ 10 billion against a demand of US$ 82 billion. Climate finance on the continent is more balanced between adaptation and mitigation than in other regions: mitigation activities amounted for 49 percent (US$ 14.6 billion) of climate finance flows,  compared  to  39  percent  (US$  11.4  billion)  for  adaptation  and  12  percent  (USD  3.5  billion)  for  activities generating dual benefits. Adaptation finance would need to be increased six-fold to meet the estimated annual needs of US$ 62 billion. Private sector investments, which are urgently needed to scale adaptation solutions, accounted for only 14 percent (US$ 4.2 billion) of climate finance in Africa. This is lower than in other regions and was invested primarily in mitigation projects (81 per cent) 88 .
238. The public  service  activities  of  the  Programme  -  e.g.,  regulatory  reform,  improvements  in  veterinary  and extension services, knowledge management, capacity development - will be financed by GCF grants. The proposed level of GCF grant support is highly justified due to the significant unmet demand for CCA&amp;M financing among the target groups of the Programme, and the need to develop new technical approaches that can be replicated elsewhere to significantly increase private sector participation in the dairy sector.
239. Furthermore, as noted in the Market Study (Appendix to Feasibility Study), the adoption of low-carbon and climate-resilient solutions and practices by dairy SMEs is constrained by challenges in access to finance paired with technical barriers (Table 3b). Dairy value chain actors are typically cash constrained that makes them reliant on debt capital to invest in climate-smart technologies. The Market Study finds there is a lack of affordable debt within the market with high collateral needs that smaller businesses cannot fulfil. Alongside this, cash-constrained businesses are more likely to prioritise investments that improve productivity over climate mitigation or adaptation. The GDFF is designed to overcome these challenges through its affordable local currency loan funding, providing result-based incentives to businesses to invest in climate-smart technologies in the form of concessional loans.

Table 3b. Summary of barriers to accessing finance for climate-smart practices and technologies and GDFF's response

|                    | Barrier                                                                                                                                                                                                    | GDFF's solution                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
|--------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Financial barriers | Real and perceived high risks of financing investments into dairy value chain actors lead to high costs of borrowing passed onto the borrower through high interest rates and high collateral requirements | • Impact-focused investment loans with a mandate to invest in dairy value chain actors 89 • Competitive interest rates                                                                                                |
|                    | Financial products are standard and not tailored to the needs of the dairy value chain actors                                                                                                              | • GDFF Green dairy loans are developed in collaboration with businesses to ensure debt financing terms enable achievement of long- term greening ambitions, with adequate loan and grace periods                      |
|                    | Enterprises are operating at low margins with cashflow challenges that reduce project invertibility with low ability to repay                                                                              | • Longer tenor loans with repayment holidays to reduce immediate burden of repayment for businesses. Two kinds of products, smaller loans for shorter-term investment and longer- term holistic greening partnerships |
|                    | Internationally financed operators often do not offer a local currency debt option. Especially smaller entities cannot absorb the costs of FX volatility if they are selling in domestic markets.          | • Lending in local currency to all SME borrowers, with the GDFF absorbing the costs of FX hedging.                                                                                                                    |

| Technical barriers   | Agri-businesses require high levels of engagement and support per investment which drives up the transaction costs of lending to them                                                        | • Local presence by local implementing partner financial institutions (see implementation) • Specialist technical assistance support with dairy-specific expertise                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |

240. A central offering that is critical to the delivery of the GDFF's impact is lending in local currency to dairy SMEs. This is a significant challenge faced by enterprises in the sector, and it poses risks to SME viability and sustainability 90 . The capital protection within the GDFF's finance structure enables it to assume the currency risks on behalf of dairy SMEs, with a two-fold justification: (a) without FX hedges, it allows for lower interest rates for the all borrower SMEs, and (b) it helps crowd investors into serving smaller, domestically oriented SMEs that are unable to borrow in foreign currencies.
241. Justification for technical assistance grant : The proposal includes a US$ 10 million technical assistance grant request that is critical for GDFF for supporting businesses to effectively adopt and sustain the adoption of climate smart technologies. This equates to 28 percent of the loan capital allocated to GDFF. With this funding, GDFF will proactively use the TA facility of Sub-Component 3.2 to (a) build the capacities of the Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks to understand and finance green dairy sector projects, and (b) maximise both the impact and returns of the financed  investments  by  enhancing  the  capacity  of  borrowers  to  implement  effective  strategies,  improve  their operational efficiencies, and achieve sustainable growth. In doing so, the TA facility will address non-financial barriers currently preventing greater climate-smart financing in the GDFF target countries.

## B.6. Exit strategy

242. The design of the Programme includes several elements aimed at improving the sustainability of its results and maintaining the paradigm shift after implementation. The exit strategy and sustainability are based on: (i) the full engagement  and  ownership  of  governments,  regional  institutions,  national  dairy  boards,  and  other  stakeholders (private sector, development partners); (ii) policy solutions to create an enabling environment for low-carbon, climateresilient investments in the dairy sector, in line with NDCs and NAMAs; (iii) capacity development of key institutions, public  services,  dairy  processors  and  dairy  farmers  on  practices  (animal  health,  feeding,  breeding,  manure management)  that  effectively  reduce  GHG  emissions  and  build  climate  resilience,  without  compromising  milk productivity; (iv) instruments to mobilise more public and private climate finance for the dairy sector, such as GHG MRV systems; (v) value chain strengthening to enhance service delivery, avoid losses and increase dairy consumption for better nutrition; and (vi) sustainable access to green finance through innovative blended-finance instruments and products under the GDFF for the benefit of the private sector. Thus, in relation to point (vi), it is anticipated that equipment  purchased  with  GCF  proceeds  for  groups  of  smallholder  farmers  (cooperatives),  e.g.,  Milk  Collection Centers and Milk Collection Points, will be sustained through improved access to finance, i.e., the GDFF financing .
243. In terms of engagement and ownership, the Programme was designed with the participation of a wide range of national and regional public institutions, private sector representatives and operators, civil society, and development partners. The validation of the GCF Project Preparation Facility (PPF) at the COP27 also raised awareness of the Programme  globally  and  at  high  level,  and  engagement  of  parties  beyond  the  participating  countries.  The Programme's Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7) provides detailed information on how these stakeholders have been and will continue to be involved during implementation. M&amp;E and knowledge management and learning (KM&amp;L) will  ensure  that  lessons  learned,  documents  prepared,  and  activities  financed  during  the  implementation  of  the Programme are archived (in the Programme monitoring information system), disseminated and replicated for the benefit of similar Programmes, including in Latin America and Asia.

90  https://acumen.org/blog/we-need-new-fx-solutions-in-africa-now/

<!-- image -->

B

<!-- image -->

244. Regarding the enabling environment, by deeply integrating low carbon, climate-resilient dairy interventions into  national  and  regional  policies  and  regulations,  the  legacy  of  Component  1  will  be  an  enhanced  enabling environment and enable the replication and scaling of CCA&amp;M solutions through other projects and programmes- for impact and sustainability at scale. In particular, the policy and regulatory review (Output 1.1) ensures an enabling framework for dairy value chain development and market integration at national and regional level. Improvements in veterinary and extension services (Output 1.2) strengthen the capacity of public services to continue to support similar extension interventions within and outside the targeted areas, beyond the duration of the Programme. In addition, the increase in MRV capacities (Output 1.3) will enable Governments and private operators to effectively monitor GHG emissions, to improve inventories and reporting to the UNFCCC, and to mobilise more public and private finance to address climate change.

245. The Programme will strengthen the capacity of dairy cooperatives to provide dairy farmers with consistent and stable access to outlets for their milk with fair prices and timely payment (Output 2.1). They will also receive support to formulate better business plans and financing applications for climate resilient and low-carbon investments. When dairy farmers have access to a stable market for their milk, they will be less risk-averse and more willing to adopt the technology packages, needed to make the dairy value chain climate-resilient and profitable. Output 2.2 will build on the well-established L-FFS approach to encourage farmers to adopt practices in the long term. L-FFS is one of FAO's flagship innovations, implemented sustainably in more than 90 countries. An estimated 20 million farmers worldwide have participated in FFS since their inception. The four participating countries are not an exception, and the extension methodology is being implemented in many projects sponsored by the IFIs and development partners such as FAO. The Programme will take advantage of the approach and combine it with digital and extension innovations, so that dairy farmers have direct and technical knowledge of the different low carbon practices being promoted.
246. The GDFF will mobilize long-term capital for additional climate financing together with related top end technical support to participating banks for climate-resilient investments in support of the dairy value chain over its 12-year life, supporting the adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices beyond the DaIMA programme period. By using GDFF funding, in the long term, the GDFF will reduce the perceived risk of the banks in investing in dairy SMEs and catalyze additional private capital into the sector. The GDFF will bridge the gap between demand and supply of finance for climate-resilient and low-carbon dairy and create a sustainable and scalable mechanism that can be replicated across other value chains and regions. The GDFF's embedded M&amp;E function (see Annex 2, Chapter 10 for detail on GDFF's M&amp;E) will ensure that learnings are captured throughout delivery, which will facilitate replication and scaling.

## C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer

C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?

- [x] Yes ☒

- [ ] No ☐

C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?

- [x] Yes ☒

- [ ] No ☐

247. Capacity  building  and  dairy  CC  adaptation  and  mitigation  technology  transfer  are  integral  parts  of  the Programme. Firstly, GCF funding is used to insert the CC aspect into the L-FFS training modules and to train trainers and lead farmers in these technologies, in addition to organizing large-scale technology transfer to smallholder dairy farmers. These technologies include all the ones that are needed for a sustainable pathway to increase productivity of  dairy  cows,  while  reducing  emission  intensity  per  kg  of  milk  produced.  Secondly,  the  management  of  dairy cooperatives  will  be  trained  in  the  use  and  management  of  renewable  energy  sources,  in  addition  to  waste management. Thirdly, stakeholders in the dairy value chain will receive TA support and capacity building to prepared business plans for green investments. Fourthly, substantial capacity building support are at the core for making the GDFF financial services to reach objectives and impact and will target both the participating banks and borrowing businesses.

<!-- image -->

## EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1. Impact potential

248. The  Programme  will  guide  smallholder  dairy  producers  and  private  sector  value-chain  operators  on  a transformational path to a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy sector. As part of this transformation, dairy production and value chains will be streamlined for maximum efficiency, financial services will be made available, and the sector's institutional framework will be enhanced. This transformation will result in increased dairy production, dairy productivity, climate resilience of the sector, reduced emissions intensity of milk, and reduced total emissions.
249. To  achieve  mitigation  targets,  the  Programme  will  provide  technical  assistance  for  the  establishment  and improvement  of  low-emission  dairy  processing,  the  optimization  of  efficiency  and  the  reduction  of  losses  in  milk processing  facilities,  as  well  as  the  promotion  of  adaptive  livestock  breeding,  integrated  rangeland  and  pasture management, and animal health systems. Moreover, the Programme aims to facilitate the establishment of the GDDF, aiming to blend GCF proceeds with other investors, including IFAD's. In this way, businesses and dairy farmers receive financial incentives to reduce their carbon emissions, providing them with potential sources of income.
250. The Programme objective of promoting climate resilience and sustainable livestock production will be achieved by supporting local private sector actors, such as dairy cooperatives, aggregators and milk processors, to overcome technological and financial obstacles. By expanding rural employment opportunities and strengthening the resilience of  local  households  to  climate  shocks,  the  Programme  will  help  them  increase  their  productivity  and  expand  their businesses. By building the management capacity of smallholder farmers, improving their creditworthiness, fueling their demand for alternative technologies, and providing them with access to finance through the GDDF, smallholders will be able to significantly reduce their emissions and maximize their efficiency. These intertwined components contribute significantly  to  the  creation  of  a  climate-resilient  and  sustainable  landscape in target countries.  The Programme is expected to make a significant contribution to GCF MRA 4 "Forestry and Land Use," ARA 1 "Most vulnerable people and communities" and ARA 4 "Ecosystems and ecosystem services". It will directly contribute to results of the GCF's Updated Strategic Plan (USP-2), i.e. T1 (by supporting the Country's adaptation and mitigation goals), T4 Food and T5 Ecosystems, through T9 locally-led adaptation approach. The Programme will also support dissemination of climate information and MRV system, directly contributing to T3 CIEWS. The Programme also aligns with T10 (by supporting resilient market chains) and T11 (by developing GDFF).
251. The total number of direct beneficiaries will be about 2.5 million individuals and value chain actors vulnerable to  climate  change  (467,105  in  Kenya,  614,013  in  Rwanda,  757,652  in  Tanzania  and  665,097  in  Uganda).  The Programme will invest in a total of 94 districts/counties (12 in Kenya, 27 in Rwanda, 25 in Tanzania, and 30 in Uganda). A total of about 2.7 million dairy cattle will be reached. Approximately 178,362 ha of rangelands and natural pastures will be brought under improved management. 15.4 million individuals (indirect beneficiaries) will be likely to receive an adaptation  benefit  due  to  strengthened  veterinary  services  (for  better  resilience  of  dairy  farmers  in  the  context  of diseases exacerbated by climate change), enhanced extension and breeding services, and improved access to climate information. See Feasibility Study (sections 5 and 6) for more details.
252. The efforts of the Programme to strengthen adaptation to climate change will result in a sustainable increase in milk production in East Africa. Within the same horizon of 20 years and relative to the baseline, the Programme is expected to significantly increase milk production of the targeted dairy farms 34 percent, of which 31 percent in Kenya, 73  percent  in  Rwanda,  36  percent  in  Tanzania,  and  30  percent  in  Uganda  (see  Annex  22).  This  increase  in  milk production will contribute to the agricultural GDP in the four countries, marking a substantial impact on the economy and climate resilience of the region.
253. As a mitigation benefit, over a 20-year horizon and relative to the BAU, the Programme targets a decrease of milk emission intensities by 29 percent, of which 28 percent in Kenya, 50 percent in Rwanda, 29 percent in Tanzania and 33 percent in Uganda.
254. Over the same period, total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms are expected to decrease (relative to BAU) by about 4.01 percent at the level of the Programme, or (i) by 2.02 percent (-61,967 tCO2-eq) in Kenya, (ii) by 9.56 percent (-56,507 tCO2-eq) in Rwanda, (iii) by 4.69 percent (-65,718 tCO2-eq) in Tanzania and, (iv) by 5.22 percent (-82,372 tCO2-eq) in Uganda. Methane from enteric fermentation is expected to decrease by 4.61 percent overall, while methane from manure management would decrease by 5.93 percent. The Programme is also expected to generate 54,435 tCO2eq/yr savings from energy generation and access. More details about the analysis, which used - among

<!-- image -->

D

<!-- image -->

D

<!-- image -->

others  -  the  Global  Livestock  Environmental  Assessment  Model  interactive  (GLEAM-i),  are  provided  in  Annex  22. Impact metrics can be found in E.3 and Annex 11.

## D.2. Paradigm shift potential

255. The Programme's comprehensive approach to the paradigm shift in the dairy sector will reduce GHG emissions while improving the livelihoods of dairy producers in East Africa. By improving policy, institutional, and service delivery environments, building climate adaptation capacity,  and enabling access to finance and climate-smart solutions for smallholder  dairy  farmers,  the  Programme  aims  to  transform  all  aspects  of  the  dairy  value  chain  (refer  to  section B.2.(a)). This holistic and transformative approach, providing support to the private sector and financial access to the dairy sector has the potential to foster sustainable, low-emission growth in the dairy market to create a lasting impact beyond the initial investment in the Programme. This is due to the scalability of its components, the generation and dissemination of knowledge, the creation of an enabling environment for the widespread adoption of climate-resilient solutions, and the improvement of national regulatory policies in Kenya, Uganda, Tanzania, and Rwanda.
256. Through the integration of two key strategies into its programming, the Programme has a strong potential for scalability. Firstly, it improves access to veterinary services and offers gender-inclusive advisory services, enabling male and female farmers to improve animal health, feed quality, and herd management, thereby increasing the adoption of climate-smart practices. Second, it improves access to finance and alternative technologies, enabling local private sector actors such as dairy cooperatives and milk processors to overcome technological and financial constraints (refer to section B.3. for more details). The dual approach not only improves productivity, but also facilitates the transition to sustainable, low-carbon livestock production, demonstrating both substantial and scalable impact.
257. The  two  main  components  of  the  Programme  are  expected  to  contribute  significantly  to  the  creation  of knowledge and lessons learned . First, the Programme generates detailed data on TIER2 emissions by enhancing national MRV capacities, which are critical to improving national inventories and informing evidence-based policies, planning, and investments. Through the implementation of this robust MRV framework, data gaps will be addressed, and climate scenarios and assessments will be strengthened. In addition,  the Programme will facilitate knowledge transfer through regional and SSTC to replicate transformative practices to build a climate-resilient and low-carbon dairy sector. A key component of this effort is supporting Regional Centres of Excellence for technology validation, as well  as  maintaining  a  regional  database  on  practices,  GHG  emissions,  and  adaptation  indicators.  In  addition,  the creation  of  regional  platforms  for  cooperation  as  well  as  participation  in  global  networks  facilitate  the  exchange  of knowledge and the development of policy initiatives (refer to section B.3. for more details).
258. In addition, the Programme plays an important role in creating a conducive environment for the adoption of climate-resilient solutions , including through the strengthening of market structures. To this end, a comprehensive review of the dairy sector will be conducted in addition to conducting farmer awareness campaigns and improving supply chain traceability. Through these efforts, market formalization and development will be fostered. The provision of tailored financial products and technical assistance will facilitate this process and improve access to financing and affordable credit for on-farm investments. The Programme also facilitates access to the carbon market and supports the development of a certification program for low-carbon production. This not only creates financial incentives for mitigation efforts but also accelerates the transition to low-carbon practices by offering potential revenue streams for dairy farmers and businesses that achieve low-carbon certification (refer to section B.2(a)).
259. In  addition,  the  Programme  will advance  regional,  national,  and  local  legislative  and  regulatory frameworks that encourage investment in low-emission or climate-resilient development (refer to section B.2(a)). In Rwanda, it will focus on the regulatory framework and standard operating procedures for dairy value chain operators, working directly with the Rwanda Food and Drugs Authority as well as the Rwanda Standards Board to address milk quality, standards, safety, and energy efficiency issues. As part of the Programme, the formation of a national regulatory framework for private veterinary practice and the development of a Dairy Value Chain (DVC) regulatory law will be supported. Tanzania and Uganda will benefit from the program by assisting in the review and approval of policies such as the Animal Health Act and the Dairy Act, which will focus on aspects such as raw milk trade regulations and disease control plans (refer to section B.3. for more details). This will allow for the finalization of policies aligned climate change priorities,  which  will  enable  private  sector  investment  policies  and research strategies (e.g.  on  the  development of climate-resilient feed germplasm, the improved genetic resources of dairy breeds, etc.).

## D.3. Sustainable development

260. The Programme will contribute significantly to social and health benefits in the targeted regions. It encompasses a comprehensive approach, such as market formalization, awareness campaigns and product traceability to improve nutrition  and  health  awareness,  with  a  focus  on  the  consumption  of  safe  and  high-quality  dairy  products,  thereby

<!-- image -->

D

<!-- image -->

improving the individual health of rural and urban consumers (sustainable development goal (SDG) 3: Good Health and Well-being). In addition, by strengthening veterinary services, the Programme aims to improve animal health, which will lead to higher milk productivity and reduced losses, which will decrease GHG emissions and increase farmers' incomes. This will incentivize famers to adopt better measures on animal health and welfare (SDG 12: Responsible Consumption and Production). By improving nutrition and food safety through increased consumption of high-quality dairy products, the Programme contributes to ending hunger, achieving food security, and improving nutrition.

261. In  addition,  the  Programme  contributes  to  positive  environmental  impacts,  in  particular  soil  quality  and conservation. A key element is the promotion of biodiversity conservation and healthier ecosystems, through  better management and restoration of rangelands. This approach is essential to maintain biodiversity and combat climate change (SDG 15: Life on Land). The holistic strategy to build climate resilience and reduce emissions along the dairy value chain includes support for rangeland management and restoration. This restores carbon in soils and encourages farmers  and  processors  to  adopt  more  efficient  and  climate-resilient  practices.  The  Programme  also  focuses  on improved pasture management and circular economy principles, with a focus on pathway-specific integrated solutions such as improving manure management for biogas production and nutrient cycling, which  improves soil health. In addition, the Programme advocates for the circular management of agricultural nutrients through livestock manure, improving soil health and on-farm productivity (SDG 12: Responsible Consumption and Production). These strategies collectively reinforce the Programme's commitment to environmental sustainability.
262. The Programme is expected to generate significant positive economic impacts, including job creation, poverty reduction, attracting private finance,  increasing  productivity, and  strengthening income-generating capacities in the dairy sector.  Smallholder  dairy producers and local private sector value chain actors will  have access to essential financial  services,  enabling  them  to  transition  to  low-carbon,  climate-resilient  dairy  production,  and  value  chain development. This approach aims to increase dairy farmers' incomes, motivate them to adopt more efficient practices, while reducing production costs. In addition, the Programme will facilitate the adoption of climate-smart technologies, leading to increased productivity and incomes of smallholder farmers (SDG 9: Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure). Capacity  building  and  adoption  of  integrated  solutions  will  improve  sustainable  and  resilient  production,  thereby improving the incomes and productivity of smallholder dairy farmers (SDG 1: No Poverty).
263. The  Programme  reduces  gender  inequalities.  By  actively  promoting  women's  participation  in  L-FFS  and building  gender  awareness  among  dairy  farming  households  through  the  GALS+,  the  Programme  addresses knowledge, social, and gender barriers by equipping male and female farmers with the tools to understand the impact of climate change and adopt alternative technologies and practices. By focusing on gender-inclusive practices and promoting inclusive participatory planning for rangeland management, the Programme plays a crucial role in mitigating the disproportionate impacts of climate change on women, thereby contributing to gender equality in climate action (SDG 5: Gender Equality).

## D.4. Needs of recipient

264. The  Climate  Impact  Potential  Assessment,  conducted  under  the  Programme  indicates  that  East  African countries such as Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda face significant challenges related to the dairy value chain 92 . The  impacts  of  climate  change  threaten  the  sustainability  of  dairy  farming  as  it  contributes  significantly  to  GHG emissions.  Smallholder  farmers  make  up  most  dairy  producers,  yet  they  face  limitations  due  to  low  productivity, inadequate training, limited supplies, and climate vulnerability.

265. Kenya is the driest among the four countries, with annual rainfall ranging from 400 to 600mm in some areas. It has the highest number of climate-related disasters, with 78 recorded since 1971. The central parts of Kenya have the lowest number of people affected by floods each year, with less than 100 people per administrative boundary affected. However, exposure to drought is more significant in the southwest, especially around cities like Kisumu, Nakuru, and Nairobi.

266. In Uganda, people are most affected by flooding in the northern, eastern, and central regions along the course of  the  White Nile. The annual average number of people affected  varies from 10,000 to 25,000 per administrative boundary, rising to 36,000 in the Northern region. Uganda, along with Kenya and Tanzania, falls under the 'alert THI class' for most areas, indicating increased exposure and vulnerability of animals to climate extremes.

267. Rwanda is  one  of  the  countries  with  the  highest  total  annual  rainfall  (1000  to  1600mm).  Due  to  the  high population density, there are also many people exposed to drought, especially in areas with high population density.

92  Kirungu, C., Alvar-Beltrán, J., &amp; Soldan, R. (2023). DaIMA (GCF-IFAD) - Study 3 - Climate Impact Potential Assessment. Office of Climate Change Biodiversity and Environment (OCB), Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO).

<!-- image -->

D

<!-- image -->

Despite this, strategies based on centuries of exposure to droughts and floods are becoming less effective due to difficulties in implementing coping strategies.

268. In Tanzania, the provinces of Tanga, Morogoro, and Pwani have the highest number of people affected by flooding, with 10,000 to 25,000 people affected per administrative boundary on average per year. Exposure to drought is comparatively lower in places such as the Kizigo Rungwa and Usungu Game Reserves, where population density is lower.
269. Each of these countries suffers from a significant development deficit, which negatively impacts their ability to steer the dairy sector on a path to low-emission and climate-resilient development. In all four countries, public fiscal space  is  limited  (see  section  B.5),  and  governments  rely  heavily  on  official  development  assistance  (ODA)  and concessional financing to fund their dairy sector programs. As a result, vulnerable populations, such as smallholders, women, and youth in the dairy sector are highly exposed to climate risks due to technological, organizational, and policy constraints.
270. The lack of funding for climate-smart agriculture and climate-resilient practices is a significant barrier in the region. Small-scale dairy pastoralists struggle to access credit and financial services, which is crucial for investing in modern equipment, infrastructure, and better farming practices. This limitation exacerbates the vulnerability of the dairy sector to climate-induced changes like unpredictable weather patterns, prolonged droughts and increased exposure to disease, leading to reduced productivity and environmental degradation.
271. In  addition,  poor  communication between climate information  suppliers and agricultural extension services, limited institutional arrangements, insufficient resources to maintain observation networks, gaps in technical skills, and underdeveloped communication networks in East Africa hinder the effective dissemination of climate information and response to climate-related challenges in the region.

## D.5. Country ownership

272. Country ownership of the Programme is ensured through alignment with national policies and ongoing GCF investments, as well as a participatory design process of the Programme.
273. Participating countries' NDCs focus on reducing GHG emissions and increasing resilience to CC. Support to the  dairy  sector  can  benefit  a  variety  of  factors,  including  climate  and  environment,  nutrition,  sustainable  rural livelihoods,  and  social  inclusion.  Therefore,  the  Programme  aligns  with  the  national  strategies  of  the  participating countries by supporting and complementing existing plans, policies and strategies related to agriculture, livestock, and climate change. In Kenya, for example, the Programme aligns with the Agriculture Sector Development Strategy (20192029), National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Implementation Framework (2018-2027),  and  Kenya  National  Strategy  on  Climate  Change  and  Low  Carbon  Development.  In  Uganda,  the Programme aligns with the Livestock Development Strategy and Investment Plan (LDSIP), NAP, and National Climate Change Strategy. In Rwanda, the Programme aligns with Rwanda's NDC, National Climate Change and Environment Policy, Rwanda Livestock Master Plan, National Adaptation Plan for the Agricultural Sector, and National Livestock Policy.
274. In  addition,  the  Programme  aims  to  integrate  climate  change  adaptation  and  mitigation  by  reviewing  and developing national dairy policies, such as the Dairy Industry Strategy and Investment Plan, and the Dairy Industry Act (1958) in Kenya. The Dairy Act to update regulations on raw milk trade; the formulation of disease contingency and control plans; the draft Tanzania Dairy Development Strategy in Tanzania. In Rwanda, the Programme will work closely with  Rwanda Food and Drugs to review the regulatory framework and standard operating procedures (SOP's). In Uganda, the program will engage DDA to review the Dairy Industry Act and the Animal Disease and Health act and the Animal Breeding Act (refer to section B.3.).
275. The Programme complements several planned and ongoing GCF programs in East Africa. At the regional level, the objectives of the Programme complement the GCF regional program, Strengthening Climate Information Systems for  Climate Change Adaptation in the Greater Horn of Africa (GCF Concept note), aimed at strengthening climate resilience in eight countries (IGAD members) through the establishment of early warning and climate dissemination information system. It is also aligned with the ARCAFIM project, which covers the same countries. At a country level, the Programme is aligned with several ongoing projects described in section B.1. (section Synergy with other projects).
276. The Programme was designed following an extensive consultation process with local stakeholders, ensuring that the needs of recipients are considered and ensuring full country ownership of the Programme. The NDAs in each

<!-- image -->

D

<!-- image -->

target countries were directly involved in all stages of Programme ideation, concept, preparation. In each country, National Programme Preparation Teams (comprising a wide range of stakeholder from ministries of livestock and dairy boards) were established in 2023, at the regional workshop in Kigali (September 2023). Through regional (Arusha, February  2023;  Kigali,  September  2023)  and  national  workshops  (July  and  September  2023),  stakeholders engagement meetings were organised to ensure that the preparation of the Funding Proposal (CN process included) involved a wide array of national and regional public institutions, private sector representatives and operators, civil society, research institutions and development partners. The Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7) details the list of stakeholders and their involvement in the Programme design. The Programme benefited from high-level exposure and engagement with Parties beyond the target countries during the validation of the GCF Project Preparation Facility (PPF) at COP27.

## D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness

277. The ex-ante financial and economic viability of the Programme is demonstrated by an economic and financial analysis  (EFA).  Economic  analysis  looks  at  the  profitability  of  investments  for  the  entire  Programme  from  the perspective of society, while financial analysis considers the profitability of these investments from the perspective of Programme  participants.  The  Livestock  Sector  Investment  Policy  Toolkit  (LSIPT)  was  used  to  develop  the  dairy production models in all four countries. All the models estimate the 'with' and 'without' Programme situation over a 20years period. For cattle models, the ECORUM module of the LSIPT for simulating bio-economic performance of herd of tropical domestic ruminants have been used. The parameters of the dairy herd models reflect the activities proposed by the Programme. Five dairy herd models were developed for each of the countries. The national parameters and ponderation  between  models  are  derived  from  the  characteristics  and  national  structure  of  the  dairy  herd  in  the Programme intervention area, as presented in the pre-feasibility studies. The dairy herd models were: (i) small scale intensive system/ zero grazing with cross breed and exotic animals; (ii) small scale intensive system/ zero grazing with exotic animals; (iii) semi-intensive with cross breeds; (iv) semi-intensive with local breeds; and (v) extensive systems with local breed. In addition, financial models were developed for MCPs; MCCs; dairy processing units, all powered by solar panels, as well as service providers such as para-veterinary services. The Programme GHG mitigation impact was evaluated using the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model, Interactive - GLEAM-i (Annex 22).
278. The Programme net cash flows are based on the incremental approach, which results from comparing the With Programme Situation and Without Programme situation 93 . The financial models have been calculated at 17.5 percent discount rate 94 , the average lending rate in Tanzania, Uganda, Rwanda, and Kenya. The selection criterion for the IRR is to accept all Programmes for which the IRR is above the opportunity cost of capital. The financial analysis indicates that the investments proposed by the Programme are profitable for the dairy farmers and cooperatives.
279. At the regional level, the analysis shows that DaIMA represents an economically viable investment for the society. Economic performance indicators for Tanzania, Uganda, Rwanda, and Kenya, individually confirm economic viability. At regional level, the aggregated ENPV of the countries' incremental net benefit streams, discounted at 13.25 percent (social discount rate), is US$ 181.20 million, with an ERR of 25.0 percent for the baseline scenario. Additionally, two additional economic performance scenarios are included, by integrating the economic value of the Programme's GHG emissions balance with a low carbon market price (LCP) assumption and a high carbon market price (HCP) assumption. Under the LCP scenario, the ERR is 27.9 percent and the ENPV is US$ 217.87 million. Under the HCP scenario, the ERR is 30.3 percent and the ENPV is US$ 254.64 million. The sensitivity analysis confirms that DAIMA represents a robust economically worthwhile investment. For more information see Annex 3.

93  IFAD, 2015, Economic and Financial analysis of rural investment projects, basic concepts, and rationale.

94  World Bank data -average 2017 to 2020.

| Scale       | Livestock, especially cattle, are the main source of methane emissions in the East Africa region, representing about 70 percent of total emissions from livestock and the second highest source, representing about 14 percent from agriculture, forestry and other land uses. Without a major shift to low-carbon practices and mitigation pathways across all dairy production systems, trends in absolute and emission intensities will continue to rise by 2050, in the context of a growing demand and growth of the dairy cattle population in the region. Paradigm shift will be accompanied by measures that strengthen low-carbon climate-resilient dairy policies, regulation and institutional capacities. | Implementing a set of integrated and transformative interventions (animal health, feed and feeding strategies, artificial insemination, herd management, methane inhibitors, manure management, renewable energies etc.), combined with strengthened national capacities (veterinary and extension support services), and enabling conditions (including better access to finance, at concessional terms) that are required to make dairy food system more efficient, climate resilient and on a sustainable pathway to decarbonization. Scaling-up of pasture management practices (rotational grazing, agroforestry, management of grazing intensity etc.) through gender-sensitive public and private extension, ensuring availability of high-quality feeds and fodder, breeds to reduce emission intensities, while sustaining production levels throughout the year, and enhancing soil carbon sequestration. | Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner | Ministries responsible for livestock, National Dairy Boards, and dairy cooperatives are spearheading the transformation of the dairy sector to a low carbon and climate resilient industry, in line with their mandates and NDCs. However, these championing institutions need more and better understanding of climate change adaptation and mitigation risks, and investment opportunities to address them. Countries' policies and regulations need to be updated (and aligned with regional/ EAC policies/ regulation) to enable access to agro-inputs and technology for more efficiency in dairy production. | highmedium                            | Ministries responsible for livestock, National Dairy Boards, large-scale processors, and dairy cooperatives develop the required skills and capacities to align their policies and regulations and enhance their capacity to carry out climate vulnerability assessments and run MRV systems and better report their emissions. | A climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment created through the review of the dairy sector's institutional frameworks in a participatory and country- driven manner. Through regional cooperation and knowledge sharing, coordinated strategies will be ensured, individual country efforts will be amplified, and joint learning on CCA and CCM will be fostered. Improving GHG MRV capacities and monitoring climate vulnerabilities. | Multi-countries |

## E.6. Programme activities and deliverables

| Activities                                                                                                                          | Description                                                                                                                                                         | Sub-activities                                                                                                                                   | Deliverables                                                                                              |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Activity 1.1.1: Review and formulate Dairy Sector policies, strategies and regulations for climate change adaptation and mitigation | Ministries responsible for livestock and National Dairy Boards will support an enabling regulatory environment for climate-sensitive dairy value chain development. | 1.1.1.1: Support the review and preparation of national dairy policies, strategies, and regulations for climate-change adaptation and mitigation | 23 national dairy policies, strategies and regulations review and amended. 8 regional policies supported. |

<!-- image -->

E

|                                                                                                                | Dairy Boards and local government are increasingly aware of the challenges and opportunities in climate change and the dairy value chain.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | 1.1.1.2: Support the regional policy dialogue to mainstream climate-change adaptation and mitigation in regional dairy policies, strategies and regulations 1.1.1.3: National dairy value chain governance and awareness creation 1.1.1.4: District/regional/county level dairy value chain governance and planning   | In each country, dairy value chain stakeholders have inserted climate change in the dairy value change governance and planning.                                                                                              |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Activity 1.1.2: Launch awareness campaigns on nutritional benefits of milk and food safety aspects             | Support to milk consumption and nutrition awareness, accompanied by nutrition education and social behavior change, aims at improving nutritionally vulnerable group's consumption of milk, therefore enhancing their individual's health and climate resilience.                                                                                                                                                                                               | 1.1.2.1: Public awareness on the contribution of milk to nutrition and resilience, with focus on climate vulnerable populations 1.1.2.2: Build capacity of dairy inspection to ensure milk quality and safety and reduce waste                                                                                        | 189 awareness campaigns on milk and nutrition 60 inspectors trained on milk quality and safety                                                                                                                               |
| Activity 1.1.3: Improve land tenure security for sustainable dairy farming                                     | The Programme will adopt a fit-for-purpose approach to identify and mitigate tenure risks, which are key barriers to sustainability, and adopt climate resilient and low carbon practices. In Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda, the main risk for intensive/zero-grazing is tenure insecurity, i.e. occupancy rights or land ownership. In Rwanda, the focus will be on optimizing land production and productivity through land consolidation and land use planning. | 1.1.3.1: Mapping of local tenure risks and preparation of training materials 1.1.3.2: Developing capacity on land tenure at decentralized level.                                                                                                                                                                      | Tenure governance capacity assessments carried out in each country Capacity development on tenure carried out at local level (district/ county)                                                                              |
| Activity 1.2.1: Strengthen veterinary services for better animal health, higher milk yields and reduced losses | Proximity veterinary and para veterinary capacity will be enhanced to be able to reach out to dairy farmers, using the One Health Approach. Reference veterinary laboratories equipped with modern diagnostic technologies in regional centers.                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | 1.2.1.1: Support installation of both private and public veterinarians and veterinary paraprofessionals 1.2.1.2: Strengthening diagnostic capacity of veterinary laboratories and capacity to test quality of animal feed                                                                                             | 1,800 veterinarians, para veterinarians and LEWs trained and equipped. Reference laboratories in Kenya (7), Rwanda (4), Tanzania (10) and Uganda (10), equipped for diagnostic analysis of animal diseases and feed testing. |
| Activity 1.2.2: Strengthen extension services for a low-carbon and climate resilient dairy sector              | Improving delivery of public and private extension services at farm and cooperative level, by district or county livestock officers and L-FFS who coordinate the community- based extension system                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | 1.2.2.1 Preparation of training modules for extension in climate mitigation and adaptation measures. 1.2.2.2: Training of master trainers (ToTs) and the L-FFS facilitators in climate-change related topics                                                                                                          | Training modules prepared on climate- smart milk production and handling, including fodder production, feeding, reproductive and health management, manure management, and animal welfare.                                   |

The regional platform will function as the knowledge management mechanism for the Programme. | 1.4.2.1: Regional platform for knowledge sharing and policy dialogue to enhance low- emission, climate resilient dairy development. 1.4.2.2: Participation in regional and global climate policy mechanisms 1.4.2.3: South-South and Triangular Cooperation                                                                                        | Participation in at least 10 regional and global climate policy events. 23 south-south cooperation events.                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Activity 2.1.1: Strengthen dairy groups and cooperatives on organizational management and service delivery                                                                          | This activity aims to strengthen cooperative, organizational, and technical capacities of dairy cooperatives, and raise their climate- change and gender awareness of their management team. Support will be provided to enhance their network of MCCs and MCPs.                                                                    | 2.1.1.1: Strengthen the governance and management structure of dairy cooperatives. 2.1.1.2: Strengthen capacity to reduce milk losses and ensure milk quality and safety. 2.1.1.3: Expand the outreach and capacity of cooperatives, MCCs and MCPs 2.1.1.4: Create awareness on gender transformative approaches among households of dairy farmers | 650 dairy cooperatives are strengthened and ensure that dairy farmers have a stable outlet to less their milk. 650 dairy cooperatives have a better capacity to manage milk in a safer way and upgraded equipment for milk collection. Management of 650 dairy cooperatives is more gender sensitive. |
| Activity 2.1.2: Build and enhance productive alliances along the dairy value chain.                                                                                                 | This activity supports productive alliances between farmer cooperatives and enterprises with farmer-allied engagement models, particularly processors, and scaling-up dairy clusters, that enable farmers to access affordable, high-quality inputs and services.                                                                   | 2.1.2.1: Strengthen cooperative capacity in business and financial management. 2.1.2.2: Build linkages along the dairy value chain. 2.1.2.3: Promote digitalization and traceability of dairy operations.                                                                                                                                          | Management of 650 dairy cooperatives received business training. 300 business links established or strengthened. 15 cooperatives/MCCs (pilot) are testing the digitalization and traceability system to ensure quality of milk.                                                                       |
| Activity 2.1.3: Promote renewable energy and waste management technologies                                                                                                          | The Programme will focus on resource use efficiency and the substitution of fossil fuels and firewood by green energy sources. Solar is the key mitigation solution and most applicable to relatively larger processors in the short term given high upfront costs.                                                                 | 2.1.3.1: Assess the need and viability of renewable energy and waste management solutions. 2.1.3.2: Support the acquisition of renewable energy and waste management solutions. 2.1.3.3: Organize capacity building in energy efficiency and waste management                                                                                      | 190 cooperatives have installed solar equipment. 130 dairy cooperatives have installed wastewater management systems. Management of 650 cooperatives received training in energy efficiency and waste management                                                                                      |

## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

280. Each EE will implement and coordinate the overall M&amp;E system building on the existing M&amp;E systems of the related IFAD-financed projects, allocate resources for M&amp;E tasks and deliverables, aggregate M&amp;E data and present them in overarching reports and knowledge documents, verify and ensure quality and validity of the M&amp;E data and information, and organize learning events to facilitate sector  wide learning, ensure that funds are effectively managed to deliver expected Outputs and achieve the anticipated Outcomes; ensure the quality of Programme monitoring,  Programme closure and final evaluation timelines are complied with. The RSC, with secretarial functions provided by the national EEs on a rotational basis, will be responsible for providing harmonized reporting and methodological guidance on overall M&amp;E implementation, as well as consolidation of timely yet high-quality reports to the GCF. M&amp;E guidance beyond the PIM will be provided by ILRI (e.g. developing templates, methodology, explanatory notes), overseen by the regional steering committee. The data capture and reporting aspects  will  be  handled  by  the  countries,  through  their  PMUs.  IFAD  will  organize  and  lead  annual  supervision  missions,  including  mid-term  and  end  term evaluations.
281. The overall M&amp;E for the GDFF will be built around its ToC and Logframe. The system will continuously be built through ongoing adaptive management so that the Programme can regularly assess it performance in supporting planning and reporting, learn and adapt to improve its climate adaptation outcomes. While IFAD will have the overall coordination tole to all M&amp;E reporting to the GCF for the GDFF, the Wholesale Banks in each country will have a prominent role in data collection and analysis. Details on the M&amp;E are proposed in Annex 11.
282. Specifically,  for  the  GDFF  under  Component  3,  a  senior  GDF  Coordinator  will  be  recruited  at  IFAD  Kenya  Regional  Office  to  support  the  overall coordination,  M&amp;E,  reporting  and  Knowledge  Management  of  the  GDFF,  for  the  entire  project  period.  In  addition,  during  the  Programme  start-up  phase, experienced Financial and M&amp;E Specialists will be recruited to assist the DaIMA PMU to develop an appropriate reporting, monitoring and evaluation system for

<!-- image -->

E

GDFF operations, based on a pre-defined reporting schedule, indicators, and formats, to be agreed-upon between IFAD, DaIMA PMU and the Wholesale Banks. The GDFF reporting and monitoring would be integrated within the reporting, monitoring, and evaluation systems to be developed for the whole regional DaIMA operation. These issues are described in detail in Annex 11 of the Funding Proposal and in the Feasibility Study on Component 3 (Annex 2).

283. Annual Work Plan and Budgets (AWPBs) . The M&amp;E Officer in each of the national PMUs will prepare the national Programme's consolidated AWPBs, specifying the following: (i) key activities in the period; (ii) specific unit targets and baseline values; (iii) expected outputs the activity is contributing to; (iv) timeline to complete each activity; (v) tabular summary information on the AWPBs of the overall project to easily assess progress; and (vi) responsible reporting parties of each activity and data. The Programme staff and key stakeholders will validate an 18-month AWPB drafted at design during the start-up phase of implementation. As the Programme implements, the preparation of annual AWPBs will be a participatory process that will be based on the activities needed and proposed by the beneficiaries, districts, and implementing partners to achieve the desired changes stated in the Theory of Change and indicator targets. The EEs will be the responsible institution for all planning processes and for inclusion of key stakeholders to guarantee transparency, accountability while ensuring sufficiency of M&amp;E information for informed and timely decision-making. The EEs will receive, review, harmonize and consolidate AWPBs from the lower planning levels in accordance with each country administrative or governing structures while ensuring consensus building among key stakeholders in each planning level. The consolidated AWPB will be reviewed by the National Programme Steering Committee (NPSC) in each country for endorsement before submission to the Regional Steering Committee (RCS) as the next endorsement level before submission to IFAD. Submission to IFAD will happen at least 60 days before commencement of the next National level Program planning year/cycle. AWPB review meetings will be conducted during each planning cycle in all levels with the involvement of all key stakeholders. This will allow common understanding and consensus building among stakeholders on national annual priorities, expected targets and budgetary adequacy for effective and sustainable implementation of the Program objectives
284. Reporting System .  Each  participating  country will  facilitate  preparation  of  each  quarterly,  six-monthly  and  an  annual  progress highlighting  achieved successes, constraints and, lessons learnt, recommendations and action plans to address the identified constraints in the subsequent plans and implementation periods. Each implementing country' national reports to be submitted to GCF will be consolidated at RSC level, with technical support provided by ILRI and/or other service providers.
285. Monitoring and Evaluation . Responsibilities for monitoring and evaluation lie with the national EEs and Component 3 implementation team. Each country has its own Logical Framework that includes GCF core indicators (outcomes and outputs), in line with the GCF requirements. Respective executing lead agencies from each country as the key implementer of the Programme, will play a key role in data collection and analysis and reporting. They will collect and report on baseline data, portfolio information, as well as output and select outcome indicators; participate in Annual Supervision Missions, Midterm and Final Evaluations; as well learning and adaptive management events organized internally, across projects or those supported by IFAD/GCF directly. The RSC, with technical support required, will be responsible for providing guidance on reporting needs requiring regional/Programme level consolidation, as well as progress realized on regional aspects of the Programme, and that regional added value is achieved and sustained beyond the Program life. Specifically, RSC will monitor progress with respect to Output 1.1, Output 1.3 and Output 1.4. At national level, the PMUs will be responsible for monitoring and evaluation, knowledge management of all activities related to the Programme. This should in accordance with the Logical Framework provided that includes GCF core indicators (outcomes and outputs), in line with the GCF requirements. The M&amp;E processes will further be refined during the inception phase of the GDFF with necessary inputs from the stakeholders. When relevant, indicators will be disaggregated by (i) type of recipient, (ii) gender, (iii) age (youth vs non-youth), (iv) country at least and may further be required at project levels and according to the information needs of each implementing country.
286. Supervision and implementation support missions will be organised jointly by IFAD, GCF and the Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda, to assess and review progress and assist implementing partners and the PMUs improve on Programme implementation based on the mission's support

<!-- image -->

E

and recommendation. The supervision missions will assess the overall physical and financial  performance, identify  implementation  challenges  and  propose possible mitigation measures to help address them. This should be in compliance with all the fiduciary aspects of the financing agreement. The supervision missions will be conducted at least twice a year or more frequently as conditions may warrant, and according to IFAD's GCF own policies and procedures including the provision for implementation support. The outputs of the supervision missions will include: i) Aide Memoire and ii) supervision mission reports with findings and recommendations, focusing on the achievements and constraints for discussion with the lead Ministry. To facilitate the work of the mission team, lead agencies will prepare implementation progress reports ahead of the mission team describing key achievements, lessons learnt, best practices to scale up implementation and constraints encountered during the implementation. The support missions will help identify and address emerging issues, build technical capacities, and to the extent possible, build on experiences and best practices from past and ongoing IFAD - GCF funded projects. The composition of the support mission team will include, but not limited to: The financial Expert, M&amp;E expert, targeting specialist etc. It may also add value and if the budget allows to include a sections of PMU teams on some mission on rotational basis for different projects. This will enhance learning, complement adaptive management and serve as best practices aimed at strengthening knowledge management within the project and government set-up.

287. Baseline Survey . The Programme will conduct a baseline survey at the beginning of implementation in the targeted areas. The survey will be designed to capture the following baseline data separately: (i) sampled existing beneficiaries; and (ii) sampled new beneficiaries. The specific GCF core indicators will be included in the baseline survey that will be updated based on the milestones by the Outcome Survey. The study will be contracted to an external service provider (SP) with the involvement of PMUs with ILRI and IFAD providing overall guidance. This involvement is aimed at ensuring harmony in indicator definition and targets refinement. This will be done in a way that ensure data collection appropriates and fir for purpose in the context of Program implementation.
288. Mid-term Review and Final Evaluation . Interim and Final Independent Evaluations will be planned and managed by IFAD according to the requirements of the GCF's evaluation criteria and as per the GCF Evaluation Policy. In advance to the end term Evaluation Missions, GCF will conduct a series of more comprehensive household/business surveys including both quantitative and qualitative data collection and analysis. IFAD will lead evaluation process following IFAD's procurement guidelines, supported by IFAD's Research and Impact Assessment (RIA) unit. The evaluations will be supported by an Evaluation Reference Group, made up of IFAD, GCF and the other co-financiers. The MTR and final evaluation will embed an assessment of the Programme's contributions to the paradigm shift and enabling environment.
289. Knowledge Management, Communication and Learning . The Programme will elaborate and implement a KM and Communication Strategy that will: (i) provide Project beneficiaries with the necessary material to sustain the technical knowledge acquired with the support of the Project (production of training materials and communication platforms for sensitization) and continuously assess their adoption; (ii) generate shared knowledge acquired from the experience of the past and current Project in various fields and based on the information collected as part of the monitoring of results or from specific thematic studies; (iii) share knowledge generated with the Technical Departments of the Ministry, IFAD, other donors, and implementing partners using various dissemination strategies and channel for learning and visibility. In achieving the Policy Component, the KM and Communication Strategy will strongly aim to develop and create channels of policy influence and dialogue through the development of policy briefs/strategies to be disseminated audience of high-level policy stakeholders in the Dairy Sector. The KM and Communication Strategy should also establish a Community of Practice (CoP) to support the development and coordination of regional implementation creating harmonized y programme requirements in all levels
