## RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

| Selected Risk Factor 1 Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability   | Selected Risk Factor 1 Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability   | Selected Risk Factor 1 Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability   |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                                              | Probability                                                                           | Impact                                                                                |
| Technical and operational                                                             | High                                                                                  | Medium                                                                                |

## Description High

290. Limited skills exist in the dairy value chain in the four countries particularly in areas of social inclusion and gender,  dairy  technicians,  extension  services,  M&amp;E  and  functional  dairy  L-FFS  specialists  in  the  implementing ministries, national dairy boards, and organisations to guarantee effective Programme implementation. In Tanzania and Uganda, the local government has limited financial, procurement and human resources to assume their mandate of  execution  particularly  on  community  service,  extension,  nutrition,  private  sector  partnerships  and  infrastructure development).  Further,  limited  M&amp;E  skills  within  PMU  and  government  staff  in  general  is  bound  to  affect  the implementation. The four countries are also at different stages of M&amp;E system set up and maturity. Given the scope of DaIMA project, data collection, harmonization and reporting at the regional level may be a challenge.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

291. In each of the four countries, a Programme management unit will be established by the IFAD project that will mobilise cofinancing, and staff recruitment will follow a competitive process to ensure quality expertise is in place. The implementation of the Programme will be structured around performance-based contracts which will also be indicated in the Financing Agreement. Service providers will be contracted through competitive government procedures and based  on  renewable  performance-based  service  contracts  to  provide  advisory  services.    As  part  of  the  support delivered, implementing partners and service providers will ensure that adequate capacity is built among recipients of their services at various levels including local governments to guarantee their exit strategy and overall sustainability. Continuous capacity building will be conducted to both government and PMU staff Exchange visits and joint reviews will  also  be conducted to enhance learning in addition to routine M&amp;E training to build the capacity of PMUs and government.

| Selected Risk Factor 2 Financial Management and Fiduciary Risks   | Selected Risk Factor 2 Financial Management and Fiduciary Risks   | Selected Risk Factor 2 Financial Management and Fiduciary Risks   |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                          | Probability                                                       | Impact                                                            |
| Governance                                                        | High                                                              | Medium                                                            |

## Description

292. The Programme involves multiple line ministries and the Project Management Units (PMU), districts /county governments, all of which hold fiduciary responsibilities. Although the PCMU will consist of qualified personnel with expertise in technical and financial management, there may be a lack of familiarity among staff with the procedures of IFAD and the GCF. The districts /county governments will receive funding from the Ministry of Finance and report to IFAD through the PMU. There is a risk of delays in flow of fund and the consolidation of project financial reports at the PMU. Furthermore, the project involves multiple financiers, including IFAD, the GCF, government contributions, and beneficiary contributions. This creates a risk of commingling funds in budgeting and expenditure allocations among these financiers. Additionally, there is a risk of inadequate audit coverage for the project due to the involvement of various districts/ counties governments located across a wide geographic area. Additionally, there is a possibility that some high-risk expenditure categories may not be adequately addressed during the audits.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

293. Formal  agreements  will  be  established  between  the  line  ministries,  the  Ministry  of  Finance,  and  the participating districts/county governments. These MoUs will clearly outline the requirements for financial reporting with penalties for Non-Compliance. The Financial Management Division (FMD) will conduct capacity-building training for finance staff recruited through a competitive process. This training will focus on: a) Familiarization with IFAD and GCF procedures; b) Understanding financial reporting requirements and timelines; c) Proper categorization of expenditures across different project components; and d) Overall financial management practices related to the project. The risks associated  with  fund  commingling  and  to  facilitate  accurate  accounting  of  any  advances  provided,  the  following measures will be implemented: a) Funds will be maintained in dedicated project accounts at the Central Bank of each country. This will help ensure that project funds are kept separate from other government funds; b) There will be monthly  bank  account  reconciliations  and  financial  reports  generated  for  these  dedicated  accounts  to  ensure transparency and accountability; c) Each district/county receiving project funds will be required to establish sub-project

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

accounts specifically for segregating the funds received. This will help prevent the mixing of project funds with other county funds; d) Counties will submit monthly financial reports to the PMU to monitor the operations of their subaccounts and facilitate consolidation of financial data; e) All district /counties will sign MoUs that clearly outline the requirements  for  maintaining  separate  bank  accounts  and  the  financial  reporting  obligations  associated  with  the Programme.

294. To mitigate the risks associated with  external  audits,  the  following  measures  will  be  implemented:  a)  the auditor will develop a detailed work plan to ensure sufficient coverage of all counties receiving project funds. This plan will  specifically address major risk areas to ensure that all critical aspects of the project are audited, b) the IFAD Finance Officer will provide the external auditors with the IFAD audit terms of reference in advance to ensure that all key elements are included in the audit TOR and c) the project finance staff will be informed of the audit requirements as outlined in the IFAD Financial Management and Financial Control (FMFCL) Handbook.

| Selected Risk Factor 3 Sector Strategies and Policies   | Selected Risk Factor 3 Sector Strategies and Policies   | Selected Risk Factor 3 Sector Strategies and Policies   |
|---------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                                | Probability                                             | Impact                                                  |
| Governance                                              | High                                                    | Medium                                                  |

## Description High

295. Each of  the  four  countries  recently  developed  a  Livestock  Sector  Transformation  Plan,  including  a  Dairy Development Plan. Sector policies including the recently developed national dairy strategy documents in the four countries are formulated in an inclusive manner, involving all organised stakeholders. The policies are aligned with their national strategies, and priorities. The main gap is the low representation of smallholder dairy farmers in these policy fora, due to the lack of professional organisation of this category of actors. Policy dialogue is thus dominated by private sector actors, who are well organised, national dairy boards, larger progressive farmers, NGOs, and public institutions. The other risk is related to the low capacities of public institutions to enforce sector regulations, particularly those related to milk hygiene. This has a significant impact on the value chain as it provides a comparative advantage to the informal raw milk sector.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

296. During the design process, the four Governments committed to adhere to IFAD's environmental safeguards and targeting policy, to maximise positive social and environmental impacts, and ensure that DaIMA is in line with IFAD's core principles. The risk mitigation strategies include: a) Participation of the four countries dairy boards in the entire design process, b) The Programme will support the organisation of smallholder farmers at regional, national, and county/district levels, and their participation in policy dialogue fora, to make the policy dialogue more inclusive and c) The Programme will also support the review and update of sector regulations, but also their enforcement. This will entail support to the national Dairy Boards, District milk inspectors, and strengthening of laboratory facilities for milk control.

| Selected Risk Factor 4 Project Scope and Approach   | Selected Risk Factor 4 Project Scope and Approach   | Selected Risk Factor 4 Project Scope and Approach   |
|-----------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|
| Category                                            | Probability                                         | Impact                                              |
| Technical and operational                           | Low                                                 | Medium                                              |
| Description                                         | Description                                         | Description                                         |

297. The Programme strategic approach responds to the sector priority needs, characterised by a lack of milk supply, dominance of the informal raw milk value chain and inadequate access to services and finance. However, the situation of the dairy value chain is volatile and may evolve during implementation: the supply/demand balance could be reversed, due to changing consumption patterns or emergence of new market segments, and to rapidly increasing production. This would require a slight adaptation of the programme activities, and a re-balancing of activities in favour of either the marketing aspects, or production. The DaIMA design covers a large range of domains due to the overall low performance of the dairy value chain, affected by challenges at all levels (production, services, access to finance, market access, low consumption, policy gaps). The Programme must address all these challenges at the same time to avoid leaving bottlenecks that would impede the overall development of the sector. This leads to a moderate level of complexity of the programme design, which is however common in similar value chain projects.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

298. In case the situation of the value chain evolves during implementation, some adjustments may be needed on project activities, including on budget allocation, without modifying the project structure, theory of change, objectives, and overall strategy. This may include for instance increased support for the processing and marketing levels, and reduced emphasis on production and productivity. These adjustments could be made at the MTR stage. Some of the mitigation measures will include: a) Implementation of specific sets of activities will be delegated to implementing partners that have experience and comparative advantage in these domains, b) Design should be flexible and not

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

overly  prescriptive  to  allow  adaptations  in  course  of  implementation,  based  on  lessons  learned,  c)  Programme governance should be solid, with mechanisms for effective national and regional coordination of implementing partners and service providers (reporting system, M&amp;E, regular technical meetings, and regional steering committee meetings) and d) Governance organs such as the National and Regional Steering Committees include stakeholders that have very good knowledge and sound analysis of the sector (e.g. ILRI).

| Selected Risk Factor 5 Regional Context   | Selected Risk Factor 5 Regional Context   | Selected Risk Factor 5 Regional Context   |
|-------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| Category                                  | Probability                               | Impact                                    |
| Governance                                | Medium                                    | Medium                                    |
| Description                               | Description                               | Description                               |

299. Despite recent protest by the youth, the four countries are characterised by a good socio-political stability, and a rather low occurrence of inter-ethnic tensions or clashes. In the four countries, there is growing concern because young people have become disenchanted with agriculture and often do not have access to (high quality) input and capital. Youth involvement in small-scale / zero-grazing dairy is critical to address the youth bulge. All IFAD projects in the four countries have been designed to reduce rural poverty and consequently inequality and social tensions. There are also risks linked to political commitment, governance and the macroeconomic situation in the 4 countries.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

300. As the largest employer in the country, agriculture will remain an entry point for job creation, inclusive growth, and poverty reduction. Small-scale / zero grazing dairy has a lot of potential to reduce poverty, increase food security, improve nutrition, and strengthen resilience. DaIMA will strengthen the livelihoods of the most disadvantaged rural categories  including  smallholder  dairy  farmers,  poor  households  without  cows,  unemployed  youth,  women,  and women-headed households. DaIMA will leverage four national projects that are co-financed by IFAD and other funding mechanisms and will invest in rural areas to increase smallholder productivity. Regarding political commitment, the relevant  counterpart  government  officials  have  been  actively  engaged  throughout  the  design  of  the  regional Programme. Three (3) regional preparation workshops were organised, in addition to a series of national events. The IFAD team will continue to work closely with the Government of the four countries during the next phases of the Programme approval and start-up, to ensure Government ownership and alignment with IFAD's and Government's policies  is  ensured.  Regarding  governance,  IFAD's  COSOP  in  the  four  countries  places  the  country-level  policy engagement  (CLPE)  at  the  core  of  its  strategy.  Key  areas  of  policy  engagement  are  focusing  on  increasing investments, improving policy coherence and coordination and improving the business environment for priority value chains. One of the focus areas of DaIMA will be the formulation and implementation, review and update of national policies, strategies and legislations with respect to the dairy sector. It is envisaged to support the Governments to strengthen regulations on milk trade, as well as their enforcement. DaIMA will also contribute to significantly improve the governance of the dairy sector. Finally regarding macroeconomic risks, The Governments of the four countries committed to macroeconomic policies, aimed at not increasing public debt, containing  inflation within their target range, and preserving external stability. The authorities have established a track record of sound macroeconomic management, but further reforms to revenue policy and administration, public expenditures, and debt management will be necessary to create adequate space to increase priority social spending and productive investment without jeopardising fiscal sustainability.

| Selected Risk Factor 6   | Limited adoption/reversal to BAU practices by beneficiaries   | Limited adoption/reversal to BAU practices by beneficiaries   |
|--------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                 | Probability                                                   | Impact                                                        |
| Other                    | Medium                                                        | Medium                                                        |

## Description

301. Small dairy farmers show a low adoption rates of improved practices or revert to business-as-usual practices

## Mitigation Measure(s)

302. The use of participative approaches for capacity development such as farmers field schools, demo farms and learning routes increases the probability of adoption of improved practices. In addition, activities will rely on the support of dairy groups and cooperatives which will be supported by sector specific organisations such as (i) SDLD and KDB in Kenya, (ii) the Rwanda National Dairy Platform ((RNDP)) in Rwanda; (iii) the Dairy Development Authority  (DDA), in Uganda; (iv) the Tanzania Dairy Board (TDB) and the Tanzania Cooperative Development Commission (TCDC). Finally, improved practices will not only result in improved resilience and lower emissions but also in better economic return for farmers, which creates economic incentives.

| Selected Risk Factor 7   | Insufficient access to markets to incentivise the paradigm shift   | Insufficient access to markets to incentivise the paradigm shift   |
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                 | Probability                                                        | Impact                                                             |

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

| Other   | Low         | Medium   |
|---------|-------------|----------|
|         | Description |          |

303. Small dairy farmers are interested in improved practices but they do not have sufficient access to market to find the economic return they need.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

304. The Programme has a strong focus on developing access to market for small holders under Output 2.1: Smallholders' and cooperative capacity development strengthened. It will expand the outreach and capacity of dairy cooperatives,  MCCs  and  MCPs.  It  will  also  build  productive  alliances  and  linkages  along  the  dairy  value  chain, including with offtake agreements between cooperatives and private off-takers (Productive Alliance model), as well as public procurement (e.g. school or hospital procurement) for better milk prices guaranteed by contract.
305. Establishing four relatively small-sized credit lines in a niche sub-sector like dairy poses several challenges. Relatively low funding base can constrain the interest of banks to participate in the scheme. Lack of understanding of the  bankable options in  green dairy lending can further reduce bank interest.  Similarly,  lack  of knowledge of the advantages  of  green  dairy  investments  among  the  dairy  businesses  can  reduce  loan  demand.  Finally,  lack  of management skills in medium and small businesses can result in low recovery rates particularly in more technically aspects of green dairy investments.

| Selected Risk Factor 8   | Risk of low demand for and repayment of GDFF Funds   | Risk of low demand for and repayment of GDFF Funds   |
|--------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| Category                 | Probability                                          | Impact                                               |
| Other                    | Medium                                               | Medium                                               |
| Description              | Description                                          | Description                                          |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

306. To address these risks, GDFF includes several design features and instruments. Substantial technical support provided by GDFF aims to substantially increase and improve the understanding of both the  banks and borrowing SMEs on the available investment options in greening of the dairy operations. Favourable loan terms, particularly the long grace and repayment periods, will support successful cash flow management in the borrowing companies and support timely loan repayment. At the same time participation in GDFF lending operations will build positive credit histories  for  small  and  medium  dairy  companies,  providing  a  soft  start  for  permanent  client  relationships  with commercial banks to enable larger future investments for participating dairy businesses.

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment

307. The  Programme  has  been  reviewed  in  line  with  IFAD's  Social,  Environmental  and  Climate  Assessment Procedures (SECAP) and classified as Moderate for the categorization of Environmental &amp; Social risks as well as Climate Risk. Based on the results of the screening, the activities proposed under the Programme do not pose a high risk of harm to people or the environment and will be located away from environmentally or socially sensitive areas. In addition, the risks associated with the programme are predictable and expected to be temporary or reversible and have a low likelihood of causing serious adverse effects to human health or the environment.
308. Recognizing that moderate adverse impacts may impinge on the environment and human health during various phases of the Programme, an Environmental, Social and Climate Management Plan (ESCMP) has been prepared as part of the ESMF. The ESMP entails potential adverse environmental and social impacts and risks associated with proposed activities, respective mitigation measures, responsible parties, and cost estimates for implementation of each mitigation measures. To keep track of the implementation of the ESMF, there will be two types of monitoring to be conducted during implementation of the programme i.e. Performance and results monitoring. The Environment and Social Safeguard Specialist will conduct at least one field visit at an interval of six months to verify reports submitted from responsible parties indicated in the ESMP. Additional site visits and/or changes in site visit frequency may be required based on the risk level of the sub-project. As regards Reviews, there will be annual reviews of the ESMF and all associated environmental and social instruments. This activity will be undertaken in a participatory manner and shall involve programme stakeholders at various levels from the sub-project level to the national level in the four countries. Annual  reviews  are  aimed  to  ensure  that  implementation  of  interventions  is  undertaken  in  compliance  with  IFAD SECAP and GCF Environmental and Social Standards.

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

309. During proposal development phase, consultation of potential stakeholders in selected sites was done to solicit their views and concerns regarding the proposed interventions. For a continuous engagement during implementation of the program, a stakeholders engagement strategy has been prepared as part of the ESMF. The strategy provides a framework for achieving effective stakeholder involvement and promoting greater awareness and understanding of issues so that the programme is carried out effectively. As part of the stakeholders' engagement and for addressing complaints that may arise  during programme implementation, a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) has been established.  The  GRM  will  address  queries  and  offer  an  avenue  for  clarifications  about  the  program,  respond  to problems, complaints, and grievances. The GRM also provides an opportunity for appeal if any aggrieved person is not satisfied with a decision or action in response to their issue.  To ensure that the Grievance Redress Mechanism is well known and accessible to all program stakeholders, various communication tools will be adopted to disseminate the information about the available opportunity, and how to register complaints and seek redress. The program will also  use  the  opportunities  from  the  existing  communication  system  and  program  awareness-raising  events  to disseminate the information on GRM.
310. As regards Information Disclosure, the ESMF will be disclosed 30 days (Category B/I-2 projects) before the GCF or IFAD Board meeting date, whichever is earlier. It will be posted on the IFAD website, on the GCF website together with the funding proposal and at locations convenient to affected peoples, i.e. at all levels at the relevant institutions. The Programme Coordination Teams at the country level will make and distribute copies of the ESMF written  in  English  and  working  language  of  the  country  in  compliance  with  the  IFAD's  Public  Consultation  and Disclosure Policy. Thereafter various communication tools will be utilized for the engagement process, such as Project notices published in local newspapers, radio advertisements, direct mailings to communities, presentations with or without focus group sessions, targeted e-mails, one-on-one meetings, social media, seminars, workshops, e-mails, and phone conversations with stakeholders.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

311. The gender assessment and action plan of the Programme provides an overview of the gender inequalities that  hinder  women,  youth,  and  other  vulnerable  groups  participation  in  and  benefitting  from  dairy  production  and climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions. Annex 8 sets out the main parameters of the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) assessment and analysis. Part 1 provides a brief GESI analysis of smallholder dairy farmers and MSMEs (dairy cooperatives and processors) i.e., the experiences of women, youth, people with disabilities and other vulnerable in dairy production and the barriers they face Part 2 provides an overview of the GESI action plan setting out actions and targets to achieve the project objectives.
312. The methodology for the gender assessment and action plan was informed by in-country missions in Tanzania, Uganda, Kenya, and Rwanda. The field missions included consultations with key stakeholders and in-depth interviews with key stakeholders in the dairy sector. This was followed by a desk literature review and synthesis, including practical and field examples to inform the needed interventions. How resources (cattle, water, money and family land) are accessed and controlled by different people, and the implications for climate resilience. For example, women and girls are impacted disproportionately from the effects of climate change due to factors such as entrenched discriminatory social and cultural norms compounded by limited mobility and decision-making power exacerbates climate-related impacts on women's poverty.
313. To address these barriers, the Programme will (i) Increase women economic empowerment through dairy production  and  access  and  control  over  productive  resources  (ii)  Reduce  time  and  labour  burdens  and  balance workloads activities (iii) Increase their voice and decision making at individual, household, group, and community levels and (iv) Increase Joint and equitable benefits sharing while promoting positive gender norms and practices. Young women and men, of different groupings, disabilities, ethnicity, and religion will be targeted to access productive assets, services and skills to create jobs and entrepreneurship. The Programme will recruit implementation partner or Service Providers  with  experience  in  community  centred  approaches  promoting  integration  of  young  men  and  women, vulnerable  groups,  persons  with  disabilities.  This  may  include  gender  focused,  youthful  enterprises,  NGOs,  the academia and researchers. The GESI action plan will be monitored and evaluated through the project M and E system ensuring sex, age and gender disaggregated data is collated to inform project interventions.

## G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

314. Disbursements/Fund  Flow. Designated  accounts  (DAs)  will  be  administered  following  'revolving  fund arrangements' as described in Annex 2 chapter 9. The Recipients/Borrowers will enter into MoU with the Implementing Partners (IPs) before any funds transferred to the IPs, except Tanzania where MoUs signed with the IPs only when they are not included in the financing agreement. Each project will open and maintain separate DAs in USD to receive

<!-- image -->

F

<!-- image -->

GCF funds from IFAD. Similarly, separate project accounts in local currency will be opened by PCUs, district offices and IPs to receive funds from the project designated/operating accounts. Countries where single treasury account is used, adequate chart of accounts will be used to segregate GCF sources and uses of funds from other financiers. In case of flow of fund issues to ILRI, direct payments to ILRI may be considered based on the MoUs signed between the Borrower and ILRI. However, all Withdrawal Applications and IFRs need to be submitted by the PCU of each Country.

315. Disbursements will be based on submission of quarterly Interim Financial Reports (IFRs) within 30 days after the period ends and upon availability of funds. IFRs and the Withdrawal Applications will be submitted to IFAD by PCUs through IFAD's Client Portal and will include a cash forecast for the next two quarters. As per GCF rules, there will be no disbursement unless 70% of GCF proceeds is utilized and all other GCF disbursement conditions are met.
316. Accounting and Financial Reporting . Rwanda (RDDP-II) and Uganda (ReLIV): Accounting and reporting will be performed through Country's IFMIS system and will follow national regulation, which is in a gradual process of transition  to  International  Public  Sector  Accounting  Standards  (IPSAS)  accrual  and  (IPSAS)  cash  standards respectively. Tanzania (C-SDTP) and Kenya (INReMP): Off-the-shelf accounting software will be used, and adequate chart of accounts will be developed to segregate sources and uses of funds. The Financial Statements will be prepared in accordance with IPSAS-accrual basis and IPSAS-Cash basis of accounting respectively.
317. Quarterly interim financial reports will be submitted to IFAD within 30 days after each quarter end and unaudited financial statements within four months of end of each financial year. In addition, Recipients/Borrowers shall certify semi-annual reports on the use of funds by source of funding, category, components, sub-components and activities in the required GCF budget format will be submitted within 30 days of period end. All implementing partners will be required to prepare the financial reports with standard templates and submit them to the executing agency's PCU. PCUs will prepare the consolidated financial statements and submit them to IFAD.
318. Internal Controls . Executing agencies will have the oversight responsibility over IPs. PCUs and all IPs will have adequate internal control arrangements in place to ensure that funds are used for intended purposes. Rwanda (RDDP-II) will be internally audited by an internal Auditor hired under the SPIU and will report to the PSC; Tanzania (C-SDTP) will be internally audited quarterly by the independent Internal Audit (IA) Unit under the aegis of the Internal Auditor General, which is established at MLF. Uganda (ReLIV) will be internally audited quarterly by the independent Internal Audit (IA) Unit under the Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development. Kenya (INReMP) will be internally audited quarterly by the independent Internal Audit (IA) Unit established under the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development.
319. External  Audit. Financial  statements  of  Rwanda  (RDDP-II),  Tanzania  (C-SDTP),  Kenya  (INReMP)  and Uganda (ReLIV) will be audited annually by the Auditors General in each country as per the International Auditing Standards, and the audit reports will be submitted to IFAD within six months of the Borrower's fiscal year-end. The auditor scope of work will provide a specific opinion on GCF funds.

## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

- X With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided confidently. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing the Secretariat with the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
- full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy, and
- redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential info.

## Secretariat's assessment of FP269

Proposal name:

Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DaiMA)

Accredited entity:

International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

Countries:

Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and United Republic of Tanzania

Project/programme size:

Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed in the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/B.42/02.

| Strengths                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Points of caution                                                                                                                                                                              |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| The first GCF dedicated livestock programme, developed in response to the climate risks affecting the sector, particularly drought and increasing temperatures and reduced availability of feed and fodder, while reducing greenhouse gas emissions, especially methane. | Multi-country programme, which might lead to unsynchronized implementation across the four countries. Mitigating measures have been included to decouple implementation in the case of delays. |
| Sectoral programme designed to build resilience across the dairy value chains across the East Africa region, through implementation by public and private actors, including local financial institutions.                                                                |                                                                                                                                                                                                |

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

## 2.1 Programme background

3. The dairy sector in East Africa, including Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania, faces significant challenges due to climate change. Dairy farming is a major contributor to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in each of these countries and the effects of climate change threaten its long-term sustainability. Inefficiencies in dairy farming lead to high GHG emission intensities per kilogram of milk. Additionally, many dairy farmers struggle with limited market access, challenges in sourcing local feed and fodder, inadequate extension and financial services, and difficulties in risk management. The sector is further impacted by low livestock genetic potential, herd management issues, animal diseases, insufficient extension support, limited awareness of climate-resilient and low-carbon practices and technologies, rangeland degradation and gender inequalities.

<!-- image -->

4. To address the above-mentioned challenges, this programme, 'DaIMA - Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation', will have a comprehensive and holistic approach to improve all aspects of the dairy sector, from improving policy, institutional and service delivery environments to building the capacity of smallholder farmers, including women, youth and marginalized groups, by strengthening climate resilience, reducing emissions across the dairy value chain and improving the livelihoods of dairy farmers in East Africa. It also involves strategies to improve access to finance and innovative technologies that offer both mitigation and adaptation benefits. As a result, dairy farmers and processors will be encouraged to adopt more efficient, climate-resilient practices that enhance incomes and lower production costs.
5. The total budget for the programme is USD 358.26 million. USD 58.20 million is allocated for component 1, USD 234.98 million for component 2 and USD 45.0 million for the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF) (component 3), with the remaining USD 13.36 million allocated for programme management and USD 6.72 million for monitoring and evaluation (M&amp;E).
6. The programme will impact 2.5 million direct and 15.4 million indirect beneficiaries across all four countries. The programme aims to reduce the milk emission intensities by 29 per cent (of which 28 per cent is from Kenya, 50 per cent from Rwanda, 33 per cent from Uganda and 29 per cent from the United Republic of Tanzania) over a 20-year lifespan. The programme is also expected to reduce total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms, while significantly increasing milk production by 34 per cent (of which 31 per cent is from Kenya, 73 per cent from Rwanda, 30 per cent from Uganda and 36 per cent from the United Republic of Tanzania).
7. Furthermore, it is projected to reduce total GHG emissions from milk production by 4.01 per cent. Specifically, emissions are expected to decline by 2.02 per cent (61,967 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq)) in Kenya, 9.56 per cent (56,507 t CO₂ eq) in Rwanda, 5.22 per cent (82,372 t CO₂ eq) in Uganda and 4.69 per cent (65,718 t CO₂ eq) in the United Republic of Tanzania. Additionally, methane emissions from enteric fermentation are anticipated to drop by 4.6 per cent, while methane from manure management is expected to decrease by 5.93 per cent. The programme will also contribute to estimated emission savings of 54,435 t CO₂ eq per year through improved energy generation and access.
8. Also, the programme contributes to the Food systems (Target 4), Ecosystems (Target 5), Clean Energy (Target 7), Local private sector ventures (Target 10) and National and regional financial institutions (Target 11) objectives of the GCF Updated Strategic Plan (USP-2).
9. The DaIMA Programme strengthens climate resilience in East Africa's dairy sector by enabling 2.5 million smallholder farmers to adopt adaptive practices. It enhances water access, pasture management, animal health, and disease control, leading to increased milk productivity and improved food and nutrition security, while reducing vulnerability to droughts and climate hazards (Target 4). The programme will also restore and sustainably manage over 178,000 hectares of degraded rangelands (Target 5) and strengthen dairy value chains by upgrading milk collection and processing facilities powered by renewable energy (Target 7). Under Component 3.1, the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF) will provide two loan products to support the adoption of climate-smart technologies and sustainable dairy practices. Through this facility, DaIMA mobilizes concessional loans and technical assistance to promote lowemission investments and institutional capacity building in the dairy sector (Targets 10 and 11).
10. The programme will have three components as set out below.

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Component 1: Strengthened national capacities and enhanced enabling environment for low emissions climate resilient dairy sectors (total cost: USD 58.2 million; GCF cost: USD 17.28 million)

<!-- image -->

11. Component 1 will create a supportive environment for transforming East Africa's dairy sector, driving higher productivity, enhancing climate resilience and reducing emission intensity. It will focus on four key outputs: establishing a supportive policy and regulatory framework, including enhanced consumer awareness and nutritional balance (output 1.1); enhancing public service delivery in the dairy sector, particularly through specialized livestock extension and veterinary services (output 1.2); strengthening the capacity to monitor GHG emissions and climate vulnerabilities (output 1.3); and promoting regional cooperation and improving knowledge management (output 1.4).
12. Output 1.1 (Climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment established) aims to establish a climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment to support the sustainable transformation of the dairy sector in East Africa. Activity 1.1.1 focuses on reviewing and formulating dairy sector policies, strategies and regulations to enhance climate change adaptation and mitigation. This includes aligning national policies with nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and national adaptation plans, improving trade and investment regulations and fostering private sector engagement. Sub-activities 1.1.1.1 and 1.1.1.2 will support national-level policy revisions and regional policy dialogue to harmonize dairy sector regulations and enhance technology transfer. Sub-activities 1.1.1.3 and 1.1.1.4 will strengthen dairy value chain governance and local stakeholder participation. Activity 1.1.2 will raise public awareness on the nutritional benefits of milk and food safety, promoting increased consumption of high-quality dairy products through educational campaigns and school milk programmes. Sub-activity 1.1.2.1 will focus on awareness initiatives for climate-vulnerable populations, while sub-activity 1.1.2.2 will enhance dairy inspection systems to ensure quality standards and reduce milk waste. Activity 1.1.3 will work towards improving land tenure security for sustainable dairy farming, identifying tenure risks and supporting land-use planning to enhance dairy productivity. Sub-activities 1.1.3.1 and 1.1.3.2 will provide training, public awareness campaigns and capacity-building efforts to secure land rights, particularly for women and youth. These activities collectively aim to create a more climate-resilient, inclusive and sustainable dairy sector in East Africa.
13. Output 1.2 (Enhancing public service delivery in the dairy sector, particularly through specialized livestock extension and veterinary services) aims to enhance service delivery in the dairy sector through improved veterinary and extension services. Activity 1.2.1 focuses on strengthening veterinary services to improve animal health, boost milk yields and reduce losses caused by climate-induced diseases. This will involve expanding veterinary outreach, investing in diagnostic laboratory capacities and equipping veterinarians, paraveterinarians and community animal health workers with better tools and training. Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 will support the deployment of veterinarians and paraveterinary professionals, particularly young and female practitioners, while sub-activity 1.2.1.2 will enhance the diagnostic capacity of veterinary laboratories and improve feed quality testing. Activity 1.2.2 focuses on strengthening extension services to support a low-carbon and climate-resilient dairy sector. This will involve innovative advisory approaches such as digital tools, Farmer Field Schools and participatory extension models to enhance farmers' knowledge of climate-smart dairy practices. Sub-activity 1.2.2.1 will develop training modules on climate mitigation and adaptation, including topics like fodder production, biogas management and animal welfare. Sub-activity 1.2.2.2 will train master trainers and facilitators to scale up climate-smart practices, with a focus on women and youth participation. Sub-activity 1.2.2.3 will promote innovative extension tools, including digital platforms, text messaging service based advisories, social media and mobile apps like iCow in the United Republic of Tanzania and Kenya Agricultural Observatory Platform. These initiatives will increase farmers' access to knowledge, improve productivity and enhance climate resilience in the dairy sector.
14. Output 1.3 (Strengthening the capacity to monitor GHG emissions and climate vulnerabilities) will enhance GHG emission measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) capacities and improve climate vulnerability monitoring in the dairy sector. Activity 1.3.1

<!-- image -->

- focuses on developing national MRV systems to track dairy emissions accurately and support climate commitments. Activity 1.3.2 will improve GHG emission measurement by collecting onfarm data, refining emission factors and using advanced modelling tools. Activity 1.3.3 will conduct climate risk and vulnerability assessments, strengthening early warning systems and national feed balance mechanisms. Activity 1.3.4 will facilitate low-carbon certification and enable dairy stakeholders to access carbon finance by developing verification standards and certification systems. These efforts will enhance climate reporting, attract climate finance and strengthen dairy sector resilience.
15. Output 1.4 (Promoting regional cooperation and improving knowledge management) will aim to enhance knowledge management and regional cooperation to promote the replication of climate-resilient and low-carbon dairy practices across East Africa. Activity 1.4.1 will support regional centres of excellence and national research institutions to build capacity in GHG measurement, livestock productivity and climate adaptation. This includes developing a regional database to document best practices and emissions data. Activity 1.4.2 will establish regional cooperation platforms for knowledge exchange, involving public and private stakeholders, research institutions and international organizations. These platforms will facilitate policy discussions, scientific innovations and best practice sharing through workshops, conferences and study tours. Additionally, participation in global climate policy events and South-South and Triangular Cooperation will strengthen regional and international collaboration on dairy sector sustainability and climate finance solutions.

## Component 2: Enabling low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain development (total cost: USD 234 million; GCF cost: USD 100.7 million)

16. Component 2 focuses on enhancing incomes and productivity for smallholder dairy farmers, cooperatives and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) by promoting sustainable, climate-resilient and low-emission growth pathways. The three associated outputs outlined in the following paragraphs will also contribute to gender equality, improved food security and nutrition for rural and urban consumers and greater empowerment and inclusion of women and marginalized groups.
17. Output 2.1 (Smallholders' and cooperative capacity development strengthened) aims to improve market access, strengthen dairy cooperatives and promote sustainable practices for smallholder dairy farmers. Activity 2.1.1 will enhance the governance, capacity and service delivery of dairy cooperatives, ensuring fair prices, reduced milk losses and improved quality standards. Activity 2.1.2 will build productive alliances along the dairy value chain, supporting business management, financial literacy and digitalization for better efficiency, traceability and access to credit. Activity 2.1.3 will promote renewable energy and waste management solutions by introducing solar-powered milk cooling, energy-efficient equipment and sustainable waste recycling. These initiatives will boost farmer incomes, enhance climate resilience and reduce emissions in the dairy sector.
18. Output 2.2 (Breeding, animal health, feed and fodder, manure management improved) will reduce farm-level GHG emissions and enhance productivity through climate-smart breeding, improved animal health, better feeding practices and sustainable manure management. Activity 2.2.1 will promote climate-resilient dairy breeds by scaling up artificial insemination and breeding programmes to enhance milk yields while reducing emissions. Activity 2.2.2 focuses on disease prevention, improved herd management and digital tools for animal health monitoring. Activity 2.2.3 will support forage production, water harvesting and certified seed multiplication to improve feed availability. Activity 2.2.4 promotes climate-smart feeding strategies to lower enteric methane emissions, while activity 2.2.5 will enhance manure management and nutrient recycling for soil health and emission reduction. Lastly, activity 2.2.6 will scale up biodigesters to convert manure into clean energy, reducing deforestation and providing sustainable fuel alternatives. These initiatives will boost dairy sector resilience, increase milk production and contribute to climate change mitigation.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.42/02/Add.11

Page 118

19. Output 2.3 (Pasture and grasslands management improved) aims to improve pasture and grassland management to enhance climate resilience, biodiversity and carbon sequestration while reducing GHG emissions from dairy production. Activity 2.3.1 will strengthen rangeland governance by supporting community-led pasture management planning, securing land tenure rights and resolving resource conflicts. Activity 2.3.2 will focus on restoring degraded rangelands through rotational grazing, integration of leguminous trees for carbon sequestration, and soil and water conservation practices. Additionally, the programme will increase water availability for dairy cattle by investing in water harvesting systems, such as micro-dams and rock catchments, with a view to ensuring sustainable access to water in extensive dairy systems. These interventions will support sustainable livestock production, reduce land degradation and enhance dairy sector resilience.

## Component 3: Green Dairy Financing Facility (total cost: USD 45 million; GCF cost: USD 17.5 million loan and USD 10 million grant)

20. Component 3 will establish GDFF in the four target countries to enhance access to finance for dairy value chain actors, including farmers, cooperatives, SMEs and technology providers. By addressing financial and technological barriers, GDFF will support the adoption of climate-smart technologies and sustainable practices, facilitating the transition to low-carbon and climate-resilient dairy value chains. Businesses identified under component 2 will be encouraged to apply for GDFF financing to further strengthen the sector's sustainability. This component is structured in two parts - output 3.1 (Effective green financing for dairy value chains) and output 3.2 (Capacity-building for green dairy investments).
21. Under output 3.1, GDFF will offer two loan products to support the adoption of climatesmart technologies and sustainable dairy practices. The Green Dairy Investment Partnership Loan is a long-term financing option for larger, well-established dairy businesses with annual turnovers above USD 800,000, offering loans between USD 250,000 and USD 550,000 with tenors of five to nine years. These loans will help businesses to expand smallholder networks, acquire productive cows, solarize equipment and improve milk transport. The Green Dairy Loan Product targets smaller SMEs and dairy cooperatives, providing short-term working capital loans of up to USD 250,000 (average USD 50,000) with a three-year tenure. Both loan types require businesses to demonstrate clear climate-smart investment ambitions and strong smallholder partnerships. Additionally, technical assistance will be provided to help businesses to prepare investment plans and optimize their operations post-financing.
22. Under output 3.2, GDFF will use a USD 10 million technical assistance facility to enhance the capacity of banks and dairy sector businesses in implementing climate-smart projects. The technical assistance facility will address financial and non-financial barriers, ensuring effective green financing. Key activities include (i) capacity development for banks - wholesale and retail banks will receive training and support to integrate green dairy finance products, improve risk management and enhance reporting on sustainability impacts; (ii) support for SMEs - dairy businesses will receive tailored assistance in business planning, investment readiness, financial management and climate adaptation strategies to improve loan access and investment success; and (iii) regional coordination and knowledge-sharing - best practices, lessons learned and technical expertise will be shared among stakeholders to strengthen green finance in the dairy sector. This initiative aims to scale up climate-smart investments, improve operational efficiency and support sustainable growth in the dairy value chain.

## Monitoring and evaluation components (total cost: USD 6.7 million; GCF cost: USD 1.4 million)

23. Each executing entity (EE) will manage and coordinate the overall M&amp;E system, leveraging existing project frameworks financed by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD). Responsibilities include allocating resources, aggregating and verifying data, producing reports and organizing learning events to enhance sector-wide knowledge. The Regional Steering Committee, supported by rotating national EEs, will ensure harmonized

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.42/02/Add.11

Page 119

- reporting and methodological guidance. International Livestock Research Institute (ILRI) will provide additional M&amp;E support, such as templates and methodologies, while data capture and reporting will be handled by country-level project management units (PMUs). IFAD will oversee the process through annual supervision missions, including mid-term and final evaluations.
24. For component 3, a senior coordinator will be recruited at IFAD Kenya Regional Office to oversee GDFF coordination, M&amp;E, reporting and knowledge management. During the start-up phase, financial and M&amp;E specialists will assist the DaIMA PMU in establishing a reporting and evaluation system for GDFF, aligned with policy of IFAD, the DaIMA PMU and wholesale banks. GDFF monitoring and reporting will be integrated into the overall DaIMA regional M&amp;E framework.

## Project management (total cost: USD 13.35 million; GCF cost: USD 3.049 million)

25. IFAD will serve as the AE for the programme, overseeing its overall management. This includes programme appraisal and administrative, fiduciary and technical supervision to ensure the achievement of the expected outputs and outcomes, as well as programme closure and final evaluation. IFAD will fulfil these responsibilities in line with the accreditation master agreement with GCF, coordinating efforts through its headquarters in Rome, the East and Southern Africa Regional Office in Nairobi and country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania. IFAD will also set up a dedicated programme task force consisting of IFAD experts from within its headquarters and regional and country offices.
26. Additionally, a Regional Steering Committee will oversee regional alignment and value addition, while governments in each country will implement the programme through designated EEs. National programme steering committees will provide oversight, and programme implementation units will handle coordination. International and national entities, including ministries of agriculture and specialized organizations like ILRI, will offer technical support to national teams.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

27. The programme will impact 2.5 million direct and 15.4 million indirect beneficiaries across all four countries. As detailed in paragraph 6 above, the programme aims to reduce the milk emission intensities by 29 per cent over a 20-year lifespan and is expected to reduce total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms while increasing milk production by 34 per cent.
28. The programme is projected to reduce total GHG emissions from milk production by 4.01 per cent (see para. 7 above). Additionally, methane emissions from enteric fermentation are anticipated to drop by 4.6 per cent, while methane from manure management is expected to decrease by 5.93 per cent. The programme will also contribute to estimated emission savings of 54,435 t CO₂ eq per year through improved energy generation and access.
29. The programme will directly contribute to the results of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024-2027 in the areas of food systems, ecosystems, clean energy, local private sector early-stage ventures and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises innovation by supporting resilient market chains and GDFF.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

30. The programme takes a holistic approach to reshaping the dairy sector in East Africa, aiming to lower GHG emissions while enhancing the well-being of dairy producers. By

<!-- image -->

Page 120

- strengthening policy frameworks, institutional capacity and service delivery, alongside expanding financial access and climate-smart solutions for smallholder farmers, it seeks to drive a comprehensive transformation across the dairy value chain. Its strategy integrates private sector engagement and financial mechanisms to promote sustainable, low-carbon growth that extends well beyond the programme's initial investment. The scalability of its interventions, coupled with knowledge-sharing, improved regulatory frameworks and the promotion of climate-resilient practices, ensures a long-term impact on the dairy industries.
31. The programme enhances access to finance and alternative technologies, creating opportunities for local private sector actors, including dairy cooperatives and milk processors, to overcome financial and technological limitations that have historically hindered their growth and sustainability. By addressing these key constraints, the programme fosters innovation, efficiency and resilience within the dairy value chain. This approach enhances productivity and profitability and also serves as a catalyst for systemic change, promoting the adoption of sustainable, climate-smart practices that contribute to a low-emission, climate resilient livestock sector. The programme supports the adoption of more resilient animal breeds, improved livestock husbandry practices and windrow composting techniques to reduce methane emissions from enteric fermentation in livestock. It also promotes more efficient manure management systems, which help to lower GHG emissions while improving soil health and nutrient recycling.
32. Additionally, it lays the groundwork for long-term sectoral transformation, ensuring that the benefits extend beyond individual businesses to influence policy frameworks, market structures and broader economic development. Through these targeted interventions, the programme demonstrates a scalable and far-reaching impact, positioning the dairy sector as a model for sustainable agricultural development in the region.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

33. DaIMA contributes to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by enhancing financial access, increasing productivity, improving food security and nutrition, fostering innovation in climate-smart technologies, promoting sustainable dairy production and supporting biodiversity conservation and rangeland restoration.
34. The programme contributes to SDG 3 (Good health and well-being) by promoting safe, high-quality dairy consumption and raising nutrition awareness. Strengthening veterinary services supports SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production) by improving animal health, increasing productivity and reducing emissions. Additionally, by ensuring better food safety and access to nutritious dairy products, it advances SDG 2 (Zero hunger), fostering food security and improved nutrition in both rural and urban communities.
35. It also supports environmental sustainability by enhancing soil conservation, biodiversity and ecosystem health. It aligns with SDG 15 (Life on land) through rangeland restoration and biodiversity conservation, combating climate change and maintaining ecosystem balance. By promoting SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production), it integrates circular economy principles, improves manure management for biogas production, and enhances soil health through nutrient recycling. It also drives economic growth by creating jobs, reducing poverty (SDG 1) and enhancing productivity through climate-smart technologies. It strengthens dairy farmers' incomes and financial access, fostering a resilient, low-carbon dairy sector while promoting innovation and infrastructure development (SDG 9).

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium to high

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.42/02/Add.11

Page 121

36. The sustainability of dairy farming in the region is increasingly at risk owing to rising temperatures, erratic weather patterns and resource constraints, all of which negatively affect milk production, feed availability and animal health. Smallholder farmers, who constitute the majority of dairy producers, are particularly vulnerable as they operate on a small scale with limited resources and infrastructure. Their productivity remains low as a result of insufficient access to quality feed, veterinary services and climate-smart technologies. Additionally, limited training and technical knowledge prevent farmers from adopting more efficient and sustainable dairy farming methods. Financial barriers further hinder investments in improved breeding practices, better manure management and energy-efficient dairy processing technologies. The programme seeks to address these issues by providing technical assistance, financial support and capacity-building initiatives to enable smallholder dairy farmers to transition to climateresilient and low-emission dairy production. By equipping farmers with the necessary skills, resources and innovative solutions, the programme aims to enhance productivity, reduce GHG emissions and ensure the long-term sustainability of the dairy sector in East Africa.

## 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

37. DaiMA serves as a comprehensive framework to address the impacts of climate change on the dairy sector and the sector's GHG emissions. It aligns with the NDCs, nationally appropriate mitigation actions, national adaptation plans for agriculture and national agriculture policies, contributing to national development plans for climate-smart dairy value chains. By promoting climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies, the programme aims to drive resilient, low-carbon growth in the dairy sector, with a particular focus on methane reduction.
38. The NDCs and other national strategies define each country's approach to reducing GHG emissions and adapting to climate change. The proposed programme aligns with these national strategies by incorporating various measures aimed at enhancing climate resilience in the agriculture and livestock sectors. These measures include the promotion of climate-resilient crops that can withstand extreme weather conditions, the development of climate-resilient livestock breeds that are better adapted to heat stress and disease, and the expansion of crop and livestock insurance schemes to protect farmers against climate-related risks.
39. Overall, the programme will provide technical and policy support to the ministries responsible for the dairy sector in reviewing and formulating national policies, strategies and regulations that are critical for the sustainable transformation of the dairy industry. These efforts will ensure that national policies are fully aligned with NDC commitments, driving a shift towards low-carbon, climate-resilient dairy production that benefits both the environment and rural communities.

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

40. The programme's economic and financial analyses assess profitability from both a societal and a participant perspective, using the Livestock Sector Investment and Policy Toolkit to model dairy production across four countries over a 20-year period. Five dairy herd models, reflecting different production systems, were developed per country based on national herd structures. Financial models were also created for milk collection points, chilling centres, processing units (all solar powered) and service providers. The programme's GHG mitigation impact was evaluated using the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model.
41. The overall financial analysis of the programme indicates the profitability of the programme's investments for the farmers and cooperatives. It uses a 17.5 per cent discount rate

<!-- image -->

Page 122

- (reflecting average lending rates in the target countries) based on incremental net cash flows comparing scenarios with and without the programme.
42. At the regional level, the DaIMA programme is economically viable, with an expected net present value of USD 181.20 million and an economic rate of return (ERR) of 25.0 per cent under the baseline scenario. Country-level analyses for the United Republic of Tanzania, Uganda, Rwanda and Kenya also confirm viability. When the economic value of GHG emissions is considered, the expected net present value rises to USD 217.87 million (ERR 27.9 per cent) under a low-carbon price and to USD 254.64 million (ERR 30.3 per cent) under a high carbon price scenario.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

43. The DaIMA programme to be implemented across four countries in East Africa is assessed as category B with a moderate environmental and social risk rating. The programme has been screened against IFAD Social, Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures and the GCF Environmental and Social Policy. The AE shared an environmental and social management framework and stakeholder engagement plan as part of the funding proposal. Anticipated environmental and social risks are likely to be related to pollution associated with the intensification of dairy production, lack of appropriate waste management and child labour associated with engaging youths in the dairy value chain. The programme also proposes to improve pasture productivity and quality by enhancing the composition of forage varieties, fodder conservation and better pasture management, coupled with low-carbon sources of energy in milk processing.
44. During the review process, information was limited on the resilient seed variety fodder crops to be adopted to eliminate invasive species, specific national legislation and policies for agriculture and livestock for the beneficiary countries and information on envisaged infrastructure works that are likely to include the construction of veterinary laboratories, centres for artificial insemination, water harvesting systems such as micro-dams and cattle sheds. Following the GCF appraisal mission to Rome in March 2025 and various review iterations, the AE provided specifications on the type of infrastructure works to be adopted on the programme and the promotion of locally proven fodder varieties to eliminate the potential of introducing invasive species.
45. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and environmental and social safeguard 7 (Indigenous Peoples). An Indigenous Peoples planning framework is provided as an annex to the environmental and social management framework. The AE has confirmed that the Indigenous Peoples planning framework will not be confined to one or two pastoralist groups and that it will be applied thoroughly. In line with its roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and EEs. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for
4. assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made. 46. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment. The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM-2021/18 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The AE provided SEAH safeguarding in its submission to this funding proposal. During the operational phases, especially construction related, it will be import for the project to ensure that there is a comprehensive SEAH policy and overall continuum of care with respect to gender-based violence (GBV). The AE has therefore highlighted potential SEAH/GBV risks for all activities, including activity risk assessment in alignment with the GCF SEAH safeguards requirement.

<!-- image -->

SEAH mitigation measures have been included, including post-mitigation, to ensure that any residual SEAH/GBV impacts are addressed and monitored accordingly. Since the project is targeting four countries in the region with dynamic SEAH/GBV and gender inequalities contexts, it is highly recommended that the AE also take a closer look (during implementation and monitoring) at the differentiated approaches where applicable.

## 4.2 Gender policy

47. In compliance with the GCF Gender Policy, the AE prepared and submitted a gender assessment together with its gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) action plan. Informed by the IFAD gender analysis approach, the assessment covers key areas required for gender mainstreaming and specific interventions to this project. The gender assessments discuss the targeting criteria and provide sex and gender/social disaggregation targets for its interventions. The GESI action plan will be carried out through an implementation partner and a range of service providers included in the action plan. These may include youth enterprises, nongovernmental organizations, academia and researchers. The line ministries will also be key during implementation of the GESI action plan as these already have gender focal points. In addition, a GESI specialist will be appointed for each country's operation. Additional expertise will be procured under GDFF and technical assistance sub-activities to ensure implementation of the gender action plan. The specific technical assistance will include (i) capacity strengthening and technical support provided to partner staff and agents to help them to deliver the gender action plan; (ii) the recruitment of experienced service providers to support outreach, gendered approaches and capacity-building at the community level; and (iii) ongoing
48. 5 per cent comprising persons with disabilities and Indigenous Peoples. Women, youth and persons with disabilities will be targeted with technological solutions to reduce methane and GHG emissions, and with financial support services to sustain efficient production and marketing processes. The objective of the DaIMA GESI action plan is to ensure that women, in, and benefit from, the programme in these communities. Social inclusion considerations for these groups will be achieved by increasing their participation in project activities, economic empowerment opportunities and access to information and skills, as well as market linkages and value addition.

GESI technical support during implementation. DaIMA aims for 40 per cent of project beneficiaries to be women, 30 per cent youth and youth, people with disabilities and marginalized groups have equal opportunities to participate

## 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1. Overall project/programme assessment (high risk)

## 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project/programme (low risk)

49. IFAD is an international AE with nearly 50 years of development work in global agriculture and food security. To date, IFAD has provided approximately USD 24 billion in grants and loans for agricultural and rural development projects in 109 developing countries. The project EEs are the finance ministries and development banks in each of the host countries.

## 4.3.3. Project/programme-specific execution risks (high risk)

50. Sovereign credit risk. The GCF loan will be extended to the governments of four host countries in the programme. Proceeds will then be channelled to public development banks and to retail banks or microfinance banks, which will on-lend to small dairy farmers and

<!-- image -->

- cooperatives. GCF credit exposure will be to the Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania with low sovereign credit rating between B- to B+.
51. Programme implementation risk. Within the four target countries, there is limited institutional capacity and skill set in the dairy industry including dairy technicians, social / gender specialists and national dairy boards, etc. which could lead to low adoption or project delays.  This is being mitigated by competitive recruitment of qualified project management units in each country with performance-based contracts.
52. Programme's concessional pricing. To ensure that full benefit of concessional loan pricing under Component 3 is passed on to end borrowers, the facility agreements will include provisions capping the margin of financial intermediaries.  Lending rates to end borrowers will be reduced by the lowered funding cost from this programme compared to the retail banks' own funding cost.

## 4.3.4. Compliance risk (medium risk)

53. The inherent money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risk associated with certain programme activities, such as the launch of a Green Dairy Financing Facility to provide green loans, is relatively elevated.
54. The AE will oversee the programme through its regional office in Kenya, as well as its country offices in the four target countries. The four target country governments will act as EEs for Components 1 &amp; 2 through their respective ministries and state-owned development financial institutions. The AE's financial management risk assessments of the recipient governments did not flag elevated risks related to ML/TF. The ministries of finance in the target countries will serve as EEs for Component 3 and will disburse local currency loans to local Public Development Banks in each target country to execute green dairy sector financing operations.
55. Anti-money laundering/countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) controls to be implemented by the AE, which is responsible for overall fiduciary management, include training conducted during project preparation and implementation. In addition, the AE assesses counterparty integrity risk taking a risk-based approach, incorporating integrity due diligence measures including daily screening of project counterparties including EEs, payment beneficiaries, and procurement counterparties, to identify ML/TF and other prohibited practices risks. Furthermore, capacity-building training for finance staff will be conducted as an additional safeguard against financial management and fiduciary risks. In addition, the four PDBs are subject to AML/CFT regulations in their respective countries, and the retails banks that the PDBs will on-lend to will be selected based on criteria that includes whether they operate under prudential supervision with solid institutional and financial conditions per central bank standards.
56. Based on the elevated ML/TF risk posed by some of the proposed programme activities and the various controls and mitigation measures to be applied during project preparation and implementation, the overall compliance risk is determined to be medium.

## 4.3.5. GCF portfolio concentration risk

57. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal within monitoring threshold on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area, AE concentration and single proposal.

## 4.3.6. Recommendation

58. It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.
