## 4.4 Fiduciary

59. IFAD serves as the AE and is responsible for overall programme management, including appraisal, fiduciary and technical oversight, supervision and closure, in line with its accreditation master agreement with GCF. IFAD will execute these functions through its headquarters in Rome, regional office in Nairobi and country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania. Implementation will be carried out by national EEs in each country, including key ministries, public development banks and agricultural agencies. Programme coordination units will be established or integrated within existing structures and staffed with competitively recruited government personnel, with IFAD involved in the recruitment process through a no-objection mechanism. The programme coordination units will oversee day-to-day coordination, implementation and reporting.
60. Component 3 centres on operationalizing GDFF, a regional initiative to expand inclusive green finance for climate-smart dairy investments. Each country will implement a dedicated line of credit managed by public development banks, which act as wholesale banks and report regularly to the programme coordination units and IFAD. These banks will on-lend to retail banks (commercial or prudentially supervised microfinance institutions), which will extend loans to end beneficiaries using their existing frameworks and digital platforms.
61. Disbursement will follow a revolving fund arrangement, with each country operating separate USD Designated Accounts and local currency accounts for downstream transfers. Where treasury single account systems are used, GCF funds will be tracked via a dedicated chart of accounts. Disbursements will be based on quarterly interim financial reports and withdrawal applications, conditional on 70 per cent utilization of prior tranches. Rwanda and Uganda will use integrated financial management information systems aligned with International Public Sector Accounting Standards, while Kenya and the United Republic of Tanzania will apply commercial accounting software with GCF-specific coding. Programme coordination units will consolidate financial reports from implementing partners and submit them to IFAD. Internal controls will be enforced through national audit systems, with Rwanda using a project-specific auditor and the other countries relying on established internal audit units. Annual external audits will be conducted by each country's Auditor General, with specific audit opinions on GCF funds due within six months of fiscal year-end.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

62. The DaIMA funding proposal presents a well-structured results architecture anchored in a robust theory of change. The programme is designed to deliver transformative impacts in the East African dairy sector through a combination of mitigation and adaptation measures across Kenya, Uganda, Rwanda and the United Republic of Tanzania. The causal logic is clearly articulated and is supported by a coherent results framework that links activities and outputs to expected outcomes and long-term impact, in line with the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.42/02/Add.11

63. The programme contributes to three GCF results areas: mitigation through forestry and land use, adaptation through the areas of most vulnerable people and communities, and ecosystems and ecosystem services. It commits to reporting on GCF core indicator 1 (GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered), with an expected reduction in emissions of approximately 2.17 million t CO ₂ eq; core indicator 2 (direct and indirect beneficiaries reached), estimated at 2.5 million and 15.4 million respectively; and core indicator 4 (hectares of natural resource areas brought under improved low-emission and/or climate-resilient management practice), with a target area of 178,362 hectares. These indicators are complemented by programme-specific and supplementary indicators.
64. The monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) system is comprehensively described in annex 11 to the funding proposal and is well aligned with GCF guidance and IFAD evaluation standards. Given the multi-country nature of the programme, the MEL plan appropriately outlines mechanisms for country-level disaggregation of data and indicator targets. The MEL system includes baseline assessments, mid-term and final evaluations, and annual performance reviews. Outcome surveys will be harmonized across countries, and the programme plans to establish a regional Community of Practice to facilitate ongoing coordination and methodological consistency. Particular attention is given to the capacity-building of implementing partners and EEs to ensure the long-term sustainability of the MEL system and its integration with national frameworks. IFAD and ILRI will provide technical support in areas such as geographic information systems, MRV systems and carbon accounting.
65. The programme has allocated approximately USD 6.72 million for MEL activities. This is considered sufficient to support the ambitious and technically demanding results monitoring, particularly given the scale and scope of the interventions across four countries. The proposed MEL arrangements meet the requirements of the GCF Evaluation Policy and Integrated Results Management Framework, and the Secretariat is satisfied that the programme has the systems and resources in place to support rigorous tracking of results, evaluation of impact and learning for scale-up.

## 4.6 Legal assessment

66. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 September 2018 (the ' AMA '), and it became effective on 9 November 2018. The Accredited Entity's five-year accreditation term lapsed on 8 November 2023 but was extended by Decision B.37/18, paragraph (q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the Accredited Entity pursuant to Decision B.37/18, and negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.
67. The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.
68. The proposed programme will be implemented in four host countries. The GCF has signed a bilateral agreement on privileges and immunities with one of these host countries, namely, Rwanda. In the case of the remaining three host countries, namely Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda, GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in such countries may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

<!-- image -->

69. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in those countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.
70. With regard to the activities being financed by the GCF loans, the Accredited Entity has proposed that it will enter into a financing agreement with each host country, acting through its ministry of finance (each a ' MoF '). Each MoF will then on-lend the GCF proceeds to the wholesale bank in that host country under the terms of an on-lending agreement between MoF that the wholesale bank. Although it has been agreed that each MoF and each wholesale bank are executing entities for the programme, under the Accredited Entity's proposed contractual structure, the Accredited Entity will not have a direct contractual relationship with each wholesale bank which is responsible for the channelling of GCF proceeds and programme implementation. For the Accredited Entity to pass down and enforce the relevant requirements of the AMA and funded activity agreement (' FAA ') on each wholesale bank, the Accredited Entity has agreed in the term sheet that it will ensure that each MoF passes down, monitors, supervises and enforces such requirements against each wholesale bank in a manner that allows the Accredited Entity to comply with the AMA and FAA requirements as if it had a direct contractual relationship with that wholesale bank.
71. Since the Accredited Entity's direct counterparty is a sovereign state (as one of the executing entities), the Secretariat has agreed to disapply the provisions of the AMA allowing GCF to step in to the Accredited Entity's contractual arrangements with the Executing Entity, i.e., each MoF as, due to the sovereign nature of the counterparty and the contracts, it is not feasible for the Accredited Entity to allow GCF or any other party to take over its contractual relationship with the executing entities under the subsidiary agreements. Accordingly, and similar to the arrangements for FP143, FP162, FP183, FP236, SAP012 and SAP017, as a condition for execution of the FAA, the Accredited Entity is required to inform the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each host country of this disapplication and its implications. An express provision has been included in the term sheet to provide for such disapplication.
72. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:
5. (a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;

(b) Signature of the FAA in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

- (c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's review of FP269

Proposal name:

Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DaiMA)

Accredited entity:

International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

Countries:

Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and United Republic of Tanzania

Project/programme size:

Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This funding proposal for a large, public-sector cross-cutting programme in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) as accredited entity (AE). 1  The Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DaIMA) programme will operate over six years in four contiguous East African countries: Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania (see figure as GCF grant finance and USD 208.26 million as co-finance from IFAD and two of the
2. 1). The total cost of the programme is USD 358.26 million, of which USD 150 million is proposed governments (see section 1.7 below).
2. The proposed executing entities (EEs) for the implementation of components 1 and 2 are as follows: in Kenya, the Government of Kenya through its National Treasury, the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development and the Agricultural Finance Cooperation; in Rwanda, the Government of Rwanda thorough its Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning, Rwanda Agriculture Board within the Ministry of Agriculture and Animal Resources and the Development Bank of Rwanda; in Uganda, Government of Uganda through its Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development, the Ministry of Agriculture, Animal Industry and Fisheries and Uganda Development Bank; and in the United Republic of Tanzania, the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania through its Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Livestock and Fisheries and Tanzania Agricultural Development Bank. No overall executing entity is identified for component 3 in the funding proposal, section A.20, but this is understood to be IFAD itself.
3. The aim of the proposed programme is:

'to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from the dairy sector while increasing the resilience of livestock-dependent communities. The programme will support a shift from low production efficiency to improved, gender-sensitive, integrated and NDC [nationally determined contribution]-aligned practices that reduce emissions at farm and food system levels (including interventions that reduce milk losses and increase milk collection, decarbonize processing with renewable energies, formalize dairy value chains and strengthen partnerships with the private sector), as well as interventions that promote circularity, and increase rangelands ecosystems resilience and services (including carbon sequestration in grasslands)' (funding proposal, para. 2).

4. The programme's theory of change is also summarized in paragraph 2.
5. The programme's interventions, geographically targeted as shown in figure 1 below, will address climate change adaptation and mitigation issues across the various dairy production systems and along the dairy value chain, while addressing a number of barriers. It will follow a

1  This assessment is based on the full and final funding proposal package received by the iTAP on 24 March 2025.

<!-- image -->

holistic approach and will simultaneously scale up: (i) farm and herd-level practices to increase productivity, reduce emission intensity, and decrease absolute emissions compared to a business-as-usual scenario; (ii) farm-level solutions for manure management and nutrient recycling, to improve circularity, resilience and reduce emissions; (iii) landscape-level interventions to improve the resilience and services of rangelands ecosystems, particularly soil carbon sequestration, and water and biomass supply for animal feed; and (iv) value-chain level interventions , to reduce milk losses (and corresponding greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions) and increase the use of renewable energy. A useful detailed table, linking the climate drivers with interventions and their benefits, is available in annex 2 to the feasibility study (table 23).

## 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

7. The programme will target two of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to enhance the resilience of: (i) most vulnerable people and communities (adaptation result area (ARA) 1), and (ii) ecosystems and ecosystem services (ARA4). Despite the core goal of enhancing dairy productivity and production, the programme does not target the GCF results area of increased resilience in relation to health, food and water security, but highlights cobenefits in this regard in section D.3 of the funding proposal.
8. In addition, the programme will target two of the GCF results areas for mitigation, aiming to reduce GHG emissions from (i) energy generation and access (mitigation result area (MRA) 1); and (ii) forests and land use (MRA4, which includes livestock farming), both by reducing emissions from dairy farming, particularly release of methane; and by increasing the capacity of carbon sinks through pasture and rangeland restoration.
9. Climate-change risk and impacts. The funding proposal highlights the climate context of the four countries, and undertakes a systematic analysis of climate hazards. This covers observed changes historically in four key parameters across the region - a mostly declining volume of precipitation, increased annual average maximum temperature, declining relative humidity in some areas, and increased rainfall variability throughout the region, as well as increased frequency of dry days, very wet days, and very warm days. Future projections on rainfall vary across the region, with arid and semi-arid regions expected to receive less precipitation, while the highlands and lake regions may receive more rainfall. Mean maximum and minimum temperature are projected to increase across the region, as is mean relative humidity in Kenya and along Lake Victoria. All areas are projected to experience rainfall variability, with some experiencing more dry days and widespread increase in very wet days and very warm days. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) notes that the AE chose not to include analysis of duration hot days, given the complexity and variety of de�initions of a heatwave 2 , thus missing an opportunity to explore the link between prolonged heat stress and declines in animal health, milk production, fertility and calving complications.
10. The funding proposal demonstrates how these observed and projected changes in climate are impacting the four countries, causing increased exposure to droughts and floods, and increased vulnerability to rangeland degradation (compounding direct anthropogenic impacts). A clear analysis is provided of the vulnerability of the dairy sector and the agroecosystems on which it depends to these climate change and other challenges, with limited access to quality inputs, limited water availability, frequent disease outbreaks, inadequate

2  Written questions from the iTAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025

<!-- image -->

- infrastructure, energy supply and access to markets, limited access to credit and finance, and land tenure insecurity.
11. The funding proposal correctly identifies heat stress as a critical climate hazard affecting dairy productivity and health. However, it defines heat stress primarily through temperature thresholds (&gt;35°C), and does not use the temperature-humidity index (THI), which is a scientifically recognized indicator for livestock stress, based on the combined effects of temperature and humidity. The absence of explicit THI consideration constitutes a methodological gap, leading to potential for: (i) underestimation of dairy-sector vulnerability; (ii) inaccurate assessment of productivity and mortality risks; and (iii) suboptimal targeting of adaptation measures.
12. Impacts of climate change at the dairy-farm level are shown to include:
- (a) A decline in milk yields, especially of high milk-yielding breeds;
- (b) Heat stress in dairy cattle;
- (c) Increased development of pathogens, diseases, parasites and vectors in dairy cattle;
- (d) Decreased feed production and productivity, due to reduced or excess soil moisture and fertility;
- (e) A decrease in the quantity and quality of pasture; and
- (f) Deterioration of fodder in unprotected warehouses due to increased humidity (see funding proposal, paras 1 and 35).
13. At the rangeland level, livestock owners face a decrease in rangeland and pasture productivity and forage quality. At the dairy value chain level, high temperatures compromise the safety of milk and dairy products, increase the risk of milk losses, and elevate costs per unit of produce.
14. Adaptation impact potential. The intended adaptation impact of the programme is not stated clearly in section D.1, which speaks broadly about efficiency, productivity and resilience of dairy value chains, this leads also to closely interwoven mitigation benefits. References to reducing vulnerability to particular climate hazards are scattered throughout the funding proposal, but are stated explicitly only in the feasibility study (see table 23) and even there, not comprehensively. The iTAP would have expected to see a clear statement in the funding proposal itself on how the programme interventions reduce vulnerability or exposure, or enhance adaptive capacity (see annex VI to decision B.33/12), in relation to the six key impacts of climate change identified in paragraphs 1 and 35. The iTAP recommends that this gap be addressed in the programme implementation manual, to avoid a situation in which adaptation impacts are tracked with less rigour than mitigation impacts.
15. Table 23 of the feasibility study (annex 2 to the funding proposal) provides the clearest analysis of how the specific exposure and vulnerability discussed above will be addressed through particular interventions. For example, the table shows how rangeland restoration interventions will build resilience against drought - increasing vegetation cover and enhancing water infiltration, as well as setting aside fodder reserves.
16. Measures to decrease cattle's exposure and vulnerability to increased heat, humidity or combined heat and humidity, are not fully covered in the funding proposal, and are mentioned only once in table 23. In response to a question from the iTAP about this, the AE indicated its intention to include a range of measures:

'to attenuate heat-stress effect on animal's reproduction and production parameters (e.g. improvement of livestock housing and animal welfare; trees for shade to reduce the exposure of livestock to heat stress / solar radiation in grazing fields; water harvesting and recycling for cattle watering; crossbreeding with heat-tolerant, disease-resilient and

<!-- image -->

- productive cross-breeds;…improved herd management - including observation on secondary signs of delayed oestrus due to heat stress).' 3
17. The iTAP recommends that this aspect be brought out strongly in the project implementation manual to be developed, with practical recommendations for management protocols on very hot days and during heatwaves.
18. Overall the programme aspires to directly benefit about 2.5 million individual dairy farmers and dairy value chain actors in 94 districts/counties that are vulnerable to climate change - approximately 467,000 people in Kenya, 614,000 in Rwanda, 665,000 in Uganda and 758,000 in the United Republic of Tanzania. Of the beneficiaries, at least 40 per cent will be women, 30 per cent youth and 5 per cent from marginalized groups (including people with disabilities). An estimated 15.4 million indirect beneficiaries will benefit from strengthened veterinary services reducing the spread of diseases exacerbated by climate change, enhanced extension and breeding services, and improved access to climate information. A total of about 2.7 million dairy cattle will be reached, and approximately 178,362 ha of rangelands and natural pastures will be brought under improved management. The funding proposal shows the following combination of criteria used to select intervention areas: (i) climate-risk hotspots; (ii) absolute methane emissions from the dairy sector; (iii) soil carbon sequestration potential in pastures and rangelands; and (iv) milk production by district/county.
19. Mitigation impact potential : The DaiMA programme aims to achieve total mitigation results of 2,169,485 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) over its 20-year lifespan. This results from a combination of emissions reductions (from the introduction of renewable-energy solutions and better dairy-cattle management) and increased carbon sequestration (from the rehabilitation of pastures and rangelands).
20. Estimates for these mitigation results were calculated using the best practice Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model-interactive (GLEAM-i) developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). GLEAM-i uses an embedded herd dynamic model, and estimates of feed requirements to predict direct emissions resulting from enteric methane and manure, based on digestibility and nitrogen content. Indirect emissions from the production of feed resources, and direct and embedded energy use are also included. Using this methodology, milk production is projected to increase by 34 per cent (through productivity increases and loss reductions), while reducing the milk emission intensity (i.e. the volume of methane emissions generated per kg of milk) by a significant 29 per cent, 4 and reducing the total feed intake by 5.23 per cent, compared with the without-project scenario.
21. The contributions of the three major clusters of interventions to the projected mitigation results are summarized in table 2.

| 3  | Pasture and rangeland restoration | Carbon sequestration | 814,220   | Significant increase in soil organic carbon, and related above-ground and below-ground biomass, compared with the without-project scenario - as a result of restoration and management interventions such as rotational grazing, earthworks for infiltration, reseeding of palatable species, and removal of encroacher bush                                                                                                                                                                                               |
|    | Total                             | Total                | 2,169,485 |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

22. Limitations to achieving mitigation potential : A challenge with achieving emission reductions related to dairy-herd management is that the reductions depend not only on successful implementation of all the improved practices (on breeding, animal health, fodder and feed, and manure management), but critically also on an assumption that herd sizes can be stabilized - an assumption which may not hold true in reality. The with-project scenario assumes a very low rate of herd size growth, and compare this with a slightly higher rate in the with-project scenario. The iTAP acknowledges that the programme interventions aim to increase milk yields without increasing head count, as a result of several efficiency and productivity-raising interventions as well as improving herd composition. But in reality, the programme and its proponents will have little control over the decisions made by the millions of farmers and producers involved over whether and to what extent to increase their cattle head count and business size in response to increasing demand, 5  as well as the incentives provided
2. by the programme itself (access to cold-chain technology, finance and markets). 23. In response to a question from the iTAP on whether herd stability is a realistic assumption, the AE pointed to evidence from Kenya showing that the dairy herd had remained around 5 million animals since 2002, while production of milk had increased by 60 per cent in the same period. According to IFAD, this is explained by various factors, including the use of mobile payment system (M-pesa) and offtake of unproductive animals. The AE also pointed out that 'the feed base and the collection capacity are not expendable, which means that farmers cannot grow a significantly larger numbers of animals, even if they wanted to'. They also pointed to the livestock-farmer field schools training modules on herd management, focusing on

5  Discussed in 'Selected Risk Factor 4' in the funding proposal, with mention also in paragraph 33 of projected dairy- grow from 1.4 billion litres of raw milk in 2020, to 2 billion litres in 2030, and 8 billion litres in 2050.

herd growth in the United Republic of Tanzania to satisfy future demand for dairy products, which is expected to

<!-- image -->

- the reproduction calendar, performance recording using technology, an enhanced female-tomale ratio through sexed-semen and reduction/off-take of unproductive animals, as well as production efficiencies (through breeding, feeding and animal health), and interventions to strengthen value chains and enhance market access 'allowing shifting the value from their livestock to the milk'. 6  The iTAP recommends tracking of herd sizes (as a combined result of births, deaths and acquisitions of new livestock) during project implementation. 24. A further factor which could undermine project results, in both mitigation and adaptation, is the uncertainty around how climate-change impacts will evolve during the programme implementation. For example, exposure of cattle to extreme climate conditions resulting from the combined effect of high temperature and high humidity may turn out to be even higher than projected. It is well documented that high THI conditions lead to higher mortality, lower fertility and reduced calving rates, which could counteract programme interventions to improve productivity, thus limiting both adaptation and mitigation results. 25. Monitoring landscape impacts: The iTAP notes the solid monitoring system (set out in annex 22 to the funding proposal) for rangeland restoration interventions, with clear indications of responsibilities for site-level ground-truthing and data collection on: (i) GPS coordinates of rangelands under intervention, and areas of rangelands where improved practices were implemented; (ii) surveys on species composition and improved biomass yield; (iii) the type of improved grazing management applied at the community level (rotational grazing, agroforestry, improved pasture management, management of grazing intensity, etc.); and (iv) the quantity and type of legume seeds used, date of planting or reseeding. This will be combined with remote sensing analysis through high-resolution satellite images (using, for example, the normalized difference vegetation index) to monitor the rangelands under restoration. On the mitigation side, a system is also in place for measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) of soil carbon sequestration in areas under restoration, including modelling for estimating the impact of management practices. Using baseline information on soil carbon stocks, soil texture, carbon inputs from vegetation and manure, as well as climate (all collected through the baseline survey), process-based models (e.g. RothC) will be used to estimate actual results. The team will also leverage data from the national MRV systems enhanced under component 1 and make estimations using tier 2 sequestration factors generated under subactivity 1.3.1.1 (e.g. with flux towers such as eddy covariance towers - see feasibility study for more details).
26. Overall, the impact potential is rated as medium to high.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

27. Paradigm shift relates to the degree to which the proposed programme can successfully catalyse impact beyond the GCF one-off investment of USD 150 million over six years, together with complementary IFAD projects. The programme, which will work with approximately 6.5 per cent of all dairy farmers in Kenya, 30 per cent in Rwanda, 11.3 per cent in Uganda and 9.1 per cent in the United Republic of Tanzania, aims to facilitate the transition from a dairy sector characterized by rising emissions, low production efficiency, market informality and climate vulnerability, with limited access to services and finance, to a more efficient, formal and lowcarbon, climate-resilient sector. By improving policy, institutional and service-delivery environments, building climate adaptation capacity, and enabling access to finance and climatesmart solutions for smallholder dairy farmers, the programme seeks to transform all aspects of the dairy value chain.

6  Written questions from the iTAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025.

<!-- image -->

28. Enabling environment for scaling up. Component 1 of the programme focuses on creating a climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment in the four countries, through work to review and formulate dairy-sector policies, strategies and regulations. Awareness campaigns will be run on the nutritional benefits of milk and food safety aspects. The programme also seeks to improve land-tenure security and management for sustainable dairy farming. Public-sector capacity for livestock support will be increased, enhancing veterinary services for better animal health, higher milk productivity and reduced losses, and extension services will be strengthened. At a regional scale, GCF financing supports the transformation of the dairy sector through interventions such as regional policy dialogues, cooperation platforms and centres of excellence; cross-country cooperation, and knowledge exchange; MRV systems using cutting-edge methods; climate risk and vulnerability assessments, including feed balance assessments; and provision of finance through the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF).
29. Focus on national MRV: The programme has the potential to make an important contribution to building MRV capacities in the four countries' governments and research institutions, for both climate risk and vulnerability assessments, improving greenhouse gas emission measurements in dairy value chains, as part of national MRV systems. The programme also aims to facilitate the development of a carbon certification system for the dairy sector, and reduce barriers to accessing carbon finance. Although the funding proposal does not mention the issue of benefit-sharing of potential proceeds from the sale of carbon credits in the future, in response to a question on this from the iTAP, IFAD stated: 'To ensure fair and inclusive access to carbon finance under pilot Activity 3.4, the Programme will support preparedness by establishing transparent mechanisms for beneficiary identification, benefit-sharing, and participation in governance structures across the dairy value chain'. 7
30. Retail bank uptake: The intention of the programme is to de-risk private investment and accelerate the adoption of low-emission, climate-resilient technologies and solutions by smallholders and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), catalysing private-sector engagement and scaling-up climate-finance flows. The programme is based on a thorough dairy market study (funding proposal, annex 2, appendix 1), and effectively targets many of the key barriers to technology dissemination and strengthening dairy value chains. At the same time, there is a possibility that demand for and uptake of loans through the GDFF will be lower than anticipated, limiting the paradigm shift achieved in financial markets. In the experience of the iTAP, a risk with providing lines of credit via commercial banks is that they end up not being drawn upon, because the banks fail to actively engage new customers in unfamiliar market sectors. Cheap liquidity alone has often proven insufficient to entice local commercial banks to broaden their lending portfolios to include higher-risk clients or enterprises with new business models. Risk participation agreements, which share a portion of credit losses between a concessional finance provider and the banks, may represent a more effective incentive mechanism but remain unexplored in the current programme design.
31. The funding proposal highlights the technical assistance programme that will work with the wholesale and retail banks, as well as the favourable loan terms, including long grace and repayment periods, but it is unclear what the programme can/will do to incentivize these retail banks to go out and actually build business with this particular client base. This is highlighted in Selected Risk Factor 8 in the funding proposal, which acknowledges that '[e]stablishing four relatively small-sized credit lines in a niche sub-sector like dairy poses several challenges. Relatively low funding base can constrain the interest of banks to participate in the scheme. Lack of understanding of the bankable options in green dairy lending can further reduce bank interest'. In response to a request to the AE to explain the approach to mitigating this risk in a bit more depth, IFAD stated that 'The financing will be made in tranches and only against proven success concerning the level and quality of the GDFF portfolio implementation. The

7  Written questions from the iTAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025.

<!-- image -->

- meetings held with candidate retail banks indicate strong demand by SMEs in the dairy sector. The low cost of capital to the retail banks, together with the TA grant, further incentivizes the utilization of resources'. 8  Nonetheless, experience shows that proactive measures beyond concessional pricing, such as risk-sharing facilities or partial credit guarantees, are often necessary to overcome entrenched risk aversion in commercial banks and to ensure that climate finance flows reach the intended underserved market segments.
32. Taking all these factors into account, paradigm shift potential is rated as medium to high.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

33. The programme is expected to have a number of social, economic and environmental cobenefits, as outlined below.
34. Social and health co-benefits. The programme will promote improved food safety and nutrition, and thus human health - using market formalization, awareness campaigns and product traceability to improve nutrition and health awareness, with a focus on the increased consumption of safe and high-quality dairy products (Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 3: Good health and well-being). The programme contributes to ending hunger, achieving food security and improving children's nutritional status (SDG 2: Zero hunger). In addition, by strengthening veterinary services, it aims to improve animal health and welfare, contributing to higher milk productivity and reduced losses, as well as decreased GHG emissions and increased farmers' incomes, which will, in turn, incentivize famers to adopt better measures on animal health and welfare (SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production). 35. Gender and social inclusion co-benefits. The programme includes strengthening of dairy groups and cooperatives, enhancing social cohesion. It has clear targets for the involvement of women, youth and marginalized groups (see paragraph 15 of this assessment). Project activities take into account the distinct traditional roles that women and men (and youth) play in rearing cattle, milking cows and producing dairy products in households and small businesses. These roles, as well as constraints on women's access to land, financing and other resources, are analysed in annex 8 (Gender assessment and action plan). The programme aims to reduce gender inequalities (SDG 5: Gender equality) by actively promoting women's participation in livestock-farmer field schools, and opening space for women and youth in traditionally male-dominated roles - including milk aggregation, animal health services, manure management, fodder production and cooperative leadership. The Gender Action Learning System Plus will be used - a participatory tool that engages households and communities in rethinking power dynamics, promoting joint decision-making, benefit-sharing, and reshaping socially acceptable roles through gender champions and peer influence. 36. Environmental co-benefits. The programme's interventions to restore and better manage rangelands and pastures will not only have core adaptation and mitigation results, but will also produce environmental co-benefits - such as an increase in biodiversity, with native species reintroduced or allowed to regenerate (SDG 15: Life on land). Although planted pastures with exotic grasses may be used more extensively in humid and semi-humid grasslands/rangelands in Uganda and Rwanda, indigenous grasses and trees will be allowed to regenerate in arid and semi-arid grasslands/rangelands in Kenya and the United Republic of Tanzania (see funding proposal, annex 2, feasibility study, textbox 14). The programme follows circular economy principles, improving manure management for biogas production and nutrient cycling, which in turn improves soil health (SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production). It is noted that sub-activity 2.1.3.3 will include strategies to avoid waste (including

8  Written questions from the iTAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025.

<!-- image -->

- e-waste) through the repair of the installed renewable energy-based equipment and the establishment of an equipment maintenance account in a local financial institution by each supported rural organization.
37. Economic co-benefits. The programme is expected to generate significant positive economic impacts, including job creation, poverty reduction, increasing productivity, strengthening income-generating capacities, and hopefully attracting additional private finance into the dairy sector. Smallholder dairy producers and local private-sector value chain actors will have access to essential financial services to improve production processes. This approach aims to increase dairy farmers' incomes, motivating them to adopt further efficient practices and climate-smart technologies (SDG 9: Industry, innovation and infrastructure). Capacitybuilding and adoption of integrated solutions will improve sustainable and resilient production, thereby improving the incomes and productivity of smallholder dairy farmers (SDG 1: No poverty).
38. Semi-nomadic pastoralists : Although the programme focuses primarily on intensive and semi-intensive livestock systems for the value-chain interventions, extensive grazing systems (rangelands on which multipurpose cattle are 100 per cent dependent for foraging) are also part of the activities on rangeland restoration and management, as well as manure management. In response to a question from the iTAP about how the programme will ensure the involvement of extensive pastoralists who are semi-nomadic, including some who selfidentify as Indigenous Peoples, the AE confirmed that such groupings will be engaged through local community governance structures, and that free, prior and informed consent processes will be conducted where appropriate. Pastoralists who so wish will be integrated into supply chains through dairy training and the establishment of new milk collection centres. 9  Although traditional knowledge is mentioned in the funding proposal only in the logframe (section E.3), the AE confirmed its centrality in the approaches to be followed by the livestock-farmer field schools, alongside science, especially in relation to rangeland management and restoration.
39. Seed sovereignty: In response to a question from the iTAP, the AE confirmed that activity 2.2.3, which makes certified forage/fodder seed available for multiplication, will be aligned with the principle of seed sovereignty:
- 'IFAD supports seed sovereignty, recognizing farmers' rights to save, use, exchange, and sell their own seeds, while promoting access to diverse, open-source, non-GMO seed systems that strengthen resilience and local control. Under Activity 2.2.3, DaIMA will align with these principles by promoting the use, multiplication, and dissemination of certified or quality-declared forage and fodder seeds, focusing specifically on openpollinated, locally adapted varieties - not hybrids or genetically modified organisms (GMO). In doing so, the programme will engage local seed businesses, farmer cooperatives, and community seed systems, in accordance with national regulatory frameworks and farmer preferences.'
40. Sustainable development potential is rated as high.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

41. Three of the four DaIMA countries are classified as least developed countries 10 , and all of them are recognized as being highly vulnerable to climate change. Observed and projected climate-change scenarios for the region indicate significant changes in precipitation and temperature patterns, as well as heat stress, increased frequency and severity of droughts and other extreme weather events. The effects will negatively impact the dairy sector, resulting in

9  Written questions from the iTAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025.

10  Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania.

<!-- image -->

- substantial losses in dairy livestock, and loss of productivity of pastures and rangelands, with profound implications for the dairy industry and dairy farmers, leading to lower milk yields and production, reduced cattle health, deterioration in pasture and feed quality, and increased risks in milk losses along the value chains. These impacts compound the sector's existing vulnerabilities, such as farmers' lack of access to knowledge and new skills, insufficient milk collection facilities, and limited access to quality inputs and finance.
42. On the mitigation side, methane emissions from the dairy sector dominate the GHG emission inventories of Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania. The expected growth of the dairy sector means that both absolute emissions and emission intensities will continue to rise if no action is taken to reduce emission intensity per litre of milk produced, and to enhance productivity such that yields can increase without growing herd sizes. The urgency of reducing the sector's GHG emissions and increasing its resilience to climate change is highlighted in national policies and plans, more particularly in the countries' NDCs. As pointed out in the funding proposal (para. 1), the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change recognizes 'efficient livestock systems' as a feasible adaptation option that has synergy with mitigation. 11
43. Each of these countries suffers from a significant development deficit, which negatively impacts their ability to steer the dairy sector on a path to low-emission and climate-resilient development. In all four countries, public fiscal space is limited (see section B.5), and governments rely heavily on official development assistance and concessional financing to fund their dairy sector programmes. As a result, vulnerable populations, such as smallholders, women and youth in the dairy sector are highly exposed to climate risks due to technological, organizational and policy constraints.
44. The needs of the recipient are rated as high.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

45. The Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania are fully supportive of the proposed DaIMA programme aiming to improve resilience to climate change in dairy production and value chains and to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from the dairy sector. Integrating the dairy sector into national climate actions will also have multiple benefits for the environment, nutrition, livelihoods and social inclusion. Partners supporting the programme design and development process include IFAD, FAO, the Global Dairy Platform, the International Livestock Research Institute (ILRI), and Bain &amp; Company.
46. According to annex 10 to the funding proposal, the programme was initially conceptualized as mitigation only and was entitled 'Pathways to Dairy Net Zero (PADNET)'. This project idea and the concept of net zero itself, however, were perceived as too complex, unclear and difficult to understand by many government and development partners. In addition, country-level stakeholders asserted that a key priority in East Africa is the high vulnerability of smallholder farmers and pastoralists and their adaptation to climate change, leading to the shift to a cross-cutting programme called 'Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DaIMA)'. The word 'daima' means 'always' or 'forever' in Swahili, a language spoken in all four countries, highlighting the importance of sustainability and a regional identity to the programme.

11  This is shown in the Sixth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, Synthesis Report,

Figure SPM.7 (fourth row).

<!-- image -->

47. Alignment with policy. The DaIMA programme aligns with the national strategies of the participating countries by supporting and complementing existing plans, policies and strategies related to agriculture, livestock and climate change. In Kenya, the proposed programme aligns with the Agriculture Sector Development Strategy (2019-2029), National Climate Change Action Plan, Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Implementation Framework (2018-2027), and the Kenya National Strategy on Climate Change and Low Carbon Development. In Uganda, the programme aligns with the Livestock Development Strategy and Investment Plan, National Adaptation Plan, and National Climate Change Strategy. In Rwanda, it aligns with Rwanda's NDC, National Climate Change and Environment Policy, Rwanda Livestock Master Plan, National Adaptation Plan, and National Livestock Policy. The funding proposal is silent on alignment with the relevant general policies of the United Republic of Tanzania, though these are shown in table 11 of the feasibility study (annex 2 to the funding proposal). Relevant
50. Component 1's work on policies, awareness and vulnerability assessment will be implemented by the relevant EEs in each country, through a wide range of additional EEs will have oversight responsibility, will include local governments, national government institutions, financial institutions, universities and knowledge institutions, local and national non-governmental organizations, ILRI, FAO and private-sector entities. The same applies to work in component 2 on cooperative strengthening, promoting renewable energy and waste management, investment in cattle-breeding centres, support to animal health and forage production. The programme will build on the fiduciary capacity of the key EE in each of the four
3. regional policy frameworks are also highlighted in section 2.11 of the feasibility study. 48. Engagement with stakeholders. The stakeholder engagement plan (annex 7 to the funding proposal) provides a record of an extensive consultation process over the course of 2023 and 2024. The national designated authorities in the four target countries were directly involved in all stages of programme ideation, conceptualization and preparation. In each country, National Programme Preparation Teams (comprising a wide range of stakeholders from ministries of agriculture/livestock and dairy boards) were established in 2023, at the regional workshop in Kigali (September 2023). Through regional (Arusha, February 2023; Kigali, September 2023) and national workshops (July and September 2023), stakeholder engagement meetings were organized to involve a wide array of national and regional public institutions, private-sector operators, civil society, research institutions and development partners in designing the programme. Four design missions were held in 2023, involving visits to farms and dairy centres, and meetings with government agencies (ministries of agriculture/livestock, ministries of the environment, meteorological services, national agricultural research institutes, dairy development boards/authorities), with private dairy processors, with milk collection centres, dairy cooperatives and farmers, with pastoral communities, with financial services providers (savings and credit cooperative societies, commercial banks, fintech companies), and with input providers (feed and fodder, machinery). 49. Capacity of accredited and executing entities: As AE for the programme, IFAD will be responsible for the overall management of the programme, building on its experience with 11 other GCF projects/programmes under implementation. This role includes programme appraisal, fiduciary and technical oversight, fund management, quality monitoring, reporting to the GCF, and overseeing final evaluation, engaging IFAD headquarters in Rome and its regional office in Nairobi, as well as its country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania. A Regional Steering Committee - constituted of Permanent Secretaries (most senior civil servants) from the EEs, 12 government entities listed in paragraph 2 above will ensure regional alignment and added value, with national governance structures in each country overseeing implementation. International and national entities, including ministries of agriculture and specialized entities (e.g. ILRI), will provide targeted services to the national implementation teams, with preference given to those that have both proven capacity and regional presence. government agencies and service providers. Contracted implementing partners, over which the

<!-- image -->

- countries (National Treasury of Kenya, Ministry of Agriculture and Animal Resources - Rwanda Agriculture Board in Rwanda, Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development, in Uganda, and Ministry of Livestock and Fisheries in the United Republic of Tanzania), and the existing expertise of the wide range of implementing partners in the four countries, further developing this capacity over the six years of programme implementation.
51. Country ownership is rated as high.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

52. With a total budget for the proposed DaIMA programme of USD 358.26 million, IFAD is seeking USD 150 million from GCF - 42 per cent of the overall budget. IFAD is investing a total of USD 193.28 million - made up of USD 17.5 million in loans from its general programme of loans and grants, and USD 175.78 million in senior loans through four existing IFAD-togovernment loan programmes: in Rwanda - the Rwanda Dairy Development Project, phase 2, in the United Republic of Tanzania - Climate Smart Smallholder Dairy Transformation Project, in Kenya - the Integrated Natural Resource and Environmental Management Project, and in Uganda - the Resilient Livestock Value Chain project.
53. The proposed financial structure for the DaIMA programme includes slightly concessional loans to dairy producers (12 per cent of GCF investment, 63 per cent of the total programme) and grants to governments, service providers and producer beneficiaries (88 per cent of the proposed GCF investment, 37 per cent of total programme). Maximum concessionality is sought from GCF for component 1 activities to strengthen the enabling environment and national capacities for low-emission climate-resilient dairy sectors, and also for component 2 investments in strengthening smallholders' and cooperative technical and business capacity, in improving breeding, feed and fodder, and manure management in the value chain, and in improving management of pasture and grasslands.
54. Loan facility : In addition to the four complementary IFAD-government loans serving as co-finance to the GCF investment, a programme-wide investment facility is being set up through component 3 - the Green Dairy Financing Facility. The GDFF will de-risk and enable affordable loans to producers for renewable energy-driven productivity enhancements, with a total of USD 45 million allocated to the facility. Of this amount, USD 35 million will be for on-lending loan capital (GCF loan of USD 17.5 million, IFAD loan of USD 17.5 million), with USD 10 million for a technical assistance facility that will improve investment demand and readiness among pipeline enterprises, and provide support post-deal to ensure successful implementation.
55. The Ministries of Finance in the participating countries, acting as EEs, will receive this combined loan package. These ministries will then disburse local currency loans to wholesale banks or public development banks (PDBs) within their respective countries. Following this, the wholesale banks provide local currency wholesale loans to retail banks operating at the national or local level. Finally, the retail banks on-lend the funds directly to SMEs, specifically targeting dairy value chain actors. An annual survey of market interest rates will help to define an appropriate range of acceptable interest rates for end-borrower lending, within which the retail banks will operate. The entire amount revolves across the GDFF financing chain until the loans to IFAD and GCF have been repaid. As per the repayment schedule, the retail banks are fully responsible for repaying the total loan amounts back to the wholesale banks, regardless of the performance of the underlying portfolio, and the wholesale banks are required to repay the full loan amounts to the Ministries of Finance for repayment back to IFAD and GCF.

## Figure 2: Green Dairy Financing Facility financing chain (from funding proposal, para. 146)

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.42/02/Add.11

Page 141

<!-- image -->

- Abbreviations: EE = executing entity, IFAD = International Fund for Agricultural Development, LCY = local currency, m = million, PDB = public development bank, SMEs = small and medium-sized enterprises 56. The programme thus includes a complex on-lending structure across four steps: 1) from GCF to the host government; 2) from the host government to a PDB; 3) from the PDB to participating retail banks; and 4) from retail banks to borrowers. The iTAP acknowledges the value of the host governments taking the hard currency risk and thus enabling on-lending in local currency. Nonetheless, this long chain of institutions involved, each taking a cut, potentially undermines the concessional nature of GCF funds provided, and seems likely to make coordination of the facility (e.g. ensuring use of proceeds clauses are properly implemented) very challenging. 57. Economic analysis : The ex-ante financial and economic viability of the programme is demonstrated by an economic and financial analysis conducted by the AE (annex 3 to the funding proposal). Economic performance indicators for the four countries individually confirm the economic viability of the investment for society as a whole. At regional level, the aggregated economic net present value (ENPV) of the countries' incremental net benefit streams, discounted at 13.25 per cent (the average social discount rate), is USD 181.20 million, with an economic rate of return (ERR) of 25 per cent for the baseline scenario. Two additional economic performance scenarios are included, by integrating the economic value of the GHG emissions balance with a low vs a high carbon market price assumption. Under the low scenario, the ERR is 27.9 per cent and the ENPV is US$ 217.87 million. Under the high scenario, the ERR is 30.3 per cent and the ENPV is US$ 254.64 million. The sensitivity analysis confirms that DaIMA represents a robust economically worthwhile investment. 58. Financial analysis : The financial analysis examines the profitability of programme investments from the perspective of direct participants. The Livestock Sector Investment Policy Toolkit was used to develop dairy production models in all four countries, using five different herd types: (i) small-scale intensive system/zero grazing with cross-breed and exotic animals; (ii) small-scale intensive system/zero grazing with exotic animals; (iii) semi-intensive with cross-breeds; (iv) semi-intensive with local breeds; and (iv) extensive systems with local breeds. Combinations of these herd models with likely investments through dairy farmers, milk collection points and centres, and dairy-processing units were used as the basis for the analysis.
59. The financial analysis used the financial discount rate for each of the four countries (i.e. the rate at which commercial banks can borrow from the central bank): 11.25 per cent in Kenya,

<!-- image -->

- 6.5 per cent in Rwanda, 9.75 per cent in Uganda and 5.5 per cent in the United Republic of Tanzania. The analysis indicates that the financial internal rate of return (FIRR) is above the opportunity cost of capital in all four countries and thus that the proposed investments can be profitable. The analysis suggests: for Kenya, an FIRR of 31 per cent and net present value (NPV) of USD 172.82 million; for Rwanda, an FIRR of 19 per cent and NPV of USD 41.62 million; for Uganda, an FIRR of 17 per cent and NPV of USD 47.48 million; and for the United Republic of Tanzania, an FIRR of 21 per cent and NPV of USD 87.11 million. A regional average rate of return is also calculated for the proposed programme as a whole, using an average discount rate of 8.25 per cent, and yielding a regional FIRR of 23 per cent and a NPV of USD 349.2 million.
60. Mitigation cost-effectiveness: The estimated cost per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent for the programme, defined as total investment cost/expected lifetime emission reductions, is USD 165 per tonne when considering the entire programme (with co-finance, including the existing IFAD loan programmes), and USD 85 per tonne when considering only the 50 per cent of the programme cost nominally associated with GCF mitigation results (see funding proposal, section A.4).
61. Given the high unit cost of the mitigation results and the complexity of the on-lending structure that reduces financial efficiency, efficiency and effectiveness are rated as medium.

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

62. This proposed DaIMA programme is expected to make a significant contribution to helping the smallholder dairy farming and dairy production sectors in these four East African countries reduce their GHG emissions and enhance carbon removals, while simultaneously building resilience of vulnerable smallholder farmers and SMEs.
63. To enhance the robustness of adaptation actions related to heat stress, the iTAP recommends that IFAD, during the inception phase and early implementation, systematically integrate the Temperature-Humidity Index into operational climate-risk assessments and adaptation monitoring frameworks. This will refine the targeting of livestock heat stress interventions (e.g. housing, shade, water management, breed selection) across intervention areas.
64. The iTAP further recommends that IFAD include in the programme implementation manual:
4. (a) A clear statement of the intended adaptation impact of the project - in reducing vulnerability or exposure, or enhancing adaptive capacity, in relation to the key climate change impacts identified: (i) a decline in milk yields; (ii) heat stress in dairy cattle; (iii) increased development of pathogens, diseases, parasite and vectors in dairy cattle; (iv) decreased feed production and productivity, due to reduced soil moisture and fertility; (v) a decrease in the quantity and quality of pasture; and (vi) deterioration of fodder in unprotected warehouses due to increased humidity.
5. (b) A detailed section on measures to be implemented for heat stress reduction in cattle, taking into account the combined effects of temperature and humidity, including both the broader project strategies like introducing shade trees in pastures and crossbreeding with heat-tolerant breeds, and also a management protocol for very hot days involving, for example, avoiding animal handling, ensuring water availability, bringing cattle under a shade roof with solar-powered fans, and spraying cattle with water.
6. (c) An addition to the project's monitoring and evaluation plan to track herd size (including mortality rate, birth rate and acquisition) over the six years of programme implementation, in order to test the assumption on which the programme's mitigation

<!-- image -->

- results depend (i.e. that herd sizes will remain stable), and to put in place adaptive management measures where growth trends are emerging.
65. The iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

IFAD takes note of the positive assessment.

## Country ownership

IFAD takes note of the positive assessment.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

IFAD takes note of the positive assessment. IFAD takes note of the iTAP comment in reference to the high unit cost of the mitigation results and the required three layers of on-lending structure which potentially increase the risk of reduced financial efficiency, and that the efficiency and effectiveness are rated as medium. IFAD will ensure that the Financing Agreements at all layers are designed to facilitate funds flow and eligible use of resources, offer substantial TA for the implementation, and closely monitor the same.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

IFAD thanks the iTAP for its positive review of the proposal.
